                                                 The Four Color Theorem with Linearly Many Reducible
                                                     Configurations and Near-Linear Time Coloring∗
                                             Yuta Inoue†          Ken-ichi Kawarabayashi‡                   Atsuyuki Miyashita§          Bojan Mohar¶
                                                                        Carsten Thomassen‖                    Mikkel Thorup∗∗

                                                                                          October 5, 2026
                                                                                                Abstract




arXiv:2603.24880v3 [math.CO] 1 Oct 2026
                                                   We give a near-linear time 4-coloring algorithm for planar graphs, improving on the previous
                                               quadratic time algorithm by Robertson et al. from 1996. Such an algorithm cannot be achieved by
                                               the known proofs of the Four Color Theorem (4CT). Technically speaking, we show the following
                                               significant generalization of the 4CT: every planar triangulation contains linearly many pairwise
                                               non-touching reducible configurations or obstructing cycles of length at most 5 (which all allow for
                                               making effective 4-coloring reductions).
                                                   The known proofs of the 4CT only show the existence of a single reducible configuration or
                                               obstructing cycle in the above statement. The existence is proved using the discharging method
                                               based on combinatorial curvature. It identifies reducible configurations in parts where the local
                                               neighborhood has positive combinatorial curvature. Our result significantly strengthens the known
                                               proofs of 4CT, showing that we can also find reductions in large “flat” parts where the curvature
                                               is zero, and moreover, we can make reductions almost anywhere in a given planar graph. This also
                                               opens possibilities for extensions to higher surfaces since we can find such flat parts in any large-width
                                               triangulation of any fixed surface.
                                                   From a computational perspective, the old proofs allowed us to apply induction on a problem that
                                               is smaller by some additive constant. The inductive step took linear time, resulting in a quadratic
                                               total time. With our linear number of reducible configurations or obstructing cycles, we can reduce
                                               the problem size by a constant factor. Our inductive step takes Opn log nq time, yielding a 4-coloring
                                               in Opn log nq total time.
                                                   In order to efficiently handle a linear number of reducible configurations, we need them to have
                                               certain robustness that could also be useful in other applications. All our reducible configurations
                                               are what is known as D-reducible.

                                                                                                     i
1     Introduction
The celebrated Four Color Theorem (4CT) was conjectured by Francis Guthrie in 1852 (see [Mad97,
Tai80] for some of the early history), and remained open for more than 100 years, until Appel and Haken
found a proof [AH77, AHK77] in 1976. The theorem states that every planar map or graph with no
loops can be colored with four colors. So 4-coloring is always possible, but how do we 4-color a concrete
planar graph? There is no easy answer. In fact, historically, it is so complicated that it has led to many
proposed counter-examples to 4CT.
   Robertson, Sanders, Seymour, and Thomas [RSST97, RSST96] found a simpler proof and proved in
1997 that a 4-coloring can be found in quadratic time. Here we show how to 4-color planar graphs in
near-linear time:
Theorem 1.1. We can 4-color a planar graph of order n in Opn log nq time.
    All known proofs of the 4CT are based on finding a so-called reducible configuration or an obstructing
cycle 1 . These both allow us to reduce the planar graph (by an additive constant size) so that it can be
colored inductively.
    The proofs of the 4CT only show the existence of a single reducible configuration or obstructing cycle
in the above statement. The existence proof is based on the notion of combinatorial curvature, which is
defined at each vertex v as the value κpvq “ 6 ´ dpvq. Euler’s formula implies that the average curvature
is strictly positive. The so-called discharging method “smooths out” the curvature by redistributing the
curvature locally, and it is designed so that a vicinity of each vertex whose curvature is positive after the
redistribution contains a reducible configuration.
    In this paper, we show that every planar triangulation contains not only one, but linearly many
pairwise non-touching reducible configurations or obstructing cycles. The main insight is that reducible
configurations or obstructing cycles also appear in large “flat” parts where the smoothed curvature is
identically zero. This is the most significant novel contribution of this paper, with several nontrivial
implications. An interesting aspect of this is that such large flat parts can be found on large-width
triangulations of any fixed surface, which provides a way to use our results much beyond planar graphs.
    From a computational perspective, as described in [RSST96], the old proofs allowed us to apply
induction on a problem that is smaller by some additive constant. The inductive step took linear time,
using so-called Kempe changes [Kem79], resulting in quadratic total time. With our linear number of
reducible configurations or obstructing cycles, we can reduce the problem size by a constant factor. With
a joint use of Kempe chains, for graphs of order n, our inductive step takes Opn log nq time, yielding
4-coloring in Opn log nq total time.
    In order to efficiently handle a linear number of reducible configurations, we need them to be of a spe-
cial, simple form called D-reducible. To find a linear number of D-reducible configurations in any planar
graph, we use more configurations than previous papers that only needed to find one reducible configu-
ration. Appel and Haken [AH89] used 1482 reducible configurations. Robertson, Seymour, Sanders, and
Thomas [RSST97] got down to 633. These papers used arbitrary reducibility, but Steinberger [Ste10]
proved that it suffices to consider D-reducible configurations; however, he then used a list of 2822 D-
reducible configurations. Here we want something much stronger. We do not just want to find one, but
rather a linear number of D-reducible configurations, which forces us to handle the flat case. For this,
we use a list of more than 8200 D-reducible configurations, including those from Steinbeger’s list. As we
shall discuss later, only computers will perform the local case studies involving all these configurations.
    Since we are simultaneously working on a linear number of reducible configurations and obstructing
cycles, one might wonder whether our Opn log nq 4-coloring could be made faster on a parallel computer.
However, Chechik and Mukhtar [CM19] have shown that we need Ωpnq rounds in the local model to
4-color planar graphs, so the scope for improvement using parallelism is limited. This implies that the
Kempe chains used in the inductive step cannot be efficiently parallelized in this model.

Computer checks in proofs. All (correct2 ) proofs of the 4CT use computers to check concrete, well-
defined, finite cases that would be unrealistic for humans to check reliably. We have also used computers.
   1 Obstructing cycles and reducible configurations will be defined in Section 2. They cannot be in a minimal counterex-

ample to the 4CT.
   2 Incorrect proofs have been proposed and even published regularly for more than a century, e.g., a human proof was

proposed in 1879 by Kempe [Kem79], but a mistake was found 11 years later by Heawood in 1890 [Hea90].


                                                           1
Our proof that there is a linear number of D-reducible configurations relies on lemmas with statements of
the form: if we run a certain C++ program with a given input, e.g., the D-reducible configurations, then
it yields a certain output. C++ is a classic imperative programming language used since 1986 [Str86]. The
C++ code and the inputs are made available on GitHub (see https://github.com/near-linear-4ct)
so that any (skeptical) reader can run the code on their own computer.
     Our use of computer checks is pragmatic. We use a computer when the code performing a case
analysis of a check is simpler to understand than a case analysis completed by hand. Using computers,
we can thus focus on high-level design of the analysis (its code) rather than on tedious low-level details.
     More specifically, we use computers, not only to check that our file of 8000+ proposed D-reducible
configurations are indeed D-reducible (as previous papers did), but also more systematically in the anal-
ysis showing that local neighborhoods with certain charging always have one of these configurations (so
no human needs to look at our configurations; it is only the computer programs that need access to a
file containing them). For a computer check, we need to prove that it suffices to consider certain neigh-
borhoods of limited size and limited degrees, but this still leaves us with more than 2100 neighborhoods
to consider. To enable the computer to handle them, we design the analysis to adaptively group the
neighborhoods. The grouping has to be so fine that each group can be analyzed efficiently. At the
same time, it has to be so coarse that there are not too many groups for the computer to handle. The
algorithms behind this adaptive analysis are described in the body of the paper. We also provide both
pseudocode and C++ code. Using a powerful computer with 256 cores, we could run all the computer
checks within a couple of hours.
     The same code was also used many times in order to identify our sufficient set of D-reducible con-
figurations. Given a file of configurations as well as the discharging rules, the code reported local
neighborhoods not containing a configuration, hence where a new configuration needs to be found. We
stopped when no more such neighborhoods were reported.
     We now make some notes on how to verify our proof. The body of the paper provides an algorithmic
description of the computer checks proving that they provide the claimed checks. The pseudocode just
clarifies the actual steps of the algorithms. This is all verified the way we normally verify mathematical
descriptions of algorithms.
     The next step is to verify that the C++ code implements the pseudocode. Since the pseudocode is
sufficiently detailed, this is a simple translation. The implementation needs to declare the variables,
procedures and templates and include the right libraries, and then it has to translate the steps of the
pseudocode into C++ statements. Note that verifying the translation between pseudocode and C++ code
can be done independently of the verification of the algorithm with the psuedocode. Also note that we
only use C++ to implement combinatorial algorithms, we have no issues with precision and rounding.
     One might ask for a computer checked formal proof, but this would require a proof system that
already had an accepted formal understanding of C++ including the libraries involved, and we need
an efficient programming language like C++, simply because the computational task is so large (much
bigger than if we just need to prove the existence of a reducible configuration as done by Gonthier
[Gon08]). However, our proof aims to explain why planar graphs have a linear number of D-reducible
configurations or obstructing cycles. It is thus intended to be read, understood, and verified by humans,
save the execution of C++ programs 3 .
     A curious aspect of using computers to model all possible neighborhoods is that it is convenient to
modify the usual dart representation of combinatorial embeddings [MT01] to include a terminal object
from category theory. This aligns well with the common use of category theory in formal program
specification [BW95].

Preliminaries. We shall use the following standard definitions and notation. For a vertex-set U Ď
V pGq, we denote by GrU s the subgraph of G induced by U . By dG pvq or simply dpvq if G is clear from
the context, we denote the degree of the vertex v in G. A plane graph is a graph with a fixed embedding
in the plane. In a 2-connected plane graph, every face boundary is a cycle. The outer face, that is the
    3 While our proof, including pseudocode and C++ code, is intended to be read, understood, and verified by humans,

we also asked Gemini to check it. Using Gemini is no guarantee of correctness, but Gemini suggested we write the
following explanation of its role: “The formal pseudocodes (Appendix A) and the corresponding C++ implementation
were cross-verified using advanced reasoning AI models (such as Gemini and DeepThink). This AI-assisted verification
acted as an advanced static analyzer—auditing loop invariants, pointer safety within the dart representations, and logical
isomorphism—ensuring that the software architecture flawlessly executes the deductive mathematics.”



                                                            2
unbounded face, is bounded by the outer cycle. Every other face is called an inner face and is bounded
by an inner facial cycle. For further reading on topological graph theory, we refer the reader to the
monograph [MT01].
    We also assume the following algorithmic understanding. Given a planar graph, we can embed it
in the plane in linear time, as proved by Hopcroft and Tarjan [HT74]. This orders the edges around
each vertex. Moreover, since the graph is planar, we can order the vertices so that each vertex has at
most 5 succeeding neighbors (recursively remove a vertex of degree at most 5, as guaranteed by Euler’s
formula). If each vertex remembers its succeeding neighbors (those removed later), we can test if two
vertices are neighbors in constant time. We can then also triangulate a plane graph, ensuring that no
loops or parallel edges are introduced. The triangulation can only make coloring harder, so we may
assume that our input is a planar triangulation supporting constant time neighbor queries.


2     Classic 4CT (high level description)
By a planar triangulation (or just triangulation) we mean a (simple) graph embedded in the 2-sphere, all
of whose faces are triangles. The 4CT says that any planar graph can be 4-colored, but since triangulation
can only make coloring harder, it suffices to consider planar triangulations.
    We say a cycle C is obstructing if it is of length at most 4 with at least one vertex on each side, or
of length 5, with at least 2 vertices on each side. We say a triangulation G is internally 6-connected if
it has no obstructing cycles. This implies that G is a 5-connected triangulation and every separating
set of cardinality 5 induces a 5-cycle surrounding a vertex of degree 5. Birkhoff proved in [Bir13] that a
minimal counterexample to 4CT is an internally 6-connected triangulation.

2.1    Reducible configurations
A key concept from the classic proof of the 4CT [AH89, RSST97] is that of a reducible configuration.
A reducible configuration is a locally defined configuration for which we can say that if it is present in
a triangulation, then it cannot be a minimal counterexample to 4CT. To define reducible configurations
formally, we first need to define configurations.
    Sometimes we view a triangulation as embedded in the plane, and we may or may not treat one
of the faces as the outer face. A near-triangulation is a connected graph embedded in the plane such
that all facial cycles, except the outer cycle, are triangles. This includes the trivial near-triangulations
consisting of a single edge or vertex.
    By a k-coloring of a graph G we mean a coloring of V pGq with colors 1, 2, . . . , k such that each pair
of neighbors gets different colors. Not all colors need to be present. If we just say coloring, we mean
4-coloring, unless otherwise stated. Given a 4-coloring of G, a Kempe chain is a connected component
of the subgraph induced by the vertices of colors a, b where a, b are distinct colors in t1, 2, 3, 4u. Two
color pairs ta, bu and tc, du are complementary if ta, b, c, du “ t1, 2, 3, 4u. A Kempe change is a series of
such color changes that result in new 4-colorings, switching colors a and b on some ab-colored Kempe
chains, followed by switching of two complementary colors c and d at some of the cd-colored Kempe
chains. With this definition, when we consider sequences of Kempe changes, no two consecutive Kempe
changes use the same or complementary pair of colors. If we only switch colors in one Kempe chain, we
call that Kempe change a single Kempe change. This approach was pioneered by Kempe in [Kem79],
and has been used in all later correct proofs of 4CT.
    Let us first recall the basic definitions of configurations and reducibility, as formally introduced in
[AS78]. We want to have the same definitions as in [AS78, RSST97, Ste10] since we will use some of
their reducibility results. By a configuration we mean a pair pZ, δq, where Z is a near-triangulation and
δ : V pZq Ñ N is the degree function satisfying the following conditions:
(Z1) If v P V pZq is a vertex that is not on the outer face of Z, then δpvq “ dZ pvq.

(Z2) If v P V pZq is on the outer face of Z, then δpvq ´ dZ pvq ą 0.
(Z3) If v P V pZq is on the outer face of Z and is a cut-vertex of Z, then v is contained in precisely two
     blocks of Z, and δpvq “ dZ pvq ` 2. We only allow one cut-vertex in a configuration.



                                                     3
We will usually assume that δ is given implicitly, and we will just speak about a configuration Z.
  We say that a plane triangulation G contains the configuration pZ, δq if the following holds:
(Z4) Z is (isomorphic to) a subgraph of G, every triangular (non-outer) face of Z is also a face in G,
     and all triangular faces of Z have the same orientation in G.
(Z5) The degree of each vertex v P V pZq in G is equal to δpvq, that is dG pvq “ δpvq. Moreover, if v is
     a cut-vertex, then the two edges in G but not in Z are non-consecutive in the clockwise ordering.
     (Informally, they go to different sides of the cut-vertex.)
We say that Z, or the configuration pZ, δq, is induced in G if Z is an induced subgraph of G, that is, two
vertices in Z are Z-neighbors if and only if they are G-neighbors. We note that if Z is not induced, it
must be because some boundary vertices of Z are joined by an edge in G that is not in Z.
    The condition in (Z4) that inner facial triangles in Z are facial triangles in G is not included in
previous proofs of the 4CT, as those proofs only consider internally 6-connected triangulations where all
triangles are facial. Here we consider arbitrary triangulations which could have separating triangles, but
we do not want inner facial triangles of Z to map to these.
    We will sometimes say that a configuration pZ, δq is contained in a certain subgraph H of G. By this
we mean that pZ, δq is contained in G with Z embedded in H. Likewise, if we say that the subgraph H
has an obstructing cycle, we mean that a cycle in H is obstructing in G.
    If pZ, δq is a configuration, the free completion of pZ, δq is the near-triangulation Zp obtained from Z
by adding a cycle R disjoint from Z and joining vertices on the outer face of Z to R in such a way that
R becomes the outer face of the near-triangulation Zp and such that Zp contains pZ, δq. The length of the
cycle R is precisely

completion. We will sometimes refer to the free completion Zp as the extended configuration of Z. By the
remark made after (Z5), there is no ambiguity about how to construct Zp even if Z contains cut-vertices.

   Suppose that G contains an induced configuration pZ, δq. Since Z is induced in G, the triangulation
G contains the free completion of Z, possibly with some vertices and edges on R that may be identified
in G. We note that there are no further requirements for the ring and the graph outside the ring. For
example, the ring could be self-intersecting, corresponding to cut-vertices in G ´ V pZq. Every 4-coloring


                                                                 4
ϕ0 of G ´ V pZq induces a 4-coloring of the ring R in the free completion. If this 4-coloring can be
extended to a 4-coloring ϕ1 of p   Z, then the combination of ϕ0 and ϕ1 gives a 4-coloring of G. In the
proof of 4CT, special configurations are shown to be reducible. This happens if any 4-coloring ϕ0 can
be changed (by using a sequence of Kempe changes in G ´ V pZq) so that the resulting coloring extends
from R to Z.p The precise definition and the process related to Kempe changes will be described next.
    We say that a coloring ϕ of R is 0-extendible (or just extendible) to Z if it can be extended to a
coloring of Z.
             p For each integer i ą 0 we say that ϕ is i-extendible to Z if it is either pi ´ 1q-extendible
or the following holds: If G1 is an arbitrary plane graph containing the ring R as a facial cycle and ϕ0 is
any 4-coloring of G1 extending ϕ, then there is a Kempe change of ϕ0 such that the resulting 4-coloring
on R after making the Kempe change is an pi ´ 1q-extendible 4-coloring of R. We define the reducibility
level (or just level ) of ϕ as the minimum i such that ϕ is i-extendible. A Kempe change in G ´ V pZq is
called an improving Kempe change if it changes the coloring of R to a lower level.
    We say that the configuration pZ, δq is Di -reducible if every 4-coloring of its ring is i-extendible,
and D-reducible means Di -reducible for some finite i. It is easy to see [AS78, Fra22] that a minimum
counterexample to 4CT cannot contain D-reducible configurations.
    Exercising the definition, we consider the cases of a single vertex of degree 3 and 4.
Lemma 2.1. A single vertex of degree 3 or 4 is D-reducible. It is 0-extendible if the degree is 3 and
1-extendible if the degree is 4.
Proof. If the vertex has degree 3, we can remove it, color the remaining graph, and then put it back,
assigning a color not used in its ring. If the vertex v has degree 4, we also remove it and color the rest.
If the ring has at most three colors, we are done as above. Otherwise, we need a Kempe change. Let
v0 , . . . , v3 be the ring vertices in the order they appear around v. Look at the Kempe chain A of v0
induced by the colors of v0 and v2 . If A does not include v2 , we simply perform the Kempe change on
A. Otherwise, A connects v0 and v2 , but this implies that we can do a Kempe change on v1 so that it
gets the same color as v3 . In each case, the modified coloring can be extended to v. The proof for the
degree-4 case goes back to Kempe [Kem79].
    As mentioned earlier, if the triangulation is internally 6-connected, then we cannot have vertices
of degree 3 and 4. However, the example is illustrative and our fast algorithm, which works on any
triangulation, will use the fact that these cases are D-reducible.
    We now discuss a more general notion of reducibility, called C-reducibility. It was introduced by
Franklin [Fra22] and used in [AH89] and [RSST97]; see also [AS78]. Suppose that X Ď EpZqzEpRq      p        is
a fixed non-empty edge-set such that the graph Z{X obtained from Z by contracting all edges of X is
                                                     p                    p
loopless. If every 4-coloring of R that arises from some 4-coloring of p Z{X is i-extendible, for some finite
i, then we say that Z is Ci -reducible with respect to the reducer X. We note that when we say that the
coloring of R is i-extendible, it still means that by using at most i Kempe changes in G ´ V pZq (not in
G{X), we can get a coloring that is extendible to Z. Some subtleties exist. In [RSST97], it is assumed
that no triangle in Zp contains more than one edge of X and also that |X| ď 4. However, we only need
that G{X cannot introduce any loop unless there is an obstructing cycle in G with all vertices in Z,    p and
that any coloring of Z{X induces a coloring of R.
                      p
    Let us consider three examples illustrated in Figure 3. Birkhoff [Bir13] proved that the configuration
consisting of two adjacent triangles with all four vertices of degree 5 is D-reducible (see Figure 3(a)).
Based on this result, this configuration is known as the Birkhoff diamond. More generally, if we increase
the degrees of the middle vertices to 6, the resulting configuration is C-reducible. In this case, the reducer
X has four edges that are indicated in Figure 3(b) as bold half-edges leaving the configuration. Franklin
[Fra22] proved that the configuration consisting of a vertex of degree 6 surrounded by six vertices of
degree 6 is C-reducible (see Figure 3(c)). This case uses a reducer that identifies all 6 vertices of degree
4 on the ring into a single vertex.

Computer check of D-reducibility. We note that checking the reducibility of a configuration Z is
typically done by a computer that will compute the extendibility of every possible 4-coloring of the ring
R (there are nearly 3|R|´1 options). Our program to check for reducibility is very similar to the one from
[RSST97].



                                                      5
                            5


                    5               5        5                5


                            5



                           (a)                      (b)                           (c)

Figure 3: (a) Birkhoff diamond, (b) diamond with increased degrees, (c) Franklin’s 6-regular configura-
tion.


    First, the program identifies which 4-colorings of R are 0-extendible. Next, for rounds i “ 1, 2, . . .,
we go through all unclassified 4-colorings. For each such 4-coloring, we check if there must exist an
improving Kempe change resulting in an pi ´ 1q-extendible 4-coloring of the ring. The computer proof
that such an improving Kempe change exists uses arguments similar to those in the proof in Lemma 2.1
that a degree-4 vertex is D1 -reducible. The only reasoning we need is that if v1 , . . . , vℓ is the walk along
the ring, whose interior has been removed, and if ta, bu and tc, du are pairs of complementary colors
and there is an ab-Kempe chain from v1 to vj , then there cannot be a cd-Kempe chain from a vertex
in tv2 , . . . , vj´1 u to a vertex in tvj`1 , . . . , vℓ u. We thus have to consider all possible configurations and
4-colorings of GzZ, including cases where a vertex is repeated in R, and hence a cut-vertex in G ´ V pZq.
    We terminate after round i if it completes the classification of all 4-colorings; in that case, Z is
D-reducible. We also terminate after round i if it finds no new i-extendible coloring. In this case, we
know that Z is not D-reducible.
    The pseudocode for checking D-reducibility is found in Algorithm A.1.1.

2.2    4CT in quadratic time
All known proofs of the 4CT show that any internally 6-connected triangulation has a reducible configu-
ration, contradicting the minimality of a hypothetical counterexample. The reducible configurations are
all of constant size, since they all belong to some specific finite set. Steinberger [Ste10] has even proved
that we can always find a D-reducible configuration [Ste10], hence the C-reducible configurations can be
avoided.

A quadratic algorithm. In [RSST96], the authors derive a quadratic-time 4-coloring algorithm.
Recall that a planar triangulation G is internally 6-connected if and only if it has no obstructing cycles,
that is, a separating 3- or 4-cycle, or a 5-cycle with at least two vertices on both sides.
    The above-mentioned proofs of 4CT imply that for an arbitrary triangulation, we can find either an
obstructing cycle or a reducible configuration, and this can be done in linear time.
    If we find a reducible configuration Z, we reduce it and color the rest by induction. This gives a
coloring of the ring R. If it is an i-extendible configuration, then we may have to perform up to i
improving Kempe changes before we can extend the coloring to the original Z.
    Note that to tell if a Kempe chain is improving, we need to know the level of all colorings of the ring
R. This is only a constant amount of information, since we only have a constant number of reducible
configurations of constant size.
    Also note that we only know that an improving Kempe change exists, but which one works depends
on the graph and the coloring outside the ring. Recall that a Kempe change is characterized by two
complementary pairs ta, bu and tc, du of colors and a subset of ab- and cd-colored Kempe chains on
which the two colors are swapped. The swapping only affects the coloring of the ring if the Kempe chain
intersects the ring. This means that there are fewer than 3 ¨ 2|R| “ Op1q relevant Kempe changes to
check, and we apply the first improving one we find. All this can be implemented in linear total time.
    [RSST96] also shows how we can benefit from obstructing cycles. The general conclusion is that we
can reduce the problem size by an additive constant in linear time, so the overall algorithm is quadratic.
    The main bottleneck in the above approach is the Kempe changes. We note that the interaction
between different Kempe chains can be very complicated. For example, if we first perform a Kempe


                                                          6
                         Figure 4: The configuration in D for the 6-regular case.


change using colors 1 and 2 and their complementary pair 3 and 4, then it affects the 13, 14, 23, and
24-colored Kempe chains. So, the result of multiple Kempe changes can be quite chaotic. It seems
hopeless to update the information about Kempe chains in sublinear time.


3      Our results (high level description)
Previous proofs and algorithms for 4CT only guarantee a single reducible configuration. We will show
here that we can always find a linear number of reducible configurations if the triangulation is internally
6-connected. In general, we find either a linear number of reducible configurations or a linear number
of obstructing cycles (or both). To make algorithmic use of the many reducible configurations, we will
need them to be D-reducible.
    To get a linear number of reducible configurations, we do need some 6-regular reducible configuration
since the graph could have all vertices of degree 6 except for 12 of degree 5. The classic 6-regular
configuration is Franklin’s from Figure 3 (c), but it is not D-reducible. However, we discovered the larger
D-reducible 6-regular configuration in Figure 4, which is the only 6-regular configuration we use.
    We will need a special property of configurations that will enable us to use the following fact.
Lemma 3.1. Let pZ, δq be a configuration. Suppose it satisfies the following conditions:
    (i) The diameter of Z is at most 4.
 (ii) If u and w are vertices at Z-distance 4, then there is a length-4 path P joining them in Z such that
      either P is not contained in the outer face boundary of Z, or P “ pu, v1 , v2 , v3 , wq is contained in
      the outer face boundary of Z, and then one of the three vertices vi is not a cut-vertex of Z and has
      external degree δpvi q ´ dZ pvi q ě 2.
If pZ, δq is contained in G and G does not contain an obstructing cycle whose vertices are all in Z, then
pZ, δq is induced in G.
Proof. If pZ, δq is contained in G but not induced, it is because G has an edge not in Z but joining
vertices u and w from Z. Then u and w must be on the boundary of Z since only boundary vertices can
have incident edges not in Z. By (i), we have a shortest path P of length at most 4 between u and w,
and if it is of length 4, we can assume it satisfies (ii). Now P and the edge uw form a cycle C, which we
want to prove is obstructing.
      We now walk clockwise from u to w along the outer face boundary of Z. Since the edge uw is not in
Z, we must meet a vertex v between u and w. By (Z2) and (Z5), v must have a neighbor v 1 to the left
and outside Z (because the edges leaving to the left cannot all be chords). Clearly, v also has a neighbor
v 2 to the right and outside Z. So, if C has length at most 4, then it is obstructing.
      Now suppose C is of length 5. Then we need to find two vertices on either side of C. Again, we walk
clockwise around the boundary of Z from u to w. If this walk is not just following P , then it is easy
to find a vertex v 1 in the interior of C and a vertex v 2 in the exterior of C. Condition (ii) implies that
v 1 , respectively v 2 , is not the only such vertex. If the walk follows P , then again, condition (ii) implies
that the interior, respectively exterior, of C has at least two vertices. Therefore, C is an obstructing
5-cycle.
   Note that the condition in (ii) is needed for the conclusion that Z is induced. Indeed, if for all
i “ 1, 2, 3, vi is a cut-vertex or δpvi q ´ dZ pvi q “ 1, then u, v may be neighbors such that P ` uv is the
cycle of neighbors around a single degree-5 vertex outside Z.


                                                       7
              Figure 5: The configuration in D whose radius is 3 (but the diameter is still 4).


    All reducible configurations used in this paper also satisfy (i) and (ii) of Lemma 3.1 (see item (D0) in
Lemma 3.2). This property, which is needed for our proof, has not been addressed explicitly in previous
proofs of the 4CT. We have verified by computer that the conditions of Lemma 3.1 are satisfied not
only for all reducible configurations used in this paper, but also for all reducible configurations used by
Robertson et al. in [RSST97] and by Steinberger in [Ste10].
    The D-reducible configurations that we shall use are from a set D that has 8202 elements. Two
of them are the single vertices of degree 3 or 4 from Lemma 2.1 that are relevant when we consider
arbitrary triangulations. All other configurations in D use only vertices of degree at least 5. These 8200
configurations are collected in our GitHub repository 4 . The set D contains the set of all D-reducible
configurations used by Steinberger in [Ste10]. We will use D0 to denote this subset of configurations.
    The important characteristics of the configurations in D are summarized in the following lemma.
Lemma 3.2. The set D consists of 8202 D-reducible configurations. It includes the D-reducible con-
figurations consisting of a single vertex of degree 3 or 4 (cf. Lemma 2.1), the “flat” case and includes
Steinberger’s set D0 Ă D of configurations from [Ste10]. Each configuration D P D has the following
properties:
(D0) D satisfies conditions (i) and (ii) of Lemma 3.1; in particular, it has diameter at most 4.
(D1) With a single exception, D has radius at most 2. We distinguish a vertex of eccentricity 2, which
     we call the center vertex or just center. The center is the only vertex in D that can be of degree
     greater than 8, in which case it is between 9 and 12. The exceptional configuration in D which has
     radius 3 and degrees 5 and 7 is shown in Figure 5. It is one of our configurations to handle the
     flat case.
(D2) D has at most 19 vertices and its ring size is at most 18.
(D3) Every 4-coloring of the ring of the free completion of D is 25-extendible.
   In order to make use of multiple reducible configurations in a triangulation, we want them to be
non-touching in the sense that they are disjoint and the graph has no edge joining them.5 This implies
that their rings cannot cross.
   Our main theorem is the following.
Theorem 3.3. There is a linear-time algorithm that, given any triangulation, finds a linear number of
non-touching either separating 3- or 4-cycles, or reducible configurations from D. All vertices involved
have degree at most 12 in the triangulation.
    As a corollary using Lemma 3.1, we get
Corollary 3.4. There is a linear-time algorithm that, given any triangulation, finds one of the following:
    • A linear number of pairwise non-touching induced reducible configurations in D.
    • A linear number of non-touching obstructing cycles.
All vertices in the above-mentioned reducible configurations or obstructing cycles have degree at most 12
in the triangulation.
   4 https://github.com/near-linear-4ct/reducible-configurations
   5 The definition of two subgraphs of G to touch, meaning that they either have a vertex in common or G contains an

edge joining them, is frequently used in graph minors theory, see [Die18, Dvo25] or [Ree97].


                                                            8
3.1    Sketch of Opn log nq recursion
To obtain an Opn log nq 4-coloring algorithm for a planar triangulation G of order n, it suffices that we
can construct in Opn log nq time a planar triangulation H that is smaller by a constant factor, color H
recursively, and use this coloring to color G in Opn log nq additional time.
   Given G, we first apply Theorem 3.3, and then we make a recursion depending on which of the two
outcomes we get. The most interesting case is the first one.

D-reducible configurations. Suppose we get Ωpnq non-touching D-reducible configurations. In the
quadratic-time algorithm, we would just handle one of them in linear time, but here we will handle all
of them in Opn log nq time. Below, we first show this using randomization.
    More specifically, our first obvious step is to remove all the reducible configurations from the graph.
This removes Ωpnq vertices. Next, using new auxiliary edges, we triangulate all the faces inside their
now empty rings. Then, recursively, we color the resulting reduced triangulation, which has a constant
factor fewer vertices.
    Having colored the reduced triangulation recursively, we restore the original triangulation exactly
as it was, removing the auxiliary edges and putting back all the reducible configurations. However, we
keep the recursive coloring, which is now a 4-coloring of all vertices outside the reducible configurations.
We note that the reducible configurations, while non-colored, have all their rings colored. This follows
because they are non-touching, implying that the ring of one reducible configuration does not intersect
any other reducible configuration.
    We will now start doing Kempe changes on the colored vertices. If the ring R of a non-colored
configuration Z gets a 0-extendible coloring, then we extend this 4-coloring of R to Z, and then Z is
no longer among the non-colored configurations (strictly speaking, it is pZ, dG q that is the reducible
configuration).
    In general, if the coloring of a ring is i-extendible, then we may have to do i improving Kempe
changes to make it 0-extendible. We know from Lemma 3.2 that all colorings of the rings of our reducible
configurations are 25-extendible. Each Kempe change may affect most of the graph, so we cannot afford
to do this for one configuration at a time. However, we claim that random Kempe changes are expected
to be improving for a constant fraction of the non-colored configurations, and this leads to the following
very simple algorithm.
    While there is a non-colored configuration, we repeat the following two steps:
  1. Make a random Kempe change. First, we randomly decide which two complementary pairs ta, bu
     and tc, du of colors will be used by the Kempe chains, that is, the Kempe chains considered are
     the ab- and cd-colored components. These partition all colored vertices. Next, for every ab-colored
     and every cd-colored component, we toss a coin to decide whether to swap the colors on it in the
     random Kempe change.
  2. For all non-colored components Z whose ring coloring became 0-extendible, we extend the coloring
     to Z.

We claim that this algorithm terminates after Oplog nq iterations with high probability. To prove this,
we consider an arbitrary non-colored configuration Z, and argue that its ring coloring will become 0-
extendible within Oplog nq rounds with high probability. The result then follows by a union bound.
    Consider the iterations from the perspective of any given reducible configuration Z with ring R. If
Z is non-colored, it wants a sequence of at most 25 improving Kempe changes. As discussed in the
quadratic algorithm, an improving Kempe change requires the right choice of complementary color pairs
ta, bu and tc, du. There is a 1{3 chance that a random choice is right since the pairs are determined
just by the pairing of color 1. Now the improvement only depends on the color change (color swap or
no-swap) of the disjoint ab and cd-colored components (Kempe chains) intersecting the ring R, of which
there are at most |R| ď 18. A random Kempe change is thus improving for Z with probability at least
1    ´18
3 ¨2     . We need at most 25 such consecutive improvements for Z for the ring to become 0-extendible.
The probability of this success for Z is at least p “ 1{p3 ¨ 218 q25 “ Ωp1q.
    The above fails for Z if we make a Kempe change that is non-improving for Z before R becomes
0-extendible, but if it fails, we still have a coloring of R, and any coloring of R is 25-extendible. Thus,
we just try again for the next at most 25 random Kempe changes. The probability that we fail f times is


                                                     9
at most p1 ´ pqf ă e´pf , so Z fails 2p´1 ln n times with probability at most 1{n2 ; otherwise Z succeeded
within 50p´1 ln n iterations. A union bound then implies that all configurations succeed within 50p´1 ln n
iterations with probability at least 1 ´ 1{n.
    Using the method of conditional expectations, we are going to derandomize the above Kempe changes
in Section 12. The derandomization always makes the greedy choice maximizing the expected number
of configurations that become 0-extendible. In particular, it will always get at least one 0-extendible
configurations within 25 Kempe changes.

Why do we insist on D-reducible configurations? It is important to note that the above approach
would not work for general C-reducible configurations Z with reducer X, e.g., the graph in Figure 3 (b).
    We could, as above, reduce all of them, removing Z and contracting X, and color the graph induc-
tively. However, X is only used to control this initial coloring of the ring R to make sure that the coloring
is i-extendable, that is, with ring R empty inside, and no contracted X, there are i improving Kempe
chains, leading to an extendable coloring of the ring.
    However, for most of these C-reducible configurations, the Kempe chains may not be improving, and
even worse, they could very easily lead to colorings that are not extendable for Z (i.e., not possible to
color Z with this ring coloring). Then there is no way to complete the coloring process. With ring R
empty inside, if there are i improving Kempe chains, then they lead to an extendable coloring of the ring,
but we may never obtain such chains because the contracted graph inside the ring may prevent them.
    One could imagine another way of working with C-reducible configurations Z, where we kept the
contractions of the reducer while doing Kempe changes. However, there are many important cases where
there are no improving Kempe changes, e.g., the classic C-reducible Birkhoff diamond in Figure 3 (b).

Obstructing cycles. Finally, we have the case of a linear number of pairwise non-touching obstructing
cycles. In this case, we apply the techniques from [RSST96] to handle a single obstructing cycle; these
techniques can be extended to a component hierarchy in which components are separated by obstructing
cycles. To achieve an efficient reduction using such a hierarchy, it is crucial that the obstructing cycles
are non-touching as described in Theorem 3.3. With a linear number of obstructing cycles, we can reduce
the problem size by a constant factor in linear time. A full description of this case is found in Section 13.

Bottleneck: the number of Kempe chains. Let us mention one important challenge. One Kempe
change may take linear time, because even a single Kempe chain may be of linear order. One important
thing to note is that the previous algorithm [RSST96] requires Op1q Kempe changes to reduce the graph
size by a constant size. This explains why the previous algorithm takes Opn2 q time. The correctness of
the algorithm requires the proof of the 4CT.
    In contrast, we do only Oplog nq Kempe chains to reduce the graph size by a constant factor. This
is our main challenge, because one Kempe chain may take linear time, and in order to achieve our
near-linear time algorithm, a linear number of reducible configurations to make reductions by a constant
factor is definitely necessary (which is our main technical contribution).
    There is a linear-time algorithm for 5-coloring planar graphs [CNS81]; however, the method presented
there does not involve any Kempe chain/change. In contrast, for 4-coloring, we definitely need Kempe
chains/changes, or we would need a completely new proof of 4CT.


4    Discharging to find a reducible configuration in classic 4CT
Let G be a triangulation in the plane. We start by assigning to each vertex v P V pGq the value

                                           T0 pvq :“ 10p6 ´ dpvqq,

which we call the initial charge at v. It follows from Euler’s formula (see Lemma 4.3 below) that
                                               ÿ
                                                    T0 pvq “ 120.
                                            vPV pGq

Now we redistribute the charge among the vertices applying discharging rules. We use the same set of
rules as in [Ste10]. As mentioned in the introduction, the initial charge T0 pvq can be viewed as a discrete


                                                      10
curvature measure. The discharging process is a way to “smooth out” the curvature locally around each
vertex, and the rules are chosen in such a way that we obtain a reducible configuration or a separating
3- or 4-cycle whenever the final charge is positive.
    Discharging rules are formally defined as follows.
                                                ´ `
Definition 4.1. A rule is six-tuple R “ pGpRq, δR , δR , spRq, tpRq, rpRqq, so that
  (i) GpRq “ pV pRq, EpRqq is a near-triangulation without cut-vertices.
       ´               `                                   ´        `
 (ii) δR : V pRq Ñ N, δR : V pRq Ñ N Y t8u, such that 5 ď δR pvq ď δR pvq for each v P V pRq.
(iii) spRq, tpRq P V pRq are distinct adjacent vertices.
(iv) rpRq is a positive integer.
A configuration pZ, δq obeys the rule R if Z is isomorphic to GpRq and every pair pvZ , vR q P V pZq ˆ
                                                                    ´                   `
V pGpRqq of corresponding vertices under the isomorphism satisfies δR pvR q ď dpvZ q ď δR pvR q.
    The graph GpRq of the rule R is a near-triangulation in which each vertex v is given a range of
                                   ´       `
possible degrees, the interval rδR   pvq, δR pvqs. The value rpRq represents the amount of charge that is
sent from the vertex spRq to its neighbor tpRq. We have rpRq “ 1 or rpRq “ 2 for all our rules. We
                               ´ `
describe the degree range rδR   , δR s in drawings (for example in Figure 6) as follows: There are three
                ´        `                                               ´
cases. When δR pvq “ δR pvq, we draw v such that it has the shape for δR   pvq shown in Figure 2. Secondly,
             ´         `                                                            `
when 5 “ δR pvq ă δR pvq, we draw v such that it has the shape for the integer δR     pvq shown in Figure 2.

Moreover, we add a minus sign close to v. Finally, when δR pvq ă δR pvq “ 8, we draw v such that it has
                 ´
the shape for δR   pvq and we add a plus sign close to v. The edge spRqtpRq is indicated by an arrow. All
our rules in Figure 6 have one of these three types of degree ranges.
    In this paper, we use the set R of 84 rules described in Figure 6. These rules are collected in our
GitHub repository6 This set is exactly the same as the set of rules used in [Ste10], except that our 12th
and 13th rules are considered as a single rule in [Ste10]. We also note that Steinberger only applied the
rules to internally 6-connected graphs, whereas we consider arbitrary triangulations, where they are not
necessarily induced. However, we recall that we insist that facial triangles in the rules must be matched
to facial triangles in the graph.
    An edge with an arrow represents the direction in which a charge moves. Thus, spRq (resp., tpRq) is
the tail (the head) of the edge with an arrow. The number of arrows in the figure represents rpRq.
    We calculate the amount of charge sent along an edge st in G by checking which rules apply to st.
We apply a rule R to st if there is a (unique) homomorphism ϕ into G such that the edge st “ ϕpstq.
The homomorphism is understood to be order-preserving, and it should map facial triangles of Z into
facial triangles of G.
    We note that in previous proofs of the 4CT, it is further required that ϕ is 1-1, that is, an embedding,
but it turns out to both simplify our arguments and yield a stronger main theorem (Theorem 3.3) when
we allow applications of discharging rules also when ϕ is not 1-1. We shall later (in Lemma 9.2) see that
if the graph is internally 6-connected, then the homomorphism ϕ from a discharging rule of diameter
at most 4 is automatically 1-1. Removing the 1-1 condition therefore only matters because we want to
handle arbitrary triangulations.
Definition 4.2. (a) For adjacent vertices u, v P V pGq, we define ϕpu, vq as the sum of the values rpRq
over all rules R P R that are applied with spRq “ u and tpRq “ v. This is the charge sent from u to v.
   (b) For a vertex u P V pGq, we set
                                                   ÿ
                                 T puq :“ T0 puq `   pϕpv, uq ´ ϕpu, vqq.
                                                   v„u

The value T puq is called the final charge at u.
   We note that it is easy to process all discharging rules and compute the final charge in linear time.
The point is that for each edge in the graph, there is only a constant number of ways that we can possibly
apply the discharging rules from Figure 6 to the edge.
   The discharging method uses the following easy observation.

    Lemma 4.3 implies that there is a vertex u with T puq ą 0. Previous proofs of 4CT show that for any
vertex u with T puq ą 0, there is a reducible configuration in the vicinity of u.7 This yields a contradiction
to the assumption that we have a minimum counterexample. However, we need much more for our main
result of having a linear number of reducible configurations (see Theorem 3.3).
Definition 4.4. Let v be a vertex in G and let the ball Bk pvq be the subgraph of G induced by the set of
vertices at distance at most k from v. We call B1 pvq the wheel with center v, and B2 pvq the cartwheel
with center v.
    As our discharging rules are exactly the same as those in [Ste10], we have the following result from
[Ste10].
Theorem 4.5. Suppose that G is an internally 6-connected triangulation that contains a vertex v with
final charge T pvq ą 0. Then one of the D-reducible configurations in Steinberger’s [Ste10] set D0 Ă D is
contained in the cartwheel B2 pvq as an induced subgraph.
    This result implies 4CT, since a minimal counterexample is known to be internally 6-connected (see
[RSST97, RSST96]). However, there may only be a constant number of vertices with positive charge, so
this does not help us find a linear number of reducible configurations.


5      Extending unavoidability to the flat case
The classic proof of the 4CT tells us that we can find a reducible configuration in the cartwheel (that
is, within the second neighborhood B2 pvq) around any positively charged vertex. However, a planar
triangulation may have only a few vertices of positive charge. In fact, almost all the vertices may have
final charge zero, and we refer to this as the flat case. To motivate this terminology, observe that in a
large piece of the flat hexagonal tiling, all the vertices would have final charge zero. For example, this
occurs with all triangulations that are dual to the class of fullerenes. Those have 12 vertices of degree 5
and all others of degree 6. Positive final charge will only be found in a neighborhood around the degree-5
vertices. In this case, the only other reducible configuration we will find there is a configuration in Figure
4.
     However, the general flat case is much more complicated. We need thousands of additional reducible
configurations to cover all cases. Formally, we say that a vertex v is flat if its final charge is zero, that
is, T pvq “ 0. In the flat case, we may need to look at a larger neighborhood. We say that the ball Bk pvq
is flat if all vertices in Bk pvq are flat.
     One of our main insights comes from analyzing large, flat balls. We will prove the following nontrivial
generalization of Theorem 4.5.
Theorem 5.1. Suppose that G is an internally 6-connected triangulation that contains a vertex v such
that B12 pvq is flat. Then B14 pvq contains a reducible configuration contained in D as an induced subgraph.
    Note above that to find the reducible configuration in the flat case, we have to go much beyond the
cartwheel B2 pvq considered in the classic Theorem 4.5.
    Theorem 5.1 above is our main structural graph theoretic insight, but more is needed for an efficient
coloring algorithm.
    7 Each proof of 4CT, by Appel and Haken [AH89], by Robertson et al. [RSST97] and by Steinberger [Ste10] has different

discharging rules, so the final charge T pvq has a different meaning for each of them, but the stated conclusion is the same.

                     B2 pvq                        B28 pvq                       s 8 pvq

Figure 7: Cartwheels. Some vertices in a cartwheel could be identified in the triangulation, but then we
would have separating 3- or 4-cycles.


6     Finding reducible configurations in triangulations
The input to our coloring algorithm is an arbitrary triangulation that need not be internally 6-connected.
For our results, we want a local view of internal 6-connectivity. Recall that a planar triangulation G is
internally 6-connected if it has no obstructing cycles. In this section, we state lemmas and theorems,
deferring most proofs to later sections. The main goal here is to communicate our new understanding.
    Both reducible configurations and obstructing cycles help us make progress towards a 4-coloring. For
the known quadratic 4-coloring algorithm [RSST96], it suffices to find one reducible configuration or
one obstructing cycle, but here we want to prove Theorem 3.3 stating that we can find linear number of
non-touching either separating 3- or 4-cycles, or reducible configurations from D. We will achieve this
locally by finding a linear number of non-touching neighborhoods of constant size, each of which contains
one of the desired objects. First, we need the following property of our discharging rules.
Lemma 6.1. For any triangulation G and v P V pGq, we have final charge T pvq ď 60 ´ 2 ¨ dG pvq.
                                                             2
Consequently, T pvq ď 54 and if dG pvq ě 31, then T pvq ď ´ 31 dG pvq.
   The proof of this lemma is deferred to Section 7. The final charge of a vertex is thus linearly decreasing
with the degree. The constants (e.g., 54, 31) in Lemma 6.1 are not important. The existence of an upper
bound on the final charge and the degree of a vertex whose final charge is possibly positive or zero is
important because we only consider neighborhoods of vertices with non-negative final charge, and now
we know they have bounded degree.

6.1    Local neighborhoods of constant size
We will carefully define a constant-size local neighborhood around v with T pvq ě 0 that contains a
homomorphic image of a reducible configuration and such that each local neighborhood can interfere
with only a constant number of other local neighborhoods. By interference between local neighborhoods,
we mean that their reducible configurations are touching.
   Our local neighborhoods are defined in terms of (extended) degree-bounded balls:

Definition 6.2. Let v0 be a vertex in G. The k-local neighborhood around v0 has the following elements:
    • The degree-bounded ball Bk8 pv0 q of the center vertex v0 is the subgraph of G induced by the set of
      vertices V1 Ď V pGq such that for every v1 P V1 , there is a path P between v0 and v1 of length at
      most k, and the degrees of the vertices of P except for the center v0 are at most 8.

    • The set of boundary vertices V2 consists of the vertices v ‰ v0 of degree at least 9 such that for
      every v P V2 , there is a path P between v0 and v of length at most k and all the internal vertices of
      P are of degree at most 8. We define B  s 8 pv0 q “ GrV1 Y V2 s calling it the extended degree-bounded
                                               k
      ball around v0 .
   The definition of the different cartwheels is illustrated in Figure 7. If dpv0 q and k are bounded above
by a constant, then this leads to local neighborhoods of constant size.

around v0 . We claim that for every j ě 2, |Sj | ď |Ssj | ď 5|Sj´1 |. For each v P Ssj , pick one of its
neighbors in Sj´1 , say u. Note that u has a neighbor w P Sj´2 and has two more neighbors u1 , u2 that
are in the two triangles containing the edge uw. Clearly, u1 , u2 belong to B        sj´1 and hence are not in Ssj .
Consequently, since dpuq ď 8, u has at most 5 neighbors in Ssj . This easily implies that |Ssj | ď 5|Sj´1 |
and hence |Ssj | ď 5j´1 dpv0 q.
    Let us now consider the cardinality of V pB  s 8 q. The claim of the lemma is easy for k ď 1, so we may
                                                   k
assume that k ě 2 and proceed with induction. Since V pB       s 8 q “ Ssk Y V pB s 8 q, the induction hypothesis
                                                                k                  k´1
and the claim derived above give that |B s 8 | ď |Ssk | ` |B
                                                           s 8 | ď 5k´1 dpv0 q ` 5k´1 dpv0 q ă 5k dpv0 q.
                                          k                 k´1

   By inspection of the discharging rules in Figure 6, we will prove:

Lemma 6.4. Let v be a vertex in G and vu any incident edge. The amount of charge, ϕpv, uq and ϕpu, vq,
sent along this edge is completely determined by the graph structure of the extended degree-bounded ball
Bs 8 pvq. Since all discharging over edges incident to v is determined by B
                                                                          s 8 pvq, it follows that B
                                                                                                   s 8 pvq also
  2                                                                         2                        2
determines the final charge T pvq.

    Our discharging rules thus act within the extended degree-bounded ball B s 8 pvq, and many of the rules
                                                                              2
are affected by high-degree boundary vertices, so these are important for the final charge of v.
    However, if we wanted local neighborhoods to have disjoint extended degree-bounded balls, we would
face the issue that a linear number of them could intersect at a single high-degree vertex. We therefore
want to claim that it suffices that the non-extended degree-bounded balls are non-touching.

6.2    Main technical result
With the above understanding of the local neighborhood, we can now state the generalization of Theorems
4.5 and 5.1, which we want to prove in the rest of the paper:
Theorem 6.5. Let G be a triangulation and v P V pGq.

  (i) If v has positive final charge, T pvq ą 0 then B28 pvq contains either a separating 3- or 4-cycle, or a
      reducible configuration in D.8 .
 (ii) If dpvq ě 9 and T pvq “ 0 then B28 pvq contains either a separating 3- or 4-cycle, or a reducible
      configuration in D.

(iii) If dpvq ď 8, and all vertices in B12 pvq have degree at most 8 and have final charge 0, then B12 pvq
      contains either a separating 3- or 4-cycle, or a reducible configuration in D.
    Note that even though Theorem 6.5 only considers degree-bounded balls, it also talks about final
charges, and to determine the final charge of a vertex u, then we actually need to consider the extended
degree-bounded ball B   s 8 puq.
                         2
    Recall that vertices of degree less than 5 are reducible configurations in D, so if any of these are
present (in B28 pvq in (i) or (ii) or in B12 pvq in (iii)), then there is nothing to prove above.
    Theorem 6.5 (i) together with Lemma 3.1 is a strengthening of the result of Steinberger [Ste10]
who assumes that the graph is internally 6-connected, meaning no obstructing cycles. In addition, the
reducible configuration in Theorem 6.5 (i) is in B28 pvq while Steinberger [Ste10] only placed it in B2 pvq.
    Theorem 6.5 (ii) and (iii) are two of the main new contributions of this paper, with (iii) being by far
the hardest. We claim they imply Theorem 5.1, which assumes no obstructing cycles, hence by Lemma
3.1, that any reducible configuration is induced. To see this, consider a vertex v such that all vertices in
B12 pvq have final charge zero. If v has degree at least 9, then the statement follows immediately from
Theorem 6.5 (ii). Suppose instead that v has degree at most 8. If all vertices in B12 pvq also have degree
at most 8, then Theorem 6.5 (iii) applies. Otherwise, we have some zero-charge vertex v 1 in B12 pvq having
degree at least 9, and then Theorem 6.5 (ii) applied at the vertex v 1 is used. Note that the resulting
configuration is contained in B14 pvq.
  8 In fact, the reducible configuration is either a vertex of degree at most 4 or it is in one of Steinberger’s [Ste10] D .
                                                                                                                          0




                                                             15
                                                                           s 8 is enormous. We have at
    We note that for case (iii), the number of possible configurations for B 12
          12                              11
most 8 ¨ 5 vertices, including up to 8 ¨ 5 boundary vertices, and for each of these we must choose a
                                            12
degree between 5 and 9, for a total of 511¨5 options. This is far beyond what a computer can handle,
so our proof requires a careful combination of human reasoning and computational power.

6.3    Finding a linear number of reducible configurations
We want to find a linear number of either separating 3- or 4-cycles or reducible configurations from D,
so as to prove Theorem 3.3 assuming all the results claimed above.

Accounting. We now show that Theorem 6.5 and Lemma 6.1 imply a linear number of non-touching
either 3- or 4-cycles, or reducible configurations, as stated in Theorem 3.3. More precisely, we want to
find a linear number of vertices satisfying the conditions of Theorem 6.5 and such that the mentioned
(non-extended) degree-bounded balls are non-touching; for this implies that the separating 3- or 4-cycle,
or reducible configurations (pick one from each ball) are non-touching.
     First, we consider the case where we have a linear number of vertices v satisfying the final-charge
condition in (i) or (ii) of Theorem 6.5, that is, either v has positive final charge or v has final charge zero
and degree at least 9.
     Let U be the set of vertices satisfying the final-charge condition in (i) or (ii). Iteratively, we will
construct a subset U ˚ of vertices v with non-touching (non-extended) B28 pvq. Note here that B28 pvq and
B28 pwq are non-touching if w is not in the extended degree-bounded radius-5 ball B      s 8 pvq. The construction
                                                                                            5
of U is as follows: If the current U is non-empty, we move an arbitrary vertex v from U to U ˚ and
      ˚

Linear time implementation. First we note that we can implement all the discharging in linear
time. This follows because we only have a constant number of discharging rules in R from Figure 6.
    Next we have to do the above cleanings, reducing U to U ˚ or W to W ˚ . For each vertex added, we
just remove the constantly many vertices in B s 8 pvq or B
                                                         s 8 pwq.
                                               5           25
    Finally, we have to search the degree-bounded balls B s 8 puq, u P U ˚ or B
                                                                              s 8 pwq, w P W ˚ , for separating
                                                            2                   12
3- or 4-cycles or reducible configurations from D. This takes constant time for each ball since it is of
constant size and the set D is finite.
    This completes the proof of Theorem 3.3 assuming our Theorem 6.5 and Lemma 6.1, both of which
are yet to be proved.

Organization of the rest of the paper. In Sections 7–8, we prove our main technical result, Theorem
6.5. We will make it clear that some parts of the proof require a computer to check a finite number
of cases. In the appendix, we provide details of the pseudocode, so it can be verified that it does the
claimed test.
    In Sections 9–11, we discuss how to combine intersecting near-triangulations and corresponding con-
figurations in order to make effective computational tasks when dealing with multiple configurations
containing the same edge in the unavoidability part of the proof. Finally, Sections 12 and 13 give details
about derandomization of our main computational tasks leading to the near-linear 4-coloring algorithm.

We will now consider discharging over edges and their impact on the final charge. First, we consider an
arbitrary triangulation with no restrictions on obstructing cycles or reducible configurations.

Lemma 7.3. Suppose that v is a vertex in G of degree at least 12 and with final charge T pvq ě 0. Then

Proof. For the proof by contradiction, suppose that there are no separating 3- or 4-cycles and no reducible
configurations as claimed. By Lemma 7.2, ϕpui , vq ď 5 for every neighbor ui of v. Hence T pvq ď

v. From Figure 9, we immediately get that if ui sends charge 5 to v, then one of its neighbors, say, ui`1 ,
has degree 5. But if ui`1 has degree 5 and sends charge 5 to v, then, again by Figure 9, its neighbor ui
has degree 5. Thus we conclude that all neighbors u0 , . . . , u11 of v have degree 5, but then we see that
the configuration in D shown in Figure 10 is contained in B18 pvq Ď B28 pvq, a contradiction.


8      Reducible configurations around low-degree vertices
Cartwheel enumeration. Our basic use of computers is for the following cartwheel enumeration: for
a center vertex v of prescribed bounded degree dpvq and final charge T pvq ě 0, assuming there are no
separating 3- or 4-cycles, enumerate all possible configurations of B s 8 pvq where v gets the prescribed final
                                                                       2
charge and where no reducible configuration from D is found in B28 pvq. We note here that by Lemma
6.4, Bs 8 pvq determines the final charge of v. For the center v, the maximal degree we consider here is 11
       2
(for larger degrees we have Lemma 7.3), and for other vertices in B   s 8 pvq it does not matter if the degree
                                                                         2
is 9 or larger, so it suffices to consider degrees up to 9. Thus we only have to consider a finite number
of extended degree bounded cartwheels, but the number is large: even if we exploit that the minimum
degree is 5, the number of such cartwheels is bigger than 2100 . For each of these cartwheels, we have
more than 8200 reducible configurations in D to look after, so the number of combinations is far too
large for any computer to handle. We therefore have to design an analysis that identifies large groups of
cartwheels that can be efficiently checked together. The cartwheel enumeration algorithm is described
in Section 11.
    For the classic 4CT proof, it suffices to consider 2-local neighborhoods (c.f. Theorem 4.5), but here
we need to consider much larger neighborhoods (up to 12-local in Theorem 6.5 (iii)), and to do so, we
rely on human reasoning.
                                                                                      s 8 pvq. Suppose
Lemma 8.1. Let v be a vertex in G such that there are no separating 3- or 4-cycles in B 2
that one of the following holds:

    1. 9 ď dG pvq ď 11 and T pvq ě 0,
    2. 7 ď dG pvq ď 8 and T pvq ą 0, or
    3. 7 ď dG pvq ď 8 and T pvq “ 0 and all neighbors of v have degree at most 6.
Then the degree-bounded ball B28 pvq contains a D-reducible configuration from D. For the cases where
T pvq ą 0, we only need configurations from D0 .
Sketch of computer-assisted proof. The proof just does the above cartwheel enumeration to check that
there are no counterexamples.

Lemma 8.2. Suppose that v is a vertex in G such that all vertices at distance at most 2 from v are of

Proof. A vertex of degree at most 4 is by itself a D-reducible configuration in D, so we can assume that
all vertices in B2 pvq are of degree five and six. Then a configuration in Figure 11 is contained in the
degree-bounded ball B28 pvq, except if v has degree five and is surrounded by neighbors of degree exactly
six. If this is the case, then any one of the neighbors of v and all its neighbors constitutes one of the
configurations in Figure 11, which is in D.
