B3 pvq have degrees of at most 7.
    Thus we may assume that B3 pvq contains T73 with vertices v1 v2 v3 . Then we can apply Lemma 8.5 to
                                                                                             Ť3
v1 v2 v3 because every vertex in v1 v2 v3 Ď B3 pvq has final charge 0 and the vertices in B “ i“1 B2 pvi q Ď
B5 pvq have degrees of at most 7.
    Combining Lemma 8.2, 8.4, and 8.7 implies the remaining case of Theorem 6.5.

Theorem 6.5.      Let G be a triangulation and v P V pGq.

(iii) If dpvq ď 8 and all vertices in B12 pvq have degree at most 8, and have final charge 0, then B12 pvq
      contains either a separating 3- or 4-cycle, or a reducible configuration in D.
Proof. If dpvq “ 8, we can directly apply Lemma 8.4 to v. We consider the case that dpvq “ 7. If B5 pvq
contains no vertex of degree 8, we can apply Lemma 8.7 to v. Otherwise, let v 1 be a vertex of degree 8
in B5 pvq. We can apply Lemma 8.4 to v 1 because every vertex u P B3 pv 1 q Ď B8 pvq has final charge 0 and
all vertices in B5 pv 1 q Ď B10 pvq have degree at most 8. It remains to consider the case where dpvq “ 5 or
6. If all vertices in B2 pvq have degree at most 6, we can apply Lemma 8.2. Otherwise, let v 1 be a vertex
of degree 7 or 8 in B2 pvq. From the discussion for the case where dpvq “ 7 or 8, if every vertex u P B8 pv 1 q
has final charge 0 and all vertices in B10 pv 1 q have degree at most 8, then the claim holds. This is exactly
the case because B8 pv 1 q Ď B10 pvq and B10 pv 1 q Ď B12 pvq.


9     Homomorphisms, free combinations, and the maximum dis-
      charge along an edge
In this section, we will discuss in more detail how we use computer to model and bound the discharge
over an edge in an arbitrary triangulation as stated in Lemma 7.1. We shall develop a formalism that
shall be reused in other parts of the computer proof in the appendix.
    In our analysis, we will often need to combine near-triangulations and configurations. One important
example is when we want to check if several of our discharging rules in Figure 6 could possibly be
combined so as to send charge along the same edge uv. We want this to be equivalent to asking if the
near-triangulations of these rules can be combined around uv, including restrictions on the degrees on
the outer cycles. We will consider the near-triangulations as embedded with a given rotation (that is,
clockwise orientation) around the vertices. As is the case for our discharging rules, we assume that the
near-triangulations discussed below are all without cut-vertices. (Conveniently, our analysis will never
combine near-triangulations having a cut-vertex, even though they do appear in some of our reducible
configurations.) This implies that the embedding is unique.
    At the end of this section, we want to prove Lemma 7.1 stating that the maximal discharge over
any edge is 8. The lemma discusses what can be done when considering how the discharging rules
can be embedded and combined in a triangulation G, but, with a computer, we cannot consider all
possible triangulations. Instead, we will consider the so-called “free combinations” that we can model
on a computer with no reference to G, and such that if rules can be combined in G, then they can also
be combined in their free combination, and we will say that the free combination succeeds. Therefore,
restrictions on which discharging rules can be freely combined impose upper bounds on what can be
combined in any triangulation.
    Finally, we lower the upper bound 8 in Lemma 7.1 to 5 in Lemma 7.2 by limiting the combinations
of discharging rules by allowing only those that do not give rise to separating 3- or 4-cycles or reducible
configurations. Again, the computer will only look at free combinations.

9.1    Homomorphic images of near-triangulations and configurations
For now, we assume that all configurations have a single specified degree δpvq for each vertex. When we
describe reducible configurations and configurations used in discharging rules, we often specify degree
ranges on the boundary, but this is only a shorthand for concrete degrees, and we shall return to this



                                                      23
issue in Section 9.5. For simplicity, we assume that our near-triangulations and configurations have no
cut-vertices. We shall return to cut-vertices in Section 10.
    An orientation-preserving homomorphism or just homomorphism of a near-triangulation Z without
cut-vertices into a triangulation G is a map ϕ : V pZq Ñ V pGq such that adjacent vertices in Z are
mapped to adjacent vertices in G, and the inner facial triangles in Z are mapped to facial triangles of
G preserving their orientation. In other words, we require that ϕ preserves clockwise orientation around
any vertex. Thus, ϕ is defined on V pZq, but it can be extended in an obvious way so that it is also
defined on EpZq. If the near-triangulation Z is in a configuration pZ, δq, then for any vertex v in Z, we
further require dG pϕpvqq “ δpvq. If ϕ is 1-1, then we call ϕ an embedding. (Note that we require that
embeddings preserve clockwise orientation.) If a configuration pZ, δq is embedded in G, we also say that
pZ, δq is contained in G.
Lemma 9.1. Let Z be a near-triangulation without cut-vertices. If ϕ is a homomorphism from Z to G,
then ϕ is locally 1-1 around each vertex, that is, for any vertex v P V pZq, the mapping restricted to v
and all its neighbors is 1-1.
Proof. Since ϕ preserves orientation of adjacent triangles around v and dZ pvq ď δpvq “ dG pϕpvqq, it
cannot happen that two neighbors of v would be mapped to the same neighbor of ϕpvq in G. Therefore,
ϕ is locally 1-1 around v.
   We now show that, if Z is of small diameter and G has no separating 3- or 4-cycle, all of whose
vertices are in ϕpZq, then every homomorphism from Z to G is an embedding.
Lemma 9.2. Let Z be a near-triangulation of diameter at most 4 and with no cut-vertex. If ϕ is a
homomorphism from Z to G, and G has no separating 3- or 4-cycle whose vertices are in ϕpZq, then ϕ
is 1-1, hence an embedding.
Proof. If ϕ is not 1-1, then Z has two distinct vertices x, y mapped to the same vertex by ϕ. By
assumption, Z has a path P of length at most 4 joining x, y. Lemma 9.1 implies that ϕpP q is a cycle
of length at most 4. The same lemma implies that P cannot have length 2 and assuming P is shortest
possible, the cycle ϕpP q has at least one vertex in its interior. Hence ϕpP q is a separating 3- or 4-cycle,
a contradiction.
    Even though our graphs are undirected, it is often convenient to think of an edge xy as oriented in
the sense of having a tail x and a head y. If a homomorphism ϕ maps xy to uv, then we write ϕpxyq “ uv
and we assume that ϕpxq “ u and ϕpyq “ v.
    It is important to notice that for a near-triangulation Z without cut-vertices, a homomorphism ϕ
from Z into a triangulation G is unique as soon as we have decided the image of an oriented edge; for
using the incidences between facial triangles and their sides, this recursively determines the images of all
facial triangles. A detailed construction of ϕ is given below in Section 9.2.
    We note that this also implies that when we are looking for a possible homomorphic image of a
configuration pZ, δq in G, we just have to decide the image ϕpxyq of a single oriented edge xy in Z. Via
the above incidences, this determines where the rest should go, so either we complete the image of pZ, δq
with the given image of ϕpxyq, or we discover that there is no such homomorphism ϕ from pZ, δq to G.
    Since embeddings are simply 1-1 homomorphisms, to check for an embedding, we need to verify
whether there is a homomorphism and, if so, whether it is 1-1. This is how our 4-coloring algorithm can
look for reducible configurations in G. We only consider the reducible configurations in our fixed set D,
including their mirror images. For each, we can decide on an oriented edge, and try to match it to each
of the two orientations of the m edges in G.

Configurations contained in configurations. We define a homomorphism ϕ from a configuration
pZ, δq to a configuration pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q just like we did it to a triangulation G but with the additional require-
ment that for all vertices v in Z, δ ˚ pϕpvqq “ δpvq. If the homomorphism is 1-1, then it is an embedding,
and then pZ, δq is contained in pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q.
    A typical application scenario is as follows. Suppose a configuration pZ, δq of diameter at most 4
(e.g., one of our reducible configurations) is contained via ϕ in pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q. Suppose further that we have
a homomorphism ϕ˚ from pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q to a triangulation G. Now, ϕ˚ ˝ ϕ is a homomorphism from pZ, δq to
G. Since pZ, δq is of diameter at most 4, we conclude by Lemma 9.2 that either G has a separating 3- or
4-cycle with vertices in V pϕ˚ pϕpZqqq Ď V pϕ˚ pZ ˚ qq, or ϕ˚ ˝ ϕ embeds pZ, δq in G.


                                                      24
9.2    Growing near-triangulations, configurations and homomorphisms
In this section we want to understand when we do or, perhaps more important, when we do not have a
homomorphism ϕ from a configuration pZ, δq into another configuration pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q mapping a given edge
uv from Z into a given edge u˚ v ˚ in Z ˚ . We assume that Z has no cut-vertices. The details will be
important in Section 11.
    First we describe a way to grow a sub-near-triangulation Z 1 of Z starting from an arbitrary edge
uv P EpZq. Here sub-near-triangulation means that Z 1 is a near-triangulation that is a subgraph of Z
and where all inner facial triangles of Z 1 are also inner facial triangles of Z. We will maintain that Z 1
has no cut-vertices. We say that the Z 1 is edge-closed if it satisfies the following: for each vertex v in Z 1
with dZ 1 pvq “ dZ pvq and where v is internal in Z, we have that v is internal in Z 1 .
Lemma 9.3. If Z 1 ‰ Z is edge-closed, then there is a triangle xyz in Z with xy a boundary edge of Z 1
and z not in Z 1 .
Proof. For any edge x1 y 1 on the outer cycle of Z 1 , there is a unique facial triangle x1 y 1 z 1 which is in Z but
not Z 1 . If z 1 is not in Z 1 we are done. Otherwise, adding this facial triangle to Z 1 gives a plane graph with
precisely one bounded face F which contains vertices in Z but not Z 1 (because Z 1 is edge-closed). We
choose x1 y 1 such that we minimize the number of vertices in F . If xy is any edge in Z 1 on the boundary
of F , then the unique triangle in Z but not Z 1 satisfies the conclusion of Lemma 9.3.


     We are now ready to provide the specifics of the growth of Z 1 starting the edge uv P EpZq, that is,
from Z 1 “ ptu, vu, tuvuq which is trivially edge-closed.
     When Z 1 ‰ Z and Z 1 is edge-closed, we add a neighboring triangle xyz from Lemma 9.3.
     Next we do the following edge-closure process. While there is a vertex w that in Z is internal but in
Z 1 is missing only one edge from its neighbor cycle, we add this missing edge, thus completing the wheel
around w in Z 1 . When done with edge-closure, Z 1 is edge-closed.
     We repeat adding neighboring triangles followed by edge closures as above until Z 1 “ Z. Clearly,

Fact 9.4. If Z is in a configuration pZ, δq and Z 1 is an edge-closed, sub-near-triangulation of Z, then
pZ 1 , δq is also a configuration, that is, every boundary vertex v of Z 1 has dZ 1 pvq ă δpvq.
     We now have a simple way of growing the unique homomorphism ϕ from pZ, δq to pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q if possible.
     First we set ϕpuq “ u˚ and ϕpvq “ v ˚ . Next we grow pZ, δq from uv as described above. When we add
the outside triangle xyz to the current boundary edge xy of Z 1 , we have already fixed x˚ y ˚ “ ϕpxyq in
Z ˚ and then there should be a corresponding triangle x˚ y ˚ z ˚ on the same side of x˚ y ˚ in Z ˚ . Otherwise,
we conclude that z is ”out of bounds” in the sense that no homomorphism mapping uv to u˚ v ˚ can map
z into Z ˚ .
     Next we do the edge-closures on Z 1 . Every time the edge-closure adds an edge xy to Z 1 , we check if
ϕpxqϕpyq is an edge in Z ˚ . Otherwise we conclude that we have a ”homomorphic edge error” implying
that the desired homomorphism does not exist.
     Now for configurations, to check if we have a homomorphism ϕ from pZ, δq to pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q, mapping uv
to u˚ v ˚ , we can first check if there is such a homomorphism from Z to Z ˚ , and next, for each vertex w
in Z check if δpwq “ δ ˚ pϕpwqq.
     However, we can also use the degree functions to discover errors earlier. First we check that δpuq “
δ ˚ pu˚ q and δpvq “ δ ˚ pv ˚ q. Later, every time we add the neighboring triangle xyz with z outside to
Z 1 , when identifying the corresponding x˚ y ˚ z ˚ in Z ˚ , we check that δpzq “ δ ˚ pz ˚ q. In any of these
degree checks fail, we have a ”degree error”. Note that with degree checks, we do not have to check for
”homomorphic edge errors”, for when we added the last edge in the wheel around a vertex w, since it
has the same degree as its image w˚ , we have a corresponding edge in the wheel around w˚ in Z ˚ .
     The strength of the above degree check is that if it fails on z when we added xyz to Z 1 (but before
edge-closures), then although we do have a homomorphism ϕ from Z 1 to Z ˚ showing that z has to map
to z ˚ , we can nevertheless stop the growth because we have found a degree error from Z 1 to Z ˚ . This
concrete witness that there is no homomorphism from pZ, δq to pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q will be important in Section 11.




                                                         25
9.3    Pseudo-triangulations and pseudo-configurations
In this section, we introduce pseudo-triangulations and pseudo-configurations.
    In our computer-assisted proofs, we use the computer to enumerate models of all relevant local neigh-
borhoods in a triangulation, e.g., all possible combinations of discharging rules sending charge along the
same edge in the same direction. For these computer models, it turns out to be much simpler if we allow
the computer to operate with some more general classes of graphs. We will define pseudo-triangulations
generalizing triangulations and near-triangulations, and pseudo-configurations generalizing configura-
tions. These graphs are multigraphs, allowing loops and multiple edges. The pseudo-triangulations will
locally look like triangulations or near-triangulations, but they do not have to be connected or planar
(indeed, the computer will never check whether they are connected or planar). Pseudo-configurations are
pseudo-triangulations with a degree function on boundary vertices in the same way that configurations
are near-triangulations with a degree function on the boundary vertices.

Dart representations and pseudo-triangulations. To define pseudo-triangulations, we will use
dart representations, whose definition is inspired by the classic representation of maps. A map is a 2-
dimensional cell complex that represents a 2-cell embedding of a graph into a surface (possibly with one
or more boundary components). Our representation is analogous to the concept of a map representation
developed by Klein [Kle79] and formalized by Tutte [Tut73]; for a more recent work, we refer to [KKM` 21,
KMNZ25]. However, contrary to this work, we will allow a dart to be its own reverse. In particular, we
get a one-dart loop that may be thought of as a terminal object in category theory (in formal program
specification [BW95]), and which will play an important role in our constructions.
    Formally, our dart representation has a set V of vertices and a set D of oriented edges or darts.
Each dart e P D has four pointers: headpeq to a vertex v which we call its head vertex, revpeq P D to a
reverse dart which we say has the opposite direction unless revpeq “ e, succpeq to the succeeding dart
with the same head v, and predpeq to the preceding dart with the same head v. Both succpeq and predpeq
can be nil, which means that e has no successor or predecessor. These nil-pointers are essential for our
interpretation of a boundary. The tail of e is defined as tailpeq “ headprevpeqq.
    If tailpeq “ headpeq, then e is a loop. If e “ revpeq, then clearly e is a “one-dart” loop. But, we also
get a “two-dart” loop if e ‰ f “ revpeq and headpeq “ headpf q.
    From the perspective of the computer program, the above dart representation should be taken formally
as having two types of objects, vertices and darts, where the darts have four associated pointers, one to
a vertex and three to darts. This format is what defines a dart representation.
    Below, we define the requirements we aim to satisfy; however, while the computer manipulates the
dart representations, these requirements will be temporarily suspended.
    First, we have some basic requirements (M1)–(M5):
(M1) There are no isolated vertices, i.e., for each vertex v P V , there is a dart e with headpeq “ v.
(M2) rev : D Ñ D is an involution, that is, e “ revprevpeqq.
(M3) succ and pred map D to the set D Y tnil u. They are inverse to each other when non-nil, that is, if
     we have two (non-nil ) darts e and f , then e “ predpf q if and only if f “ succpeq.
(M4) Heads are consistent in the sense that if e has successor f ‰ nil, then headpeq “ headpf q.
(M5) If succpeq ‰ nil, then there are darts f and g such that f “ revpsuccpeqq, g “ revpsuccpf qq, and
     e “ revpsuccpgqq.

If e, f, g in (M5) are distinct and their three heads a, b, c are also distinct, then we call e, f, g an inner
facial triangle with vertices a, b, and c.
    The requirement (M5) implies that if e has a successor, then revpeq has a predecessor; namely the
dart g above. If a dart e has no successor or has no predecessor, we call it a boundary dart and its head a
boundary vertex. Then its reverse dart is also a boundary dart, and hence the tail of e is also a boundary
vertex. All other vertices and darts are called inner.
    By (M3), the succ and pred pointers partition the darts into doubly-linked lists, each of which may
or may not be cyclic. If a list is acyclic, we view it as ordered following successors, starting from the
dart e with predpeq “ nil. By the head consistency requirement (M4), the darts in each list all have the


                                                     26
same head. In addition to the basic requirements (M1)–(M5), we have one last requirement for a dart
representation:
(M6) Single-list condition: For each vertex v, there is exactly one list with v as its head. This is called
     the incidence list of v. The number of darts in the incidence list is the degree dpvq of v. The list
     is cyclic if v is an inner vertex. If v is a boundary vertex, then its incidence list is acyclic with nil
     at both ends.
When (M1)–(M6) are satisfied, it follows that a vertex is inner if and only if its incidence list is cyclic.
If our dart representation satisfies all the above requirements, then we call it a pseudo-triangulation
(although “pseudo-near-triangulation” might have been a better word).
    We note that any triangulation or near-triangulation without cut-vertices has a unique representation
as above, where each edge gives two darts that are reverse to each other, each dart representing one
orientation of the edge. Moreover, succ corresponds to the clockwise orientation of the darts around the
vertices. Also note that (M6) implies that we cannot represent a near-triangulation with a cut-vertex.
    We now extend the definition of orientation-preserving homomorphisms to dart representations. An
orientation-preserving homomorphism or just homomorphism from a dart representation Z to another
dart representation Z 1 is a mapping ϕ from vertices and darts of Z to vertices and darts in Z 1 such that
for any dart e in Z:
    • headpϕpeqq “ ϕpheadpeqq;
    • revpϕpeqq “ ϕprevpeqq;
    • if succpeq ‰ nil, then succpϕpeqq “ ϕpsuccpeqq;
    • if predpeq ‰ nil, then predpϕpeqq “ ϕppredpeqq.
Note that, if we have a connected dart representation Z satisfying (M6), then its homomorphism ϕ into a
dart representation Z 1 is uniquely determined as soon as we have decided the image e1 P EpZ 1 q of a dart
e P EpZq. The reason is that, starting with e, we can trace all of Z just using pointers to heads, reverse
darts, successors, and predecessors, and a homomorphism has to follow the corresponding pointers in
the image. A homomorphism with ϕpeq “ e1 may not be possible, so at the end, we do have to check
that the constructed mapping really is a homomorphism.
    We say that Z 1 is a minimal image of Z if all vertices and darts in Z 1 are images of vertices and darts
in Z, and moreover, for any dart e1 in Z 1 , the dart e1 has a non-nil successor/predecessor if and only if
there exists a dart e P EpZq with ϕpeq “ e1 , where e has non-nil successor/predecessor.
    The following lemma is now straightforward:
Lemma 9.5. Let Z, Z 1 be dart representations such that Z 1 is a minimal image of Z. If Z satisfies the
basic requirements (M1)–(M5) for pseudo-triangulations, then so does Z 1 .
   Note that the definition of a homomorphism implies that inner triangles of the pseudo-triangulation
Z are mapped to inner triangles of a pseudo-triangulation H. Note also that our general definition of
homomorphisms of pseudo-triangulations coincides with the original one if Z is a near-triangulation and
H is a triangulation.

The terminal pseudo-triangulation: a special one-dart loop. Recall that a loop is a dart e with
headpeq “ tailpeq “ headprevpeqq where e may or may not be its own reverse. We note that we have
a special pseudo-triangulation consisting of a single vertex v and a one-dart loop e with v “ headpeq
and e “ revpeq “ succpeq “ predpeq. It trivially satisfies (M1)–(M6) (with (M5) as a degenerate case of
a facial triangle where all sides are the same), and it is the image of every pseudo-triangulation using
the unique homomorphism that maps all darts to the single dart and all vertices to the single vertex.
As such, it plays the role of a terminal object in category theory, and we refer to it as the terminal
pseudo-triangulation (see [BW95]). While this one-dart loop has the terminal role in category theory, we
note that in the theory of combinatorial embeddings [MT01], they typically only allow two-dart loops
because no dart is allowed to be its own reverse, but then there is no terminal pseudo-triangulation.
    An example showing that the above terminal loop is not always a minimal image (although it is
always an image) is if we consider a near-triangulation with a single boundary edge between two vertices


                                                      27
u and v. Its dart representation has two darts e1 and e2 that are reverse to each other, and whose heads
are headpe1 q “ y and headpe2 q “ x. Both are on the boundary, so all successors and predecessors are nil,
but then a minimal image must also have nil-pointers for all successors and predecessors. Consequently,
an image need not contain a minimal image.
    We finally make two simple observations concerning loops.
   • If there are no loops, and if there is a dart e with non-nil successor, then in (M5), the edges e, f ,
     g are all distinct, forming an inner facial triangle.
   • If there is a loop in a pseudo-triangulation, then it is mapped to a loop in any homomorphic image.

Pseudo-configurations. A pseudo-configuration pZ, δq is a pseudo-triangulation Z without loops to-
gether with a degree function δ such that for each v P V pZq, δpvq “ dZ pvq if v is an inner vertex, while
δpvq ą dZ pvq if v is a boundary vertex.
    A homomorphism from one pseudo-configuration pZ, δq to another pZ 1 , δ 1 q is a homomorphism ϕ from
Z to Z 1 such that for every vertex v P Z, δ 1 pϕpvqq “ δpvq. The requirement that degrees have to be pre-
served makes homomorphisms between pseudo-configurations far more restrictive than homomorphisms
between pseudo-triangulations.
    One of the important properties of homomorphisms between pseudo-configurations is that they can
be composed to new homomorphisms, that is, if ϕ is a homomorphism from pZ, δq to pZ 1 , δ 1 q and ϕ1 is a
homomorphism from pZ 1 , δ 1 q to pZ 2 , δ 2 q, then ϕ1 ˝ ϕ is a homomorphism from pZ, δq to pZ 2 , δ 2 q.
    We shall, for example, use this when pZ, δq is a reducible configuration, pZ 1 , δ 1 q is a pseudo-
configuration that is the free combination of discharging rules, and Z 2 is an arbitrary triangulation
G, and δ 2 “ dG . Then ϕ2 “ ϕ1 ˝ ϕ is a homomorphism from pZ, δq to G, and if pZ, δq is of diameter at
most 4, then ϕ2 is 1-1 by Lemma 9.2, implying that ϕ2 is a planar embedding of pZ, δq in G. Crucially,
this holds even if Z 1 itself is not planar. (We believe that Z 1 must be planar in this case, but we do not
need that, and we do not check it.)

9.4     Requesting identification of darts
We will now present the basic subroutine that shall be used for the free combination of configurations.
We are given a pseudo-configuration pZ, δq and a request to identify a set of pairs of darts

                                           S “ tpe1 , f1 q, . . . , pek , fk qu.

We shall refer to each individual pair pe1 , f1 q as an identification request. Consider a pseudo-configuration
pZ 1 , δ 1 q that is the homomorphic image of pZ, δq via some homomorphism ϕ1 . We say that this homo-
morphic image respects S if for i “ 1, . . . , k, we have ϕ1 pei q “ ϕ1 pfi q. Whenever a homomorphic image
respecting S exists, we can construct one, pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q, together with a homomorphism ϕ˚ : pZ, δq Ñ pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q
(see Lemma 9.9 below) such that the following holds. If there exists any other pZ 1 , δ 1 q that is a homo-
morphic image of pZ, δq via some homomorphism ϕ1 that respects S, then there is a homomorphism
ϕ˚ 1 : pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q Ñ pZ 1 , δ 1 q such that ϕ˚ 1 ˝ ϕ˚ “ ϕ1 . We call pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q the free homomorphic image respect-
ing S.

Free combinations of discharging rules. As mentioned earlier, we shall define a free combination
of discharging rules so that if the rules can be combined in some triangulation to send charge over
an oriented edge st, then we can also form their free combination. Let pZ1 , δ1 q, . . . , pZk , δk q be the
configurations of these discharging rules with si ti the discharging edge of pZi , δi q. When we say they can
be combined in a triangulation G to send a charge over the dart st, we mean that for each i “ 1, 2, . . . , k,
we have an embedding ϕi from pZi , δi q to G with ϕi psi ti q “ st.
    Translating into the setup of pseudo-configurations with identifications, we consider the pseudo-
configuration pZ \ , δ \ q which is the disjoint union of pst, dG q and all the pZi , δi q (1 ď i ď k). Then our
identification requests are pst, si ti q for i “ 1, . . . , k.
    The union of the embeddings ϕi shows us that we have a homomorphism ϕ\ from pZ \ , δ \ q to G that
respects all our identification requests, so we know that we will successfully construct a free homomorphic
image pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q of pZ \ , δ \ q respecting the identifications. Then pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q is the previously claimed free
combination of pZ1 , δ1 q, . . . , pZk , δk q identifying the darts si ti .


                                                           28
  We note that our discharging rules actually have specified degree ranges, not just individual degrees.
We shall return to this issue in Section 9.5.

9.4.1   The free homomorphism from a pseudo-triangulation respecting identification re-
        quests.
Given a pseudo-triangulation Z (without degree constraints) and some identification requests
pe1 , f1 q, . . . , pek , fk q, we will construct a special homomorphism ϕ˚ defined on Z into a pseudo-
triangulation Z ˚ respecting the identification requests, that is, ϕ˚ pei q “ ϕpfi q for i “ 1, . . . , k We
note that there always is such a Z ˚ ; namely, the terminal pseudo-triangulation which identifies all darts
in a single one-dart loop. However, the homomorphism ϕ˚ that we construct will respect the identifi-
cation requests, but otherwise identify as few vertices and darts as possible. At the end, our image Z ˚
will be unique up to isomorphism, and we will call pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q is the free homomorphic image respecting
our identification requests.
     We start the construction by placing all given identification requests in a queue of unprocessed iden-
tification requests.
     The construction of ϕ˚ is iterative, identifying more and more vertices and darts. We will maintain
the identifications in a union-find data structure, specifically a forest of rooted trees. The nodes of the
trees are the vertices and darts in Z, and we let ϕ˚ of a vertex/dart be its root in the forest. When we
are done constructing Z ˚ , the roots will be the vertices and darts of Z ˚ .
     As we construct Z ˚ , we may add new dart identification requests pe, f q to our queue of unprocessed
identification requests. The guarantee for any pe, f q in the queue is that when we are done constructing
ϕ˚ , we will have ϕ˚ peq “ ϕ˚ pf q.
     As we construct Z ˚ , we will also have to reassign some of the pointers P P thead, rev, succ, predu. For
every dart e in Z, we will use P peq to denote the pointer value in Z, while P ˚ peq denotes the current
value in the construction of Z ˚ . Initially, P ˚ peq “ P peq for all darts in e. The head and rev pointers will
only be changed in the very end, but for P P tsucc, predu, we shall maintain the invariant that if e˚ is a
current root, then P ˚ pe˚ q ‰ nil if and only if P ˚ pe˚ q “ P peq ‰ nil for some e in Z with root e˚ .
     When we start, each vertex and dart forms its own tree, so ϕ˚ is just the identity function, which is
a trivial homomorphism. However, the identity does not respect any of our identification requests.
     We now take an arbitrary identification request pe, f q from our queue of unprocessed identification
requests. If we already have ϕ˚ peq “ ϕ˚ pf q, then no further processing is needed for this request.
     Below, we describe the processing of an identification request pe, f q where we do not already have
ϕ˚ peq “ ϕ˚ pf q. Let e˚ “ ϕ˚ peq and f ˚ “ ϕ˚ pf q. Our first step is to do the identification itself by making
e˚ a child of f ˚ in the forest of rooted trees. Thereby f ˚ becomes the root representative of both e and
f with ϕ˚ peq “ ϕ˚ pf q “ f ˚ . However, for ϕ˚ to become a homomorphism, we need to make several
other identifications. First of all, the heads should also be identified, so if ϕ˚ pheadpeqq ‰ ϕ˚ pheadpf qq,
we make ϕ˚ pheadpeqq a child of ϕ˚ pheadpf qq, so that we also get ϕ˚ pheadpeqq “ ϕ˚ pheadpf qq.
     If we did not have e˚ “ f ˚ , we would also generate up to three new identification requests, called
homomorphic identification requests. We always generate the identification request prevpe˚ q, revpf ˚ qq.
For P P tsucc, predu, if neither P ˚ pe˚ q nor P ˚ pf ˚ q is nil, we generate the homomorphic identification
request pP ˚ pe˚ q, P ˚ pf ˚ qq; otherwise we make no identification request on the successors. However, if
P ˚ pf ˚ q “ nil and P ˚ pe˚ q ‰ nil, we set P ˚ pf ˚ q “ P ˚ pe˚ q.
     The homomorphic identification requests are all added to our queue of identification requests to be
processed. When we process a homomorphic identification request, we refer to it as a homomorphic
identification.
     We continue processing identification requests from our queue until it is empty. This must happen
eventually, since we generate new identification requests only when we perform a new identification,
thereby reducing the total number of representative roots by one.
     We are now done with the construction of ϕ˚ . The vertices/darts in Z are mapped by ϕ˚ to their
representative roots, and these roots form the set of vertices/darts in Z ˚ . We now need to assign final
pointers to each of these darts e˚ in Z ˚ . Recall that we use a superscript ˚ to indicate pointers in Z ˚ .
For P P thead, revu, we simply set P ˚ pe˚ q “ ϕ˚ pP ˚ pe˚ qq “ ϕ˚ pP pe˚ qq. For P P tsucc, predu, we set
P ˚ pe˚ q “ ϕ˚ pP ˚ pe˚ qq if P ˚ pe˚ q ‰ nil; otherwise we leave P ˚ pe˚ q “ nil.
     We will refer to ϕ˚ and Z ˚ as the free homomorphism and free homomorphic image of Z respecting
S. So far, we have not even proved that ϕ˚ is a homomorphism. We shall prove that ϕ˚ is not only a


                                                      29
homomorphism, but it is the uniquely most general one in the sense that any other homomorphism ϕ1
from Z respecting S can be obtained from ϕ˚ by composition with another homomorphism ϕ˚1 , that is,
ϕ1 “ ϕ˚1 ˝ ϕ˚ . It is the latter aspect that makes us call ϕ˚ and Z ˚ “free”.
   The pseudocode for constructing ϕ˚ and Z ˚ respecting S is found in Algorithm A.3.1.

Analysis. We shall now analyze the process outlined above. At the moment, it is not even clear if Z ˚
is a pseudo-triangulation, but it is a dart representation.
Lemma 9.6. The constructed map ϕ˚ is a homomorphism from Z to the constructed Z ˚ which is the
minimal image (as defined before Lemma 9.5) of Z under ϕ˚ .
Proof. For ϕ˚ to be a homomorphism to the final Z ˚ , we need to prove for each dart e in Z, and for
each pointer P that if P peq ‰ nil, then ϕ˚ pP peqq “ P ˚ pϕ˚ peqq. The minimality also requires for e˚ P Z ˚
that P ˚ pe˚ q “ nil if P peq “ nil for every dart e in Z with ϕ˚ peq “ e.
    The simplest case is for P P thead, revu where we do not have issues with nil. Suppose e˚ “ ϕ˚ peq.
This implies that e has been transitively identified with e˚ (resulting in the tree containing e having root
e˚ ), and for each of these identifications pe1 , e2 q, we ensured that we would end up with ϕ˚ pP pe1 qq “
ϕ˚ pP pe2 qq. We conclude that we end up with ϕ˚ pP peqq “ ϕ˚ pP pe˚ qq. After the construction of ϕ˚ , for
each root e˚ , we set P ˚ pe˚ q “ ϕ˚ pP pe˚ qq, so we conclude that ϕ˚ pP peqq “ P ˚ pϕ˚ peqq, as desired.
    We now consider the more complicated case where P P tsucc, predu. Our construction maintained
the invariant that if e˚ is a current root then P ˚ pe˚ q ‰ nil if and only if P ˚ pe˚ q “ P pf q ‰ nil for some
f in Z with root e˚ . To prove that we get a homomorphism, we need the following additional invariant:
      Consider any dart e P Z with P peq ‰ nil and root e˚ . Then P ˚ pe˚ q “ P pf q ‰ nil for some f
      with root e˚ , and then we will end up with ϕ˚ pP peqq “ ϕ˚ pP pf qq.
To see this, let e, e˚ , f be as above, and suppose we identify pe1 , e2 q with ϕ˚ pe1 q “ e˚ and ϕ˚ pe2 q “ g ˚ ‰
e˚ . This is the case where we will make e˚ a child of g ˚ .
    Suppose we had P ˚ pg ˚ q “ P phq ‰ nil. This value will not change, but we generate the identification
request pP ˚ pe˚ q, P ˚ pg ˚ qq “ pP pf q, P phqq, so we will get ϕ˚ pP pf qq “ ϕ˚ pP phqq. The invariant on e, e˚ , f
implied that we would get ϕ˚ pP peqq “ ϕ˚ pP pf qq, so we conclude that we end up with ϕ˚ pP peqq “
ϕ˚ pP phqq, recovering the invariant for e, g ˚ , h.
    Suppose instead that P ˚ pg ˚ q “ nil. This is the case where we set P ˚ pg ˚ q “ P ˚ pe˚ q “ P pf q. This
immediately recovers the invariant for e, g ˚ , f .
    We could also have identified pe2 , e1 q, making g ˚ a child of e˚ . Since we had P ˚ pe˚ q ‰ nil, we would
not change P ˚ pe˚ q, so the invariant on e, e˚ , f is preserved. Since the invariant is maintained in all cases,
it remains true when we are done constructing ϕ˚ .
    Let e, e˚ , f be as described in the invariant when we are done constructing ϕ˚ . Since P ˚ pe˚ q ‰ nil,
the final step is to set P ˚ pe˚ q “ ϕ˚ pP ˚ pe˚ qq “ ϕ˚ pP pf qq “ ϕ˚ pP peqq, as required for ϕ˚ to be a
homomorphism from Z to Z ˚ .
    Finally we note that Z ˚ is a minimal image since we maintain that P ˚ pe˚ q “ nil if P ˚ pf q “ nil for
every f with ϕ˚ pf q “ e˚ .
Lemma 9.7. The constructed ϕ˚ and Z ˚ are unique up to an isomorphism. Moreover, suppose there is
a homomorphism ϕ1 from Z to some pseudo-triangulation Z 1 and that ϕ1 respects the identifications in
S. Then there also exists a homomorphism ϕ˚1 from Z ˚ to Z 1 such that ϕ1 “ ϕ˚1 ˝ ϕ˚ .
Proof. Let ϕ1 be an arbitrary homomorphism from Z respecting S, as stated in the lemma. We claim
that in the construction of ϕ˚ , there can never be a first homomorphic identification request pe, f q such
that ϕ1 peq ‰ ϕ1 pf q. This follows because if we, for all previously processed identification requests, had
ϕ1 peq “ ϕ1 pf q, then the definition of a homomorphism ensures that all the homomorphic identification
requests pe1 , f 1 q generated from pe, f q also have ϕ1 pe1 q “ ϕ1 pf 1 q. We thus have ϕ˚ peq “ ϕ˚ pf q if and only
if ϕ1 peq “ ϕ1 pf q for every possible homomorphism. The identifications under ϕ˚ are thus unique, but
then the minimal homomorphic image Z ˚ must be unique up to an isomorphism.
     The map ϕ˚1 is defined by setting ϕ˚1 pϕ˚ pxqq “ ϕ1 pxq for every vertex or dart x in Z. We need to
argue that ϕ˚1 is a homomorphism, but this follows because Z ˚ was a minimal image. The argument
holds for all pointers; therefore, we focus only on the successor. If e˚ has successor f ˚ in Z ˚ , then we
know that there exists an e with successor f in Z with e˚ “ ϕ˚ peq and f ˚ “ ϕ˚ pf q. However, ϕ1 is a
homomorphism, so we also have ϕ1 pf q “ succ1 pϕ1 peqq, so indeed ϕ˚1 pf ˚ q “ succ1 pϕ˚1 pe˚ qq.


                                                         30
    From Lemma 9.6, we have that Z ˚ is a minimal homomorphic image, and since Z is a pseudo-
triangulation, we get from Lemma 9.5 that Z ˚ satisfies the basic requirements for being a pseudo-
triangulation. It remains to prove that Z ˚ satisfies the single-list condition.

Lemma 9.8. Z ˚ is a pseudo-triangulation.
Proof. It remains to prove the single-list condition (M6) that all darts with head y are in a single, possibly
cyclic, incidence list.
     To prove this, we partition darts and vertices from Z ˚ into equivalence classes. Vertices are in the
same equivalence class if they have been identified. Darts are in the same equivalence class if they are
connected via identifications, successor, or predecessor pointers. Note that we do not consider reverse
pointers.
     When we start from Z, we have no identifications, so each vertex has its own equivalence class. The
darts are partitioned into incidence lists, connected via successor and predecessor pointers.
     We claim that the construction of Z ˚ maintains the invariant that each vertex equivalence class is
the head set of exactly one dart equivalence class. This is trivially the case when we start from Z ˚ “ Z
since Z satisfies the single-list condition (M6).
     In the construction of Z ˚ , we now perform a series of dart identifications. Note that these do not
immediately change successor or predecessor pointers. Identifying darts could unite dart equivalence
classes, but every time we identify darts, we also identify their heads, so the invariant is maintained.
     In the final cleaning, we keep only a representative for each set of identified darts or vertices, redirect-
ing successor and predecessor pointers to go between representatives, but it is done so that the invariant
is maintained.
     Having only representatives, each vertex class consists of a single representative vertex. Each dart
equivalence class is representatives connected via successor and predecessor pointers, that is, an incidence
list. The invariant now implies that no two incidence lists can have the same head.
   Summing up, we have proved the following result.

Lemma 9.9. Suppose that we are given a pseudo-triangulation Z and a set S of dart identification
requests. Our construction yields a homomorphism ϕ˚ from Z to a a minimal image Z ˚ that is a
pseudo-triangulation. Both ϕ˚ and Z ˚ are unique up to an isomorphism. Moreover, consider any other
homomorphism ϕ1 from Z to a pseudo-triangulation Z 1 that respects S. Then there exists a homomor-
phism ϕ˚1 from Z ˚ to Z 1 such that ϕ˚1 ˝ ϕ˚ “ ϕ1 .

   Note that if Z 1 has no loops, then Z ˚ cannot have a loop since any loop in Z ˚ would be mapped to
loop in Z 1 .

9.4.2    The free homomorphism from a pseudo-configuration respecting identification re-
         quests
We are now given a pseudo-configuration pZ, δq from which we want to construct a free homomorphism
ϕ˚ into a pseudo-configuration pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q such that ϕ˚ respects a given set S “ tpe1 , f1 q, . . . , pek , fk qu of
input identification requests. Contrary to the case with pseudo-triangulations, there will be many cases
where the identifications fail to produce a pseudo-configuration. However, we will prove that we never
fail if there exists a pseudo-configuration pZ 1 , δ 1 q that is a homomorphic image of pZ, δq respecting S. In
that case, we will even show that there is a homomorphism ϕ˚1 from our pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q to pZ 1 , δ 1 q. Below we
first present the construction of pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q. Later we present the analysis showing that it has the desired
properties.
    For ease of reading, we will use uv for a dart with head v and tail u. Since we may have parallel
edges, u and v may not determine the arc uniquely, but the identity will be clear from the context, e.g.,
if we say that uv is the first arc in the incidence list of v.
    Our first step is to apply Lemma 9.9. This yields the pseudo-triangulation Z ˚ and a homomorphism
ϕ from Z to Z ˚ respecting S, but we do not expect to Z ˚ to the final answer. Rather we expect
  ˚

to manipulate Z ˚ over several rounds, possibly reporting an error. Recall that pseudo-configurations
have no loops, so we fail with a loop error if Z ˚ has a loop, that is, a dart e with headpeq “ tailpeq “
headprevpeqq. We now check if the degree constraints are satisfied. First of all, for all vertices u and v
with ϕ˚ puq “ ϕ˚ pvq, we need δpuq “ δpvq; otherwise we report a degree mismatch error.


                                                         31
    If there is no degree mismatch error, then we define δ ˚ pϕ˚ pxqq “ δpxq. We note that it may be more
natural to check degrees during the construction of Z ˚ in Lemma 9.9 which repeatedly identify darts
and vertices: whenever we identify two vertices, we just check that they have the same degree. However,
the check for loops has to await the completion of Z ˚ when the reverse pointers have attained their final
values.
    We want pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q to be a degree respecting configuration in the sense that for all inner vertices v,
dZ ˚ pvq “ δ ˚ pvq and for all boundary vertices dZ ˚ pvq ă δ ˚ pvq. We are going to look iteratively for
vertices violating of these constraints, either fixing them, as described below, or reporting an error. The
simplest case is if there is an inner vertex v with dZ ˚ pvq ă δ ˚ pvq. If so, we report a sub-degree error.
    Suppose there is a vertex v with dZ ˚ pvq ą δ ˚ pvq. Then there must be distinct incident darts e and
f of successor distance exactly δ ˚ pvq, that is, in the incidence list of v, we get from e to f following
successor pointers δ ˚ pvq times. If so, we make a degree identification request on pe, f q. We process
this degree identification request as an input identification request in Lemma 9.9 where it triggers new
homomorphic identification requests in Z ˚ until we have a new homomorphic image. When done we
have to check again both for degree mismatches and loops, reporting an error if any of these are found.
    The final issue is if there is a boundary vertex v with δ ˚ pvq “ dZ ˚ pvq. We will fix this as follows. Let
uv and wv be the first and last dart in the incidence lists of v. We want to make v an inner vertex with
incident inner facial triangle uvw. In particular, this implies that u, v, and w must be distinct; otherwise,
we report a boundary error. We now add new boundary darts uw and wu. This completes the neighbor
cycle around v and adds the last inner triangle uvw. We have to update the successor/predecessor
pointers accordingly. First, we make uv the successor of wv and wv the predecessor of uv, so now the
list of incident darts is cyclic and v has become internal with the correct degree δpvq. We take the new
dart uw and make it the new predecessor of vw around w, and vw the successor of uw. Symmetrically,
we make its reversal wu the successor of vu and vu the predecessor uw. Finally, uw gets the predecessor
set to nil and wu gets the successor set to nil. Note that this way, u, w are still boundary vertices while
v is not. The corresponding pseudocode is presented in Algorithm A.4.8.
    We repeat applying the above cases as long as there are vertices v in Z ˚ violating the degree con-
straints. If no error is reported, then ϕ˚ is our free homomorphism respecting S, and pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q its free
homomorphic image. We will say that we successfully constructed a free homomorphism ϕ˚ with image
pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q.
    We shall argue that pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q is unique. To appreciate this, let us consider a simple example. Suppose
we have a boundary face uvw. More precisely, uv and wv is the first and last dart around v, vw and
uv is the first and last dart around v, and wu and vu is the first and last dart around u. Suppose,
moreover, that each of the vertices x P tu, v, wu has dZ ˚ pxq “ δ ˚ pxq. What will happen is that uvw
becomes an inner triangle where for each x P tu, v, wu, what was the first incident dart becomes successor
of what was the last incident dart. To get to this state will, however, take multiple iterations that can
happen in different ways. First, pick a vertex, say v, and make it internal by adding a new parallel
boundary edge between u and w. Now, dZ ˚ puq “ δ ˚ puq ` 1 and dZ ˚ pwq “ δ ˚ pwq ` 1. We can now do
a degree identification of the two parallel boundary darts incident to w. The resulting homomorphic
identifications will also identify the two parallel boundary darts incident to u, and now uvw is the claimed
inner triangle.
    The pseudocode for this is found in Algorithm A.4.3. Note that Algorithm A.4.3 is designed for the
more general case where we instead of specifying a single degree specify a range of possible degrees.
However, by treating a single value δpvq as the range rδpvq, δpvqs and calling the function with this input,
we obtain the same procedure described in this section.
Lemma 9.10. Suppose that we are given a pseudo-configuration pZ, δq and a set S of dart identification
requests. Suppose there is a homomorphism ϕ1 from pZ, δq to a pseudo-configuration pZ 1 , δ 1 q such that ϕ1
respects S. Then we can successfully construct a free homomorphism ϕ˚ with image pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q respecting S.
Up to an isomorphism, the image pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q is unique, independent of ϕ1 and pZ 1 , δ 1 q, and it is guaranteed
that there exists a homomorphism ϕ˚1 from pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q to pZ 1 , δ 1 q so that ϕ˚1 ˝ ϕ˚ “ ϕ1 .
Proof. The first part of our construction is to ignore the degrees and just apply Lemma 9.9 to Z and Z 1 .
This yield a homomorphism ϕ˚ with image Z ˚ respecting S and with a homomorphism ϕ˚1 from Z ˚ to
Z 1 such that ϕ˚1 ˝ ϕ˚ “ ϕ1 .
    Now that S is respected, the rest of the construction aims to manipulate Z ˚ to become a degree-
respecting configuration pZ ˚˚ , δ ˚˚ q (which will play the role of pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q in the statement of Lemma 9.10),

                                                       32
or produce an error if this is not possible. However, here pZ 1 , δ 1 q serves as a witness that the construction
is possible, hence that an error should not occur.
     As we do the modifications to Z ˚ we will maintain that Z ˚ is a pseudo-triangulation with homomor-
phisms ϕ˚ from Z to Z ˚ and ϕ˚1 from Z ˚ to Z 1 such that ϕ˚1 ˝ ϕ˚ “ ϕ1 .
     Assuming the above homomorphisms, we claim that Z ˚ cannot have a loop; for since Z 1 is in a
configuration, it has no loops, but then Z ˚ cannot have a loop since ϕ˚1 would map such a loop in Z 1 .
     Likewise we argue that there cannot be a degree mismatch; for consider any two vertices u and v
with ϕ˚ puq “ ϕ˚ pvq. Then we must have ϕ1 puq “ ϕ˚1 pϕ˚ puqq “ ϕ˚1 pϕ˚ pvqq “ ϕ1 pvq, and the definition of
homomorphisms between configurations imply that δpuq “ δ 1 pϕ1 puqq “ δ 1 pϕ1 pvqq “ δpvq.
     Let us now consider an inner vertex v in Z ˚ . It has a cyclic list u1 v, . . . , ud v of darts around v with
d “ dZ ˚ pvq. Because ϕ˚1 is a homomorphism, ϕ˚1 pu1 vq, . . . , ϕ˚1 pud vq must be a cyclic walk around ϕ˚1 pvq
following successors in Z 1 . Therefore ϕ˚1 pvq is an inner vertex in Z 1 with dZ 1 pϕ˚1 pvqq ď dZ ˚ pvq. But
since Z 1 is a configuration, we have dZ 1 pϕ˚1 pvqq “ δ 1 pϕ˚1 pvqq “ δ ˚ pvq. Thus, we have a contradiction if
we report an error saying dZ ˚ pvq ă δ ˚ pvq.
     Now let v be any vertex in Z ˚ with dZ ˚ pvq ą δ ˚ pvq. Let uv and wv be the incident darts from our
construction that are exactly δ ˚ pvq apart in the successor order around v. Then in Z 1 we must get from
ϕ˚1 puvq to ϕ˚1 pwvq following successors δ ˚ pvq times around ϕ˚1 pvq. However, δ ˚ pvq “ δ 1 pϕ˚1 pvqq, and
since Z 1 is a configuration, this implies ϕ˚1 puvq “ ϕ˚1 pwvq.
     We know that ϕ˚1 puvq “ ϕ˚1 pwvq, so as in our construction, we can apply Lemma 9.9 with Z “ Z ˚
and ϕ1 “ ϕ˚1 and an input identification request puv, wvq. The result is a homomorphic image Z ˚˚ with
                                                    1
homomorphisms ϕ˚˚ from Z ˚ to Z ˚˚ and ϕ˚˚ from Z ˚˚ to Z 1 . We can now let Z ˚˚ play the role of
Z ˚ , with ϕ˚˚ ˝ ϕ˚ the new ϕ˚ and ϕ˚˚1 the new ϕ˚1 .
     Finally, we have the last case in our construction with a boundary vertex v of Z ˚ where uv and wv
are the first and the last in the successor list of darts with head v and the length of the list is exactly
δ ˚ pvq. The image in Z 1 with the homomorphy ϕ˚1 has the same successor sequence from ϕ˚1 puvq to
ϕ˚1 pwvq of length δ ˚ pvq but since Z 1 is a configuration and δ 1 pϕ˚1 pvqq “ δ 1 pvq, we conclude that the list
is cyclic in Z 1 with ϕ˚1 puvq the successor of ϕ˚1 pwvq.
     Since Z 1 is loop free, we know it has an inner facial triangle with distinct vertices u1 “ ϕ˚1 puq,
v “ ϕ˚1 pvq, and w1 “ ϕ˚1 pwq including an edge (consisting of two darts) between u1 and w1 . This implies
  1

that u, v, and w are distinct, so we have a contradiction if we report a boundary error because they are
not distinct.
     In our construction, we added the boundary dart uw with reversal wu to Z ˚ , assigning all the relevant
predecessors and successors so that the homomorphism ϕ˚1 can be extended to map uw and wu to the
darts in u1 w1 and w1 u1 . The homomorism ϕ˚ from Z to Z ˚ is not affected by this addition, for in the
image of Z, we only changed boundary nil-pointers from uv and wv.
     We have thus proved that all our modifications of Z ˚ maintain the homomorphisms ϕ˚ and ϕ˚1 .
It is also clear that if we terminate, it is because we respect the degrees in δ ˚ so that pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q is a
pseudo-configuration.
     The next question is whether we are sure to terminate. First consider the case where we add a new
boundary dart uw with reversal wu around v that thereby becomes inner. The homomorphic image of
an inner vertex cannot be a boundary vertex, so each vertex can only turn from boundary to inner once.
The number of boundary darts added is therefore at most twice the number of boundary vertices in Z.
All other operations are dart identifications, each of which reduces the number of dart identifiers in Z ˚ .
The total number of dart identifications is therefore bounded by twice the number of boundary vertices
plus the number of dart identifiers in Z.
     Finally, we want to argue that the outcome pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q is unique up to an isomorphism. Suppose we
have obtained two outcomes pZ1˚ , δ1˚ q and pZ2˚ , δ2˚ q. We can then let pZ2˚ , δ2˚ q play the role of pZ 1 , δ 1 q for
pZ1˚ , δ1˚ q and vice versa. We now know that ϕ˚1        ˚       ˚
                                                   1 ˝ ϕ1 “ ϕ2 , so any vertex from Z identified by ϕ1 must
                                                                                                             ˚

also be identified by ϕ˚2 , and vice versa. We also note from our construction that V pZi˚ q “ ϕ˚i pV pZqq
(this does not hold for the darts, as we may add darts). We conclude that |V pZ1˚ q| “ |V pZ2˚ q|. This
further implies that ϕ˚1                          ˚          ˚                ˚1                       ˚
                         1 must be 1-1 from V pZ1 q to V pZ2 q, but then ϕ1 is also 1-1 from EpZ1 q to EpZ2 q,

implying |EpZ1˚ q| ď |EpZ2˚ q|. A symmetric argument implies |EpZ2˚ q| ď |EpZ1˚ q|, so |EpZ1˚ q| “ |EpZ2˚ q|,
and therefore ϕ˚1  1 is a bijective homomorphism, which is equivalent to saying it is an isomorphism.

    Often, it is important that we can handle dart identification requests in batches or sequentially, one
at a time. To this end, we can use the construction of Lemma 9.10 to prove:

                                                                    , δ12 q respecting S1 YS2 , and therefore
  ˚     ˚                       ˚ ˚
pZ12 , δ12 q is isomorphic to pZ , δ q.

9.5     Degree ranges
For our discharging rules, it is important that we can specify, for boundary vertices v, not just a degree
δpvq but a range of degrees rδ ´ pvq, δ ` pvqs. Indeed we may have δ ` pvq “ 8 implying that v can embed
to a vertex of arbitrarily high degree. We shall use pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q to denote such a configuration with a
range of degrees. For an inner vertex v, we have only one degree option dZ pvq “ δ ´ pvq “ δ ` pvq. For
a boundary vertex v, we need δ ´ pvq ą dZ pvq. We can think of pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q as representing the set of
configurations pZ, δq such that δpvq P rδ ´ pvq, δ ` pvqs for all vertices v in Z.
    We will now describe how to handle a set S of dart identification requests for a configura-
tion pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q with degree ranges. The desired output is set of configurations with degree ranges
pZ1˚ , δ1˚´ , δ1˚` q, . . . , pZk˚ , δk˚´ , δk˚` q satisfying that pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q belongs to some pZi˚ , δi˚´ , δi˚` q, 1 ď i ď k, if
and only if there is a pZ, δq P pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q such that pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q is the free homomorphic image of pZ, δq
respecting S as described in Lemma 9.10. If there is no such pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q, then the construction is given up.
    We shall basically mimic our previous algorithm to construct such a homomorphic image for config-
uration with specified individual degrees (not ranges). The first step is again to apply Lemma 9.9. This
yields the pseudo-triangulation Z ˚ and a homomorphism ϕ˚ from Z to Z ˚ respecting S. Again we check
for loops, giving up if any is found. For the degree ranges, for each v ˚ in Z ˚ , we let the degree range
rδ ˚´ pv ˚ q, δ ˚´ pv ˚ qs be the intersection of the degree ranges rδ ´ pvq, δ ` pvqs of all v with ϕ˚ pvq “ v ˚ . If the
intersection is empty, we give up. This corresponds to the degree mismatch when we only had a single
specified degree for each vertex. The corresponding pseudocode is presented in Algorithm A.4.1.
    We now have a pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q that respects S, but it is typically not a configuration yet, so we
need to modify it as we did in the specified single degree case. However, many of the modifications
we perform on a vertex v depend on its specified degree, and we may therefore have to subdivide the
interval rδ ˚´ pvq, δ ˚` s. More precisely, first we create a copy pZ ˚1 , δ ˚1´ , δ ˚1` q of pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q. Next,
for some d P rδ ˚´ pvq, δ ˚` pvqq, we set δ ˚` pvq “ d and δ ˚1´ pvq “ d ` 1. In combination, pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q
and pZ ˚1 , δ ˚1´ , δ ˚1` q cover exactly the same single degree configurations as before, and we can now
continue the construction with a branch for each of them. However, we want to minimize the number of
subdivisions so that we do not end up with more configurations pZi˚ , δi˚´ , δi˚` q than necessary.
    As a first simple case, recall from the specified single degree case that we gave up with a sub-degree
error if there was an inner vertex v with dZ ˚ pvq ă δ ˚ pvq. With degree ranges, we can give up with the
same error if dZ ˚ pvq ă δ ˚´ pvq. As we modify our configuration, we will always check for such errors, so
below we assume for all inner v that dZ ˚ pvq ě δ ˚´ pvq. The corresponding pseudocode is presented in
Algorithm A.4.5.
    Since we are trying to avoid subdividing ranges, we prioritize looking for a vertex v with a single
specified degree δ ˚ pvq “ δ ˚´ pvq “ δ ˚` pvq with degree issues, as described below.
    One degree issue is if a vertex v has a single specified degree dZ ˚ pvq ą δ ˚ pvq, then as in the case
without degree ranges, we identify two incident darts e and f of successor distance exactly δ ˚ pvq. This
identification triggers a new cascade of homomorphic identification requests in Z ˚ until we have a new
homomorphic image. The process involves vertex identifications, and every time we identify two vertices
u and v, the resulting range is the intersection of the ranges of u and v, and we give up if this intersection
is empty. At the end, we also check for loops. The corresponding pseudocode is presented in the if clause
of Algorithm A.4.7.
    Another degree issue is if a boundary vertex v has a single specified degree δ ˚ pvq “ dZ ˚ pvq. If uv and
wv are the incident boundary arcs, then this is the case where we added a new boundary edge between
u and w, turning v into an inner vertex. The corresponding pseudocode is presented in the else-if clause
of Algorithm A.4.7.


                                                                34
     If no vertex with a single specified degree has degree-issue, we look for a vertex v with δ ˚´ pvq ă δ ˚` pvq
and δ ˚´ pvq ď dZ ˚ pvq We subdivide this interval into a single degree δ ˚ pvq “ δ ˚´ pvq and the remainder
rδ ˚´ pvq ` 1, δ ˚` pvqs. We continue with a branch for each of the resulting two configurations. The
corresponding pseudocode is presented in Algorithm A.4.9. With each branch we return to see if some
of the previous cases apply before we do any additional branching.
     Note that when none of the cases apply, then for each inner vertex v, we have a single specified degree
δ ˚ pvq “ δ ˚´ pvq “ δ ˚` pvq “ dZ ˚ pvq and for each other vertex v, we have δ ˚´ pvq ą dZ ˚ pvq, so indeed this
is a pseudo-configuration with specified degree ranges. The overall algorithm for resolving degree-issues
is presented in Algorithm A.4.4.
     The above process terminates (in exponential time, but here, for computer checks, we are not con-
cerned about efficiency) even if some vertices v have δ ˚` pvq “ 8. To see this, first note that when
we subdivide a range, the branches correspond to disjoint degree combinations. Also, when we branch,
we single out a specified single degree δ ˚ pvq ď dZ ˚ pvq. Thus it suffices to bound the maximal possible
degree. It is easy to see that the sum of boundary degrees can only decrease, and for inner vertices, the
degree can only decrease. Thus we can never get a degree that is bigger than the sum of the boundary
degrees or the maximal in-degree in the input pseudo-triangulation Z.
     Since we are just mimicking the single degree case, it should be clear that the branching leads to the
desired set of configurations. Thus we have
Lemma 9.12. Assume that we are given a pseudo-configuration pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q with degree ranges
and a set S of dart identification requests. We will construct configurations with degree ranges
pZ1˚ , δ1˚´ , δ1˚` q, . . . , pZk˚ , δk˚´ , δk˚` q with homomorphisms ϕ˚i from Z to Zi˚ such that pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q belongs
to some pZi˚ , δi˚´ , δi˚` q, 1 ď i ď k, if and only if there is a pZ, δq P pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q such that pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q is the
free homomorphic image by ϕ˚i of pZ, δq respecting S (as described in Lemma 9.10). If there is no such
pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q, then the construction will tell that there are no solutions.
    The pseudocode for the algorithm behind Lemma 9.12 is found in Algorithm A.4.3.
    While         this          is     not      important   for    correctness, we      note    the     representation
pZ1˚ , δ1˚´ , δ1˚` q, . . . , pZk˚ , δk˚´ , δk˚` q that we construct for Lemma 9.12, is minimal in the following
sense. For any pZ, δq P pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q with a homomorphic image respecting S, there is exactly one
homomorphism ϕ˚i that is valid in the sense that for every v P Z, if v ˚ “ ϕ˚i pvq is internal to the
image Zi˚ , then dZ ˚ pv ˚ q “ δpvq, and if v ˚ is on the boundary of Zi˚ , then dZ ˚ pv ˚ q ă δpvq. In fact,
                              i                                                              i
pZi˚ , δi˚´ , δi˚` q is exactly the union of the images of all pZ, δq P pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q for which ϕ˚i is valid.

9.5.1    Free combination of discharging rules with degree ranges
We want to ask if a given set of discharging rules R1 , . . . , Rℓ can possibly be combined to send charge
over an oriented edge or dart e “ st in some triangulation G.
    Recall that a rule Ri consists of a configuration with degree ranges pZi , δi´ , δi` q, a charge ri , and an
oriented edge si ti . For the purpose of combining rules, we will just view si ti as a dart ei with tailpei q “ si
and headpei q “ ti . Rule Ri applies to e “ st in G if and only if there is a homomorphism ϕ1i from Zi to
G such that ϕ1i pei q “ e and such that for each vi P Zi , dG pϕ1 pvi qq P rδ ´ pvi q, δ ` pvi qs. If rules R1 , . . . , Rℓ
                                            řℓ
all apply to e, then they send charge i“1 ri over e.
    To see if the rules R1 , . . . , Rℓ apply, we can equivalently look at the disjoint union

                               pZ \ , δ \´ , δ \` q “ pZ1 , δ1´ , δ1` q \ ¨ ¨ ¨ \ pZℓ , δℓ´ , δℓ` q.

We then ask if it contains a pseudo-configuration pZ, δq P pZ \ , δ \´ , δ \` q with a homomorphism ϕ1 to G
mapping each ei to e.
  We now apply Lemma 9.12 to pZ \ , δ \´ , δ \` q with identification requests

                                          S “ tpei , ei`1 q | i “ 1, . . . , ℓ ´ 1u.

Since ϕ1 respects S, the construction will be successful, resulting in free homomorphic images
pZ1˚ , δ1˚´ , δ1˚` q, . . . , pZk˚ , δk˚´ , δk˚` q, one of which contains the homomorphic image pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q of pZ, δq.
Note that Lemma 9.12 also provides the free homomorphisms ϕ˚i from Z to Zi˚ .

Each of these Ri˚ is called a free combination of R1 , . . . , Rℓ . If the above free combination of R1 , . . . , Rℓ
fails in the sense of producing an empty set of combined discharging rules, then we know that these rules
cannot be combined to send charge over any oriented edge st in any triangulation G. The converse is not
true, for we only know that each Zi˚ is a pseudo-triangulation. In principle, it may not even be planar.
     Instead of combining many discharging rules directly as suggested above, we will add discharging
rules to the combination, one at a time, as justified by Lemma 9.11. For example, suppose that we
have computed the combined discharging rules R1˚ . . . , Rk˚ for R1 . . . , Rℓ . To add a new rule Rℓ`1 to the
combinations, for i “ 1, ¨ ¨ ¨ , k, we compute the set of combined discharging rules for the two rules Ri˚
and Rℓ`1 , and then we take the union of all these sets.
     Returning to the problem of computing the maximal discharge over any edge, let R1 , . . . , Rs be the
complete set of discharging rules from Figure 6. For i “ 0, . . . , s, we want to construct Ri consisting
of all the free combinations of all the subsets of rules from R1 , . . . , Ri . At the end, we will return the
maximal charge of any rule in R “ Rs .
     We define a “neutral” discharging rule R0 “ ppZ0 , δ0´ , δ0` q, e0 , 0q, where Z0 has two vertices s0 and
t0 and two darts; namely e0 “ s0 t0 and its reverse t0 s0 . Moreover, δ0´ ps0 q “ δ0´ pt0 q “ 1 and δ0` ps0 q “
δ0` pt0 q “ 8. Note that the free combination of R0 and any rule Ri is a set consisting of a rule isomorphic
to Ri .

Lemma 9.14. For every free combination
                                              R˚ “ ppZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, e˚ , r˚ q
of our discharging rules from Figure 6, for every vertex v ˚ P Z ˚ , the degree range is one of the following
three types:
     • a single degree, that is, δ ˚´ pv ˚ q “ δ ˚` pv ˚ q,
     • the range r5, 6s, or
     • an infinite range with δ ˚´ pv ˚ q ă δ ˚` pv ˚ q “ 8.
Proof. The statement is true for all the original rules from Figure 6, and is also trivially closed under the
intersection of intervals (when different vertices are identified). The only other way we get new intervals
is by subdividing an interval, splitting off the lowest degree, but we still remain within the three types
above.


                                                               36
10      Reducible configurations in pseudo-configurations
We shall often need to check if a reducible configuration pZ, δq embeds into a given pseudo-configuration
pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q.

Cut-vertices. So far, we have only defined homomorphisms for configurations without cut-vertices.
However, a configuration pZ, δq may have a cut-vertex v (in principle, there could be more cut-vertices,
but we never have more than one in our reducible configurations in D). By (Z3) such a cut-vertex
should have exactly two outer neighbors, that is, δpvq “ dZ pvq ` 2. By (Z5), when we embed pZ, δq
into a triangulation, then these two outer neighbors should be non-consecutive around v as illustrated
in Figure 1.
    A different way of describing the requirements (Z3) and (Z5) is that we consider extending Z with
one of the outer neighbors in one of the two possible positions between the two blocks of Z incident
to v. The result Z u has no cut-vertex, and now any embedding of Z u into a triangulation includes an
embedding of Z satisfying (Z3) and (Z5).
    Using degree ranges, we extend δ to cover u. We have no special degree restrictions on u so we set
δ u´ puq “ dZ u puq ` 1 and δ u` puq “ 8. All other vertices v P Z have δ u´ pvq “ δ u` pvq “ δpvq. Now
pZ u , δ u´ , δ u` q is configuration without cut-vertices, so pZ u , δ u´ , δ u` q is also a pseudo-configuration.
Recall that we have two possible placements of the outer neighbor, hence two corresponding extensions
pZ u1 , δ u1 ´ , δ u1 ` q and pZ u2 , δ u2 ´ , δ u2 ` q.
    We now define that ϕ˚ is a homomorphism from the configuration pZ, δq with cut-vertex v
into a pseudo-configuration pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q if and only if ϕ˚ can be extended to a homomorphism from
pZ u1 , δ u1 ´ , δ u1 ` q or pZ u2 , δ u2 ´ , δ u2 ` q into pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q. The reason that we have to consider both of these
extensions is that the original cut-vertex v could map to a boundary vertex of pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q, and then it
would only be one of the outer neighbors of v that would map into pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q. We note that this definition
easily extends to cases with multiple cut-vertices, but with c cut-vertices, we would have to consider 2c
different extensions.
    Having defined homomorphisms from configurations pZ, δq, it is easily seen that Lemma 9.2 holds
for this case as well (nothing in the proof rules out cut-vertices except that homomorphisms were not
defined with cut-vertices). Thus, allowing for cut-vertices, we have
Lemma 10.1. Let pZ, δq be a configuration of diameter at most 4. If ϕ is a homomorphism from pZ, δq to
G, and G has no separating 3- or 4-cycle whose vertices are in ϕpZq, then ϕ is 1-1 (so ϕ is an embedding
showing that pZ, δq is contained in G).
     Often the question is if there is any reducible configuration pZ, δq P D that has a homomorphism ϕ˚
into pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q. For uniformity of code, it is convenient to convert all pZ, δq P D to configurations with
degree ranges pZ 1 , δ 1´ , δ 1` q. If Z has no cut-vertex, we just use pZ 1 , δ 1´ , δ 1` q “ pZ, δ, δq. If Z has a
cut-vertex, we check both the extended configurations pZ u1 , δ u1 ´ , δ u1 ` q and pZ u2 , δ u2 ´ , δ u2 ` q described
above. When we have a configuration with degree pZ 1 , δ 1´ , δ 1` q, we need to check that the homomorphism
ϕ˚ satisfies δ 1´ pvq ď δ ˚ pϕ˚ pvqq ď δ 1` pvq for all v P Z 1 .
     To facilitate the checking for homomorphisms, it is convenient to select a special dart f “ xy for
each reducible configuration pZ, δq. We select it to maximize pδpyq, δpxqq lexicographically. When we
have our reducible configuration with degree ranges pZ 1 , δ 1´ , δ 1` q, we have δ 1´ pxq “ δ 1` pxq “ δpxq and
δ 1´ pyq “ δ 1` pyq “ δpyq. Since Z 1 has no cut-vertex, a homomorphism ϕ˚ from Z 1 to Z ˚ is uniquely
determined by the image f ˚ of f in Z ˚ . The rest of ϕ˚ is then determined by the homomorphic
requirements. Constructing ϕ˚ from ϕ˚ pf q “ f ˚ fails if it cannot map all of Z 1 into Z ˚ . Assuming
that ϕ˚ was constructed successfully into Z ˚ , we have to check that δ 1´ pvq ď δ ˚ pϕ˚ pvqq ď δ 1` pvq for all
v P Z 1.
     Note that we only have to check for f ˚ P Z ˚ such that δ ˚ pheadpf ˚ qq “ δptq and δ ˚ ptailpf ˚ qq “ δpsq.
This restriction on f ˚ was quite important for speed.

Center vertices and centered reducible configurations. The target pseudo-configuration pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q
may have a distinguished center vertex c˚ P Z ˚ . Recall from Lemma 3.2 (D1) that our reducible
configurations pZ, δq in D have at most one vertex of degree above 8. If Z has such a high degree vertex
c, δpcq ą 8, then we want the homomorphism ϕ˚ from pZ, δq to pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q to map c to c˚ . To emphasize
this property, we say that the homomorphism ϕ˚ and the image of pZ, δq are centered.


                                                            37
    Note that the special edge f “ st from pZ, δq was chosen above to maximize δptq, so t is the high
degree vertex of Z if any. If so, we should only consider mapping f to darts f ˚ with headpf ˚ q “ c˚ .
    The centering is important when we have a homomorpism ϕ from a reducible configuration pZ, δq to
an extended degree-bounded ball B   s 8 pvq, for if the image is centered in B
                                                                             s 8 pvq, then it must be contained
                                     2                                         2
                       8
in the non-extended B2 pvq which is what we normally require.
    The pseudocode to check if there is homomorphism ϕ˚ from a reducible configuration pZ, δq to a
centered pseudo-configuration pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q is described in Algorithm A.6.6.

10.1      Configuration with degree ranges blocked by reducible configurations
We say that our set of reducible configurations D blocks a configuration with degree ranges pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q
and center c˚ if every pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q P pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q contains the homomorphic image of some reducible
configuration from D. Unfortunately there could be infinitely many pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q P pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q because
degree ranges could be infinite if δ ˚´ pv ˚ q ă δ ˚` pv ˚ q “ 8 for some v ˚ P Z ˚ . However, we claim that for
each v ˚ P Z ˚ , we only need to consider a bounded range, as described in the lemma below.
Lemma 10.2. Consider a configuration pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q with degree ranges and center c˚ . Define δ ˚˘
such that δ ˚˘ pc˚ q “ δ ˚´ pc˚ q if δ ˚` pc˚ q ď 12; otherwise δ ˚˘ pc˚ q “ δ ˚` pc˚ q. For vertices v ˚ ‰ c˚ ,
δ ˚˘ pv ˚ q “ δ ˚´ pv ˚ q if δ ˚` pv ˚ q ď 8; otherwise δ ˚˘ pv ˚ q “ δ ˚` pv ˚ q. Then pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q is blocked by D
if pZ ˚ , δ ˚˘ , δ ˚` q is blocked by D.
Proof. Suppose that pZ ˚ , δ ˚˘ , δ ˚` q is blocked by D, and consider any pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q P pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q. Define
δ ˚1 such that δ ˚1 pvq “ δ ˚ pvq if δ ˚ pvq ě δ ˚˘ ; otherwise, δ ˚1 pvq “ δ ˚˘ pvq. Then pZ ˚ , δ ˚1 q P pZ ˚ , δ ˚˘ , δ ˚` q.
Therefore, by assumption, we have a homomorphism ϕ˚ from some pZ, δq P D to pZ ˚ , δ ˚1 q. However, if
for any v ˚ P Z ˚ , we have δ ˚1 pv ˚ q ą δ ˚ , then δ ˚1 pv ˚ q ą 12 if v ˚ “ c˚ ; otherwise δ ˚1 pv ˚ q ą 8. In either
case, we conclude that v ˚ is not the image of any v P Z. This is clear in the case where v ˚ ‰ c˚ , and
for the center, it follows because reducible configurations from D have no vertices of degree above 12
(c.f. Lemma 3.2 (D1)). We conclude that ϕ˚ is also a homomorphism from pZ, δq to pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q. The same
consideration holds for the center c˚ .
    The pseudocode to check if a configuration with degree ranges is blocked by D is described in Algo-
rithm A.7.1.

10.2      The maximal charge over an edge without reducible configurations

Checking if a combined discharging rule is blocked. Recall from Lemma 9.14 that for any free
combination ppZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, e˚ , r˚ q of our concrete discharging rules R from Figure 6, for every vertex
v ˚ P Z ˚ , the degree range is one of the following three types:
   • a single degree, that is, δ ˚´ pv ˚ q “ δ ˚` pv ˚ q,
   • the range r5, 6s, or
   • an infinite range with δ ˚´ pv ˚ q ă δ ˚` pv ˚ q “ 8.
Recall from Lemma 10.2 that to check if pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q is blocked, it suffices to check if pZ ˚ , δ ˚˘ , δ ˚` qqis
blocked. In the above case with δ ˚` “ 8, we get δ ˚˘ “ δ ˚` pv ˚ q. It follows that the maximal de-
gree range in pZ ˚ , δ ˚˘ , δ ˚` qq is 2. It is thus a finite problem to check if a combined discharging rule
ppZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, e˚ , r˚ q is blocked by D.

Generating all non-blocked freely combined discharging rules. Finally, we note that if the
discharging rule R˚ “ ppZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, e˚ , r˚ q is blocked by D, then so is any free combination of R˚ and
other discharging rules. Instead of first generating the complete set of discharging rules R˚ from Lemma
9.13, and then dropping those that are blocked by D, it is more efficient to only generate those that are
not blocked as follows.
    Let R1 , . . . , Rs be all discharging rules, and let R0 be the neutral discharging rule.
    For each i “ 0, . . . , s, we want to construct R˚´D   i    consisting of all free combinations not blocked by

11      Free cartwheels
We will now make a generic description of what we call free cartwheels and free extended degree-bounded
cartwheels. They are basically like the cartwheels in Figure 7 where no vertices have been identified.
These are configurations that will be used in our computer checks to model the cartwheels B2 pvq and
the extended degree-bounded cartwheels B      s 8 pvq in any near-triangulation G, assuming that there are no
                                                 2
vertices of degree less than 5 (lower degree vertices are themselves reducible configurations by Lemma
2.1).
    We start from a single center vertex v with some degree δpvq ě 5.
    Next, we do the free completion to get the wheel around v. It is the near-triangulation W pvq consisting
of v in the center with its neighbors v1 , . . . , vδpvq around the boundary in this clockwise order. To get a
configuration, we need to select degrees δpvi q for the neighbors.
    Next, we do the free completion again, to construct a generic free cartwheel Cpvq around v, which
is also a near-triangulation. We start from the above wheel      Ť W pvq. Next, for each neighbor vi , we pick
a degree δpvi q ě 5. The free cartwheel Cpvq is the union uPV pW pvqq W puq of the wheels around these
vertices. This includes degrees for all the new vertices outside W pvq. The wheels only intersect minimally
as indicated in Figure 7. In particular, if two vertices on the neighboring cycle of v have distance (on that
cycle) more than 2, their wheels intersect only in v. If their distance is precisely 2, their wheels intersect


                                                            39
in precisely an edge including its ends. If their distance is 1, their wheels intersect in a diamond. The
two latter cases are true because δpvq ě 5.
    For a free extended degree-bounded cartwheel C  s 8 pvq, we use the same generation as above, but we only
                                                               s 8 pvq “ W pvqY Ť
add the wheel around a vi if its degree is at most 8, that is, C                 uPtv1 ,...,vδpvq u,δpvi qď8 W pvi q Ď
Cpvq. Clearly,
Fact 11.1. Free cartwheels and free extended degree-bounded cartwheels are configurations and they have
no separating 3- or 4-cycles.

dC puq ă δpuq.

then it suffices to prove that all free extended degree-bounded cartwheels C     s 8 pvq with non-center degrees

so all non-center vertices have degrees between 5 and 9. Moreover, to prove Lemma 8.1, we only need
to consider centers with degrees between 7 and 11. For brevity, we shall refer to these as free cartwheels
with limited degrees.
    Note that there are only finitely many free cartwheels with limited degrees, but still there are far
too many for a computer to consider. For example, if the center has degree 11 and degrees in the first
neighborhood are 8, we have 44 vertices in the second neighborhood, each of which having 5 degrees to
chose from, leaving us 544 ą 2100 options which is way too many for any computer today.

11.2      Enumerating bad cartwheels with tail ranges
A bad cartwheel is a free cartwheel with limited degrees that has no centered reducible configuration in D
and where the final center charge is non-negative. Any counterexample to Lemma 8.1 would correspond
to a bad cartwheel. Other bad cartwheels are important to the proof of Lemma 8.3, and we would like
the computer to enumerate all bad cartwheels9 .
    Again, we have an issue with the numbers being too large. To handle this, we will use degree ranges.
The ranges will only be used for vertices w in the second neighborhood of the center v, and we only
use degree ranges of the form rd, 9s for some d P t5, 6, 7, 8u. We call them tail ranges. These can be
                                       ´ `
represented as configurations pZC , δC  , δC q with degree ranges. We refer to them as free cartwheels with
                                                                        ´ `
limited degrees and tail ranges, noting that every pZC , δC q P pZC , δC , δC q is a free cartwheel with fixed
limited degrees.
    The output of our enumeration will not be individual bad cartwheels, but a set of free cartwheels
with limited degrees and tail ranges that is guaranteed to include all bad cartwheels. If, for example,
none of these have a center degree below 9, then the first case of Lemma 8.1 follows.
    Our algorithm iteratively refines cartwheels with tail ranges, considering different fixings of the degrees
within the ranges. In general, we want to refine as little as possible to avoid generating too many
combinations. What drives the refinements is the need to identify reducible configurations or determine
which discharging rules could apply. We will prune, removing configurations that cannot contain bad
cartwheels if either we conclude that the final charge must be negative or if they are blocked by reducible
configurations in D.
    We note here that what is special about only using tail ranges is that when we want to check if
       ´ `
pZC , δC , δC q is blocked by D, then by Lemma 10.2, we can replace each tail range by a single degree 9,
and then all degrees are fixed when we look for reducible configurations. While this keeps the complexity
down, we also note that a non-center vertex of degree 9 is not in any reducible configuration, so we
cannot have too many of them.

11.2.2    Enumerating degrees of neighbors
We describe the procedure to enumerate free cartwheels with limited degrees with tail ranges that include

combinations in total. However, we only consider the assignments unique up to rotation, reducing the
number of combinations10 . We will use v1 , . . . , vd to denote neighbors of v. Fixing the degrees of vi
determines the shape of C, as described in Section 11. Recall that we only add second neighbors of v,
