                                                                44
11.2.4      Refinement
Up to this point, we have determined the set of rules applied to vi v for every neighbor vi to obtain
Cd . The free cartwheels with limited degrees and tail ranges in Cd may have second neighbors whose
degrees have not been determined yet (i.e., their degree-ranges remain a tail range rd, 9s for some d). We
construct the final output C of the algorithm from Cd by selectively refining some of these degrees, but
not all of them.
     We first outline the overall flow of the algorithm to construct the final set C from Cd . Initially, we
add every free cartwheel with limited degree and tail ranges in Cd into a queue. While the queue is
                                                                                                       ´ `
not empty, we pop one free cartwheel with limited degrees and tail ranges pZC , δC                       , δC q from the queue.
Then, we iterate over all pairs of a rule R P R and a neighbor vi . For each pair pR, vi q, we determine
                             ´ `
whether to refine pZC , δC     , δC q into two sets of pseudo-configurations Calways : where the rule R always
applies to vvi , and Cnever : where the rule R never applies to vvi . If we determine not to refine for a given
pair, we proceed to the next pair. Otherwise, we perform the refinement, and apply the three previously
described pruning methods to the free cartwheels with limited degrees and tail ranges in Calways and Cnever .
                                                                                                                              ´ `
We push the surviving free cartwheels with limited degrees and tail ranges to the queue. If pZC , δC                            , δC q
is not refined for any pair, we add it to the final set C. We repeat until the queue becomes empty. The
pseudocode corresponding to the overall algorithm for constructing C from Cd is presented in Algorithm
A.9.14.
     It remains to describe the criteria for refinement and the refinement procedure itself. The fundamental
idea is that if R always applies, then it is sure to decrease the final charge at v, increasing the chance
that we conclude that the final charge is negative. However, for the cases where R never applies, we
also want an advantage; namely, a vertex in the second neighborhood with fixed degree below 9; for this
increases the chance that a cartwheel is blocked by a reducible configuration (reducible configurations
can not use non-center vertices with degree 9). To ensure we get this advantage when R never applies,
we will use only R that ”dominantly” applies, as defined below.
                      ´ `
     Let R “ ppZR , δR  , δR q, eR , rR q P R be a rule and vi be a neighbor. We check whether R always applies
                  ´ `
to vvi in pZC , δC , δC q. If so, no refinement is performed. Next, we check whether R sometimes applies to
                                                                    ´ `                  ´ `
vvi . If so, we consider the homomorphism ϕ from pZR , δR            , δR q to pZC , δC    , δC q satisfying ϕpeR q “ vvi . We

    To see that this algorithm terminates, note that if a free cartwheel with limited degrees and tail
ranges is refined into Calways and Cnever by a pair pR, vi q, no resulting free cartwheel with limited degrees
and tail ranges in Calways or Cnever will be refined by the same pair again. This is because the application
of R to vvi is definitely resolved (i.e., it either always applies or never applies) for all free cartwheels
with limited degrees and tail ranges in Calways and Cnever . Thus, the refinement process occurs at most
|R| ¨ d times for a single free cartwheel with limited degrees and tail ranges in Cd . Therefore, the queue
eventually becomes empty, ensuring that the algorithm terminates.

11.3     Free combinations of free cartwheels
In this section, we describe the algorithms used to prove Lemma 8.3, 8.5, and 8.6. In these lemmas, we
want to combine cartwheels in order to find reducible configurations. We only need to consider cartwheels
that do not already have reducible configurations, so our starting point is the set C “ Call of cartwheels
that survived from the last section. Note that the number of free cartwheels in Call is 728 for center
degree 8, and 9366 for center degree 7.
    In the previous section, we considered degrees up to 9, but in this section, we only consider vertices
of degree up to 8. In particular, this means that we can eliminate from C any configuration pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q
which for some vertex v P Z has fixed degree δ ´ pvq “ δ ` pvq “ 9. The only ranges we have for cartwheels
in C are tail ranges rd, 9s. We could reduce those to rd, 8s, but this would not be a good idea unless d “ 8.
The point is that to see if pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q is blocked by the reducible configurations in D, by Lemma 10.2,
we can reduce a tail range to the single value 9 while we have to do all possible combinations over all
other degree ranges. The role of a tail range is simply to say that a vertex cannot be used in any of the
reducible configurations. After this pruning, the vertices in configurations C have either fixed degrees at
most 8 or tail ranges. The pseudocode corresponding to this process is presented in Algorithm A.10.1.
    For combining cartwheels, we shall use a general routine that takes a configuration pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q with a
specified dart e and look for all possible free combinations with a cartwheel pZ 1 , δ 1´ , δ 1` q P C identifying
e with some dart e1 incident with the center of pZ 1 , δ 1´ , δ 1` q. We eliminate all free combinations blocked
by a reducible configuration from D, returning only those combinations that are not blocked. The
pseudocode for this is found in A.10.2.

A vertex of degree 8. We shall now describe the algorithmic check needed to prove prove Lemma
8.3.
    Consider every cartwheel pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q P C with center v of degree 8. If v has a neighbor of degree
8, then for all such degree-8 neighbors v 1 , we consider all the above mentioned free combinations with
cartwheels from C, identifying v, v 1 with a dart to the center.
    A computer check found that all combinations were blocked, and from this, we want to conclude that
Lemma 8.3 (i) is true. However, Lemma 8.3 (i) is about cartwheels in G, not free cartwheels.
    In more detail, for Lemma 8.3 (i), we consider the situation in G where all vertices in B3 pvq have degree
at most 8 and v 1 is a degree-8 neighbor of v, v and v 1 have final charge 0. With B “ B2 pvq Y B2 pv 1 q,
the lemma claims that B has a separating 3- or 4-cycle or a reducible configuration in D. Suppose
for a contradiction that B has no separating 3- or 4-cycle or reducible configuration in D. Then, by
Lemma 11.2, B  s 8 pvq “ B2 pvq is isomorphic to a free cartwheel pZ, δq with center v. The free cartwheel
                 2
inherits from B2 pvq that v has charge 0 and no reducible configuration in D, so pZ, δq P pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q P C.
Likewise, we get a pZ 1 , δ 1 q P pZ 1 , δ 1´ , δ 1` q P C isomorphic to B2 pv 1 q. By Lemma 9.10, this implies that
we from the free combination pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q of pZ, δq and pZ 1 , δ 1 q get a homomorphism into B. But since
the free combinations were blocked by D, we know that there is a reducible configuration from D with
a homomorphism into pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q and hence further on into B where we know it is embedded because
B has no separating 3- or 4-cycles. This completes the formal proof of Lemma 8.3 (i) based on the
computer verified check in Algorithm A.10.5 that the free combinations were all blocked by reducible
configurations in D. In the rest of this section, we will just assume this understanding of the relation
between the cartwheels in G and our free cartwheels from C.



                                                        47
     The proof of Lemma 8.3 (ii) is very similar. Here, v has no neighbor of degree 8 and only one
neighbor, v 1 , of degree 7. In this case, again we look at all free combinations with cartwheels in C,
identifying vv 1 with a dart to the center, checking that they are all blocked. We checked this with a
computer in Algorithm A.10.6, completing the proof of Lemma 8.3 (ii).
     Finally, we have the case of Lemma 8.3 (iii). Here v has no neighbors of degree 8 and at least
two neighbors of degree 7. We consider any two such neighbors v 1 and v 2 minimizing the succes-
sor distance from v 1 v to v 2 v around v. In this case, we want to look at all free combinations with
pZ 1 , δ 1´ , δ 1` q, pZ 2 , δ 2´ , δ 2` q P C, identifying vv 1 with a center dart in Z 1 and vv 2 with a center dart
in Z 2 . However, recall from Lemma 9.11 that we can take the free combinations, one at a time. For
efficiency, it is better to first do the free combinations pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q of pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q with pZ 1 , δ 1´ , δ 1` q,
removing those blocked by D, and then only combine the survivors with pZ 2 , δ 2´ , δ 2` q. In the full com-
binations, we again remove everyone that is blocked by D. This routine for combining cartwheels from
C twice is presented in Algorithm A.10.3. A computer check in Algorithm A.10.7 guarantees that the
remaining configurations all contain the exceptional configuration X from Figure 13 with v in the center.
This completes the proof of Lemma 8.3 (iii).

Maximum degree 7. We shall now show the algorithmic check needed to prove Lemma 8.5 and 8.6.
In this section, we consider only degrees up to 7. In particular, all center degrees are 7. Specifically, we

δ ` pvq “ 8. The only degree-ranges we have for cartwheels in C are tail ranges rd, 9s. Similarly to the
case with degree 8, we reduce the degree-range rd, 9s to rd, 7s only if d “ 7.

     This was the last lemma for which a computer check was needed, so this completes the proof of
Theorem 6.5, our main technical result on reducible configurations.


12      Recursing deterministically with D-reducible configurations
We will now show that we can derandomize the simple randomized algorithm from Section 3.1. Recall
the situation. We have a linear number of non-touching D-reducible configurations. Using induction, we
have 4-colored all vertices outside these reducible configurations.
   We will now start doing Kempe changes on the colored vertices. If the ring R of a non-colored
configuration Z gets a 0-extendible coloring, then we extend this 4-coloring of R to Z, and then Z is
no longer among the non-colored configurations (strictly speaking, it is pZ, dG q that is the reducible
configuration).
   In general, if the coloring of a ring is i-extendible, then we may have to do i improving Kempe
changes to make it 0-extendible. We know from Lemma 3.2 that all colorings of the rings of our reducible
configurations are 25-extendible.


                                                             48
    In Section 3.1, we proved that Oplog nq random Kempe changes sufficed to get all reducible configu-
rations colored with high probability.
    We will derandomize the construction using the method of conditional expectations. The main step
is to show that we can deterministically find a Kempe change that improves the level of coloring for a
constant fraction of the rings. The key is to prove the following lemma:
Lemma 12.1. Let Z1 , . . . , Zr be some (not all) non-colored reducible configurations in G. The colorings
of their rings R1 , . . . , Rr are not yet 0-extendible.
    We can then, deterministically in linear time, find and execute a Kempe change that is improving for
at least a fraction p “ 3¨2118 of the rings, and also tell which rings got improved.
Proof. We assume that for every one of our reducible configurations D P D (c.f. Lemma 3.2), we store
the Kempe level of each coloring of its ring. This is a huge constant amount of information, but we need
it to determine if a given Kempe change will improve.
    For each of the three color combinations, that is, for i P t1, 2, 3u, we use the color pairs t0, iu and
t1, 2, 3uztiu, and we identify all the bi-colored Kempe chains. Each vertex now knows which Kempe
chain it belongs to.
    We now consider the perspective of a given ring Ri . Its coloring is only affected by the swap or
no-swap of the Kempe chains intersecting it, and there are at most |Ri | ď 18 such Kempe chains. We
now consider all combinations of swap and no-swap for each intersecting Kempe chain. We know which
vertices are involved, hence the effect on the coloring, so we can check if a Kempe change following a
given combination of intersecting swap/no-swap is improving.
    We repeat this for all three color combinations, and we know that for each ring there is at least one
color combination and a combination of intersecting swap/no-swap combinations that leads to improve-
ment, and we pick just one such combination for each ring Ri .
    For the next Kempe change, if we picked both the color combination and the swap/no-swap for each
Kempe chain uniformly at random, then the probability that the change was improving for Ri would be
at least 1{p3 ¨ 2|Ri | q ě p “ 1{p3 ¨ 218 q, and hence we would expect a fraction p of all rings to get improved.
    We can get an improvement for at least a fraction p of the rings using the method of conditional
expectation as described below.
    At the beginning, all our rings are “live”. For each of them, we know the color pair combination for
the improving Kempe change, and we pick the color pair that works for most of the rings. Only the rings
that get their color combination stay alive; the rest will “die”. We know that at least 1/3 stay alive.
    We are now going to go through each Kempe chain using the selected color pairs and decide whether
to swap the colors. A ring Ri only stays live as long as all decided swaps were as it wanted them. If
the number of undecided Kempe chains intersecting Ri is ui , and if all remaining choices were made at
random, then the probability that Ri would survive till the end is 2´ui . Thus, conditioned on decisions
made, the expected number of survivors is
                     ÿ
                              2´ui , where ui is the number of undecided chains intersecting Ri .
              Ri is still live

We can now pick any undecided Kempe chain X. We know that one of the two choices — swap or
no-swap — will not decrease the expectation, and we can determine which one it is by considering only
the live rings that intersect X. Because the active reducible configurations are non-touching, their rings
are strictly vertex-disjoint. Therefore, each vertex in the graph belongs to at most one active ring.
By maintaining a simple global array mapping each vertex to its host ring (or null), iterating over the
vertices of X allows us to identify all intersecting rings in strictly Op|X|q time. Thus, the decision for
X is done in time Op|X|q, and since the Kempe chains are disjoint, we conclude that the total time is
linear.
    Since the conditional expectation is non-decreasing as we make our choices, and since we end up with
all Kempe chains decided, we conclude that the final set of live rings is at least as big as the original
expectation.
    We will now use the above lemma to decide on 25 Kempe changes. We say a ring Ri is active if all
previous Kempe changes have been improving, and it is not yet 0-extendible. We apply Lemma 12.1 to
all active rings. We know that a fraction p gets an improving move. For the rings Ri whose coloring
becomes 0-extendible, we extend the coloring to Zi . The active rings that got an improving move but


                                                       49
are still not 0-extendible remain active. We repeatedly apply Lemma 12.1 to the active rings, finding a
new Kempe change that improves by a factor of p for the remaining active rings. After 25 repetitions,
we know that there are no active rings left, and that at least a fraction p25 of the original rings have
been extended. Conversely, this means that the number of empty rings has been reduced by at least a
factor 1 ´ p25 .
    We now restart with all the empty rings, all of which are 25-extendible11 . If we started with r reducible
configurations, then after at most log1{p1´p25 q r “ Oplog rq “ Oplog nq rounds of 25 Kempe changes, we
know that no empty rings remain, hence that we have colored the whole original configuration G.
    The recursive step, reducing the problem size by a constant factor, is thus completed in Opn log nq
time. This suffices for our Opn log nq time 4-coloring algorithm.


13     Recursing with non-touching obstructing cycles
We now assume that we are in the case of Theorem 3.3 where we get a linear number of non-touching
obstructing cycles, which we can assume are chordless. These obstructing cycles will play a role similar
to that of 0-extendible reducible configurations.
    It is now convenient to view our triangulation G as embedded in the plane. To do so, take an arbitrary
triangle and make it the outer face. Now, each obstructing cycle has the inside (interior) and outside
(exterior).
    Any family of non-touching obstructing cycles in a near-triangulation can be represented by a rooted
tree structure, whose nodes correspond to the obstructing cycles R1 , . . . , Rr and whose root R0 corre-
sponds to the outer face cycle. In the tree, Rj is a descendant of Ri if Rj is inside Ri . An example is
shown in Figure 16.

                                        R0
                                                                                    R0
                                         R2
                                                         R3
                       R1                                                 R1   R2    R3   R4


                                                       R4

 Figure 16: A family of non-touching obstructing cycles can be represented by a rooted tree structure.

    We will now find all obstructing cycles that have no other obstructing cycle from our family in their
interior. They correspond to the leaves of the mentioned rooted tree. The idea is to recurse on their
interiors, and replace the interiors with a constant size near-triangulation. If H was the original near-
triangulation with outer face R and its constant-size replacement is T , we want that any coloring of R
that extends to a coloring of T can also be extended to H. For an obstructing cycle R of length 3, this
is trivial – we let T be just the triangle R. In the algorithm, we first color the interior H of R, including
R. Next, we remove the interior and color the rest. This yields a coloring of R. The two colorings of the
three vertices in R are equivalent up to a color permutation. Using this permutation, we can combine the
exterior coloring with the coloring we found recursively for the interior. For longer obstructing cycles,
things get more complicated. For 4-cycles, there will be four possibilities for T , while for the 5-cycle,
there are 11 different cases. This is described in the next subsection.

13.1     Obstructing 4-cycles and 5-cycles
It has been known since the work of Franklin [Fra22] that obstructing cycles give a coloring reduction
procedure. Robertson, Sanders, Seymour, and Thomas [RSST96] showed how to perform such reductions
efficiently. They described things via consistent 3-edge-colorings. Since this is critical for our algorithm,
we provide a self-contained description, which also shows how we can use many non-touching obstructing
cycles so that the overall time is still near-linear.
 11 This is guaranteed by D-reducibility. C-reducibility is not enough.




                                                            50
   Let us start by observing that a cycle R “ v1 v2 v3 v4 of length 4 has four 4-colorings (up to a
permutation of colors). Two of them have v1 , v3 colored the same, two have v2 and v4 colored the same
(and the coloring using only two colors appears in both cases), and there is a coloring where all vertices
are colored differently.

Lemma 13.1. Consider a 4-colored near-triangulation H with outer facial 4-cycle R “ v1 v2 v3 v4 . By
using a single Kempe change in H, we will either:
  (i) specify a value of j P t1, 2u and produce both 4-colorings of R where vj and vj`2 have different
      colors, or
 (ii) specify a value j P t1, 2u and produce both 4-colorings of R where vj and vj`2 have the same color.

The above colorings (together with the specified j) are found in time that is linear in the size of H.
Proof. The first of the two colorings will always be the one we already have (since H is colored). For
the second one, we first consider the case where R has all four colors 1,2,3,4, that is, vi has color i for
i “ 1, 2, 3, 4. Either H has a Kempe chain containing v1 , v3 or a Kempe chain containing v2 , v4 , say
the former.12 Then the Kempe chain C with colors 2,4 containing the vertex v4 does not contain v2 .
Consequently, the Kempe change on C results in a coloring of R where v2 and v4 have the same color.
So, we have (i) with j “ 1.
     Consider the second case where R has three distinct colors. We may assume that vi has color i for
i “ 1, 2, 4 whereas v3 has color 1. If the Kempe chain with colors 1,3 containing v3 does not contain
v1 , then the Kempe change on this Kempe chain gives (i) with j “ 2. So assume that the Kempe chain
contains v1 . Then the Kempe change with colors 2,4 containing v4 will change the color of v4 but not
v2 . So, we have (ii) with j “ 1.
     In the third and final case, v1 , v2 , v3 , v4 have colors 1,2,1,2. Then an appropriate Kempe change will
change just one of the colors and give us outcome (ii).
    We can now describe our recurrence over an obstructing 4-cycle R. As in Lemma 13.1, let H be the
interior of R, including R. First, we color H recursively and then perform the Kempe change described
in the lemma in time Op|H|q. This will generate another coloring of H corresponding to case (i) or (ii).
    We now remove the interior of R. If we are in case (i), we add the edge from vj to vj`2 . This
triangulates the empty face inside R. After this reduction, we color the graph. No matter how this colors
R, we know that the coloring is extendable to H using one of the colorings we computed recursively.
    If we are in case (ii), we identify vj and vj`2 . This creates parallel edges to the other vertices, and
we only keep one edge from each parallel pair. After this reduction, we color the rest of the graph.
No matter how this colors R, we again know that the coloring can be extended to H using one of the
colorings we computed recursively.
    Let us now look at 4-colorings of a 5-cycle R “ v1 v2 v3 v4 v5 . Again, we are only interested in how the
color classes partition V pRq. Note that there are five colorings that use only three colors. The following
result is analogous to Lemma 13.1.
Lemma 13.2. Consider a 4-colored near-triangulation H with the outer facial 5-cycle R “ v1 v2 v3 v4 v5 ,
where precisely three colors appear on R. Using at most six single Kempe changes in H, we will either
  (i) specify a value j P t1, 2, 3, 4, 5u and produce all four 4-colorings of R where vj is the only vertex of
      color 1, say, or

 (ii) specify a value of j P t1, 2, 3, 4, 5u and produce all three 4-colorings of R where vj and vj`2 (index
      j ` 2 taken modulo 5) have the same color, or
(iii) produce all five 3-colorings of R.
The above colorings (together with the specified j) can be found in Op|H|q time.
 12 Because the graph is planar, we cannot have both, and it is a well-known fact that one of them must occur.




                                                         51
Proof. Assume without loss of generality that v1 , v2 , v3 , v4 , v5 are colored 1,2,3,2,3, respectively. If we
can rotate this coloring one step clockwise (that is, we get the coloring 3,1,2,3,2 up to permutation of
colors) by one Kempe change, and then again rotate one step and repeat five times, we have case (iii). So,
we may assume that the coloring 3,1,2,3,2 (of v1 , v2 , v3 , v4 , v5 , respectively) cannot be obtained by one
Kempe change. So, the Kempe chain with colors 1,2 containing v1 , v2 also contains v4 . This implies that
H has no Kempe chain with colors 3,4 containing v3 , v5 . So, one Kempe change produces the coloring
1,2,4,2,3.
    Consider again the coloring 1,2,3,2,3. If there is a Kempe chain with colors 2,4 containing v2 , v4 , then
a Kempe change changes the colors of v1 , v5 without changing the color of v3 , that is, we get 3,2,3,2,1.
In summary, we now have the colorings 1,2,3,2,3, 1,2,4,2,3, and 3,2,3,2,1. This is case (ii) with j “ 2.
So, we may assume that there is no Kempe chain with colors 2,4 containing v2 , v4 . This implies that one
Kempe change with colors 2,4 results in the coloring 1,4,3,2,3. Using this, we consider the Kempe chain
with colors 3 and 4, which contains v5 . If that does not contain v2 , v3 , then one Kempe change results
in the coloring 1,4,3,2,4, in which case we have (i) with j “ 1. On the other hand, if there is a Kempe
chain with colors 3,4 containing v2 , v3 , v5 , then the Kempe chain with colors 1,2 containing v4 can be
used to produce the coloring 1,4,3,1,3, in which case we have (ii) with j “ 3.
     Note that the 4-colorings of R in Lemma 13.2(i), respectively (ii), are precisely the 4-colorings of
the near-triangulation with R as outer cycle and with the edges vj vj`2 , vj vj`3 being added, respectively
vj , vj`2 being identified, whereas (iii) describes the colorings of R after a vertex of degree 5 has been
added.
     We can now describe our recurrence over an obstructing 5-cycle R using Lemma 13.2. We have the
plane graph H with boundary R. An interesting new aspect is that, before we recurse on H, we have
to add a new degree-5 vertex v to H outside R, whose neighbors are the vertices in R. Now we do the
recursive coloring of H with v attached, so that only three colors will be used on R. Next, we get the
colorings of H corresponding to cases (i)–(iii). We now remove the interior of R from the graph, and
the case tells us how to replace it before we color the rest of the graph, e.g., in case (iii), we add a new
degree-5 vertex v inside R whose neighbors are the vertices in R. Now we color the rest of the graph,
and no matter how this colors R, we know how to extend the coloring to H.

13.2     The recursive algorithm
We will now describe the recurrence more carefully. For accounting, instead of having an outer triangle,
it is easy to just use a single minimal degree vertex v0 as the outer vertex, since it will touch at most a
constant number of our non-touching obstructing cycles, that we can then give up. When we start, we
have an outer ring R0 that just consists of v0 .
     Generally, for the recursive call, we have some near-triangulation H with some ring R around the
outer boundary. This ring corresponds to one of our chordless obstructing cycles.
     In H, we do a search to find all outermost obstructing cycles Ri ‰ R in H.13 Note here that since
G is triangulated and R is chordless with non-empty inside, all vertices in R have neighbors inside R.
Since our obstructing cycles are non-touching, any such neighbors must be outside all the Ri in H.
     Excluding the inside of each Ri , we get what we refer to as the component of the recursive call. The
component C is found in time proportional to its size.
     We want to make a recursive coloring of H, but before doing so, we need to make some modifications.
If R is longer than 3, we place a vertex outside R whose neighbors are the vertices in R. The resulting
H 1 is a triangulation, and when we 4-color H 1 , we get a 3-coloring of R (as required for Lemma 13.2).
However, the output of our recursive call is not just this 3-coloring. Rather, the output is one of the
cases in Lemma 13.1 or 13.2, including a representation of the constantly many colorings mentioned in
the case.
     Now, for each Ri , let Hi “ IntpRi q. We make a recursive coloring of Hi . This tells us one of the
cases in Lemma 13.1 or 13.2. As described under the lemmas, depending on the case, we will make some
changes across the empty face inside Ri in the component C: in cases (i) we add the specified edges
between vertices in Ri , in cases (ii) we identify the specified vertices in Ri , and in case (iii) we place the
star in the middle of Ri . Besides these changes, we also add a star outside R to H. The resulting graph
is a modified component C 1 .
 13 These are the sons of the root R in our rooted tree mentioned before.




                                                          52
    We now color C 1 inductively (we could also say recursively, but we want to distinguish this from
the recursive structure separated by our obstructing cycles). Afterwards, we do the Kempe changes
specified in Lemma 13.1 or 13.2, producing a case including all the specified colorings of C. There are
only constantly many, so we can represent all of them in space proportional to the size of C.
    When we are done with our recursion starting from G, our recursion tree will have a component for
each recursive call, and the components are connected via our obstructing cycles. For each modified
component C 1 , we have the specified colorings. We now construct the final coloring of G in a top-down
manner. At the root, we have the outer ring R0 “ tv0 u, which we color arbitrarily. Now, in the top
down process, we have a component C with outer boundary ring R that is colored in a way we know
is consistent with one of the colorings of C that we stored, so now we can extend the coloring of R to
C using this coloring, which we know has a valid coloring of each Ri . Thus, given all the component
colorings produced in the recursive call, we can color all of G in linear time.

13.3     Runtime analysis
For our runtime analysis, let c be the number of obstructing cycles, hence the number of recursive calls.
We only recurse via obstructing cycles if c “ Ωpnq.
    As the recursion is currently described, it makes the problem bigger, not smaller; that is, the total
number of vertices in the components that we color inductively may be larger than n.
    To be more precise, in a recursive call with ring R in graph H and component C, the ring R is also in
the parent call as some ring Ri1 , so we can view the ring with its up to 5 vertices as a copy. In addition,
when we modify C to C 1 , adding a vertex outside R, we may add a vertex inside each Ri . There could
be many Ri , but we can think of the vertex inside Ri as paid for by the recursive call via Ri . In total,
this means that we get up to 7 extra vertices per inductive call.
    We can fix this by not using induction for small components. More precisely, we know that the
average modified component size is bounded by pn ` 7cq{c “ Op1q. We say that a modified component is
big if it is bigger than d “ 100pn ` 7cq{c “ Op1q. The number b of big components is at most c{100. The
modified components that are not big, we 4-color with the classic quadratic algorithm from [RSST96].
Each of them is of constant size, hence colored in constant time, so the total time spent on coloring small
modified components is only linear in the size of the graph.
    We claim that the total number of vertices in the big modified components is n ´ Ωpcq, which implies
that our induction has reduced the number of nodes by a constant factor.
    At first, this may seem trivial because we only had to pay at most 7 extra per component, but recall
that a component paid one for the vertex that the parent call had to place in its center, and the parent
of a small component could be big.
    Consider the recursion tree again, and identify all cases where we have a big component C with a ring
Ri such that the recursion of the graph Hi in Ri only uses small components. Since Ri is obstructing, if
Ri is of length 5, we know that Hi has at least two vertices inside Ri , but when we modify C to C 1 , in
case Lemma 13.2 (iii), we have to place a vertex in Ri . The gain is thus at least 1. In all other cases, we
do not add any other vertex to C 1 , and we know that we remove at least one vertex from H inside Ri .
Let x be the number of obstructing cycles in Hi , Ri included. If x “ 1, we just saw that we gain at least
1 when we remove Hi . If x ą 1, we note that all the x ´ 1 rings inside Ri are non-touching, and they
are all of size at least 3, so it follows easily that we gain at least x. Therefore, if c1 is the total number
of small components with no big descending component, then we get savings of at least c1 from the big
components.
    Now, for each big component C, we let it pay both for its own ring R, including putting a vertex
inside, but we also go to its nearest big ancestor C ` , and let it pay for the ring Ri` containing C,
including possibly putting a vertex in Ri` . The total payment is thus at most 12, adding up to 12b over
all big components.
    For all small components with descending big components, we know that the ring has size at least 3,
so if we have c2 of these small components, then we can save at least 3c2 . We have c “ c1 ` c2 ` b, and
we save c1 ` 2c2 ´ 12b. Since b ď c{100, we conclude that we save at least c{2, hence at most n ´ c{2
vertices are transferred to the induction, as desired.
    The recursive step, reducing the problem size by a constant factor, is thus completed in Opnq time,
which is more than sufficient for our Opn log nq time 4-coloring algorithm. This completes the description
of the algorithm, hence the proof of Theorem 1.1.


                                                     53
14       The overall algorithmic result
In this paper we show that we can 4-color a planar triangulation in Opn log nq time. First, applying
Corollary 3.3, in linear time, we find either (a) a linear number of pairwise non-touching induced reducible
configurations in D, or (b) a linear number of pairwise non-touching obstructing cycles.
   In both cases, we can recursively reduce the problem by a constant factor. However, in case (a), the
recursive step takes Opn log nq time, while case (b) takes Opnq time.
   We now clarify that any progress on case (a) leads to progress on the overall 4-coloring. First, we
formalize case (a) as follows.
Task 14.1 (Color Extension). Let be given a planar triangulation G of order n with a family Z of Ωpnq
pairwise non-touching induced D-reducible configurations in D.
   ŤFind a fast algorithm that selects a subfamily Z 1 Ď Z of size Ωpnq such that, for any 4-coloring of
       1
Gz Z  Ť , we can (again by the fast algorithm) perform Oplog nq Kempe changes that result in a 4-coloring
of Gz Z 1 which extends to all of G.
   Our overall constructions show:
Theorem 14.2. If we can solve the Color Extension Task 14.1 of order n in time T pnq, then we can
4-color any planar graph of order n in time T ˚ pnq ď Opn ` T pnqq ` T ˚ pn{2q.
   In this paper we do the color extension implementing each of the Oplog nq Kempe changes in Opnq
time. This leads to Opn log nq time both for color extension and 4-coloring. However, in a forthcoming
paper, we shall get down to linear time, as discussed below.


15       Further works
In a forthcoming paper we shall show that the Oplog nq Kempe changes for color extension in Task 14.1
can all be identified and implemented in Opnq time, hence that the whole 4-coloring can be implemented
in linear time. The idea is to spend Opnq time to preprocess the graph so that we can identify and simulate
each good Kempe change in Opn{ log nq amortized time. Thus we implement all Oplog nq Kempe changes
in Opnq total time, implying that we 4-color in linear time.
     We note that creating such a representation for fast identification and simulation of good Kempe
changes is a challenge of data structures. This is a very different type of research than the new un-
derstanding of reducible configurations developed in this paper, leading to a combinatorial algorithm
4-coloring any planar graph in Opn log nq time.
     In another forthcoming paper based on this work, solving a problem of Albertson [Alb81], we expect
to prove that every compact 2-dimensional surface S, there exists a natural number qS such that each
graph G embedded in S has a set X of at most qS vertices such that G ´ X is 4-colorable.

           Soc., 362(12):6633–6661, 2010.
[Str86]    Bjarne Stroustrup. The C++ Programming Language, First Edition. Addison-Wesley, 1986.
[Tai80]    Peter Guthrie Tait. Note on a theorem in geometry of position. Trans. Roy. Soc. Edinburgh,
           29:657–660, 1880.

                                                 56
A     Appendix: Pseudocodes
Our proof relies heavily on computer checks. We have already presented an algorithmic description of
these checks. In this section, we present pseudocode implementations of these algorithms along with
pointers to C++-code implementing the pseudocode. The pseudocode here serves as the connection
between the algorithm and the C++code. It is a precise description of the algorithm’s actions, without
the reasoning about why and how it is correct. To verify the overall correctness, the reader should first
understand the correctness of the algorithms in the body of the paper, next verify that the pseudocode
implements the algorithms, then verify that the C++-code implements pseudocode, and finally run it on
their own trusted computer.

A.1     Reducibility of configurations
The program corresponding to the pseudocode in this section is presented in this GitHub repository .
The link is removed in order to anonymize paper, will be included in the camera-ready version.
   In the following pseudocodes, the four colors we use to color a graph are in t0, 1, 2, 3u.
   We first present the CheckDReducibility algorithm, which takes a configuration as input and returns
whether it is D-reducible. The definition of D-reducibility is equivalent to that used in the original
4-coloring theorem proof, and the algorithm follows previous work.
   In this algorithm, we use two subroutines, AllRingColorings and AllKempeChains, explained later.

Algorithm A.1.1 CheckDReducibility(pZ, δq)
Input: A configuration pZ, δq
Output: True if the configuration is D-reducible, False if it is not
 1: Zp Ð the free completion of Z
 2: R Ð the ring of Z    p
 3: C Ð AllRingColorings( p     Z, R)
 4: repeat
 5:     for all ϕ R C do
 6:         for all colorPair P tt0, 1u, t0, 2u, t0, 3uu do
 7:              Rϕ,colorPair a cycle created from R by contracting the edges connecting vertices colored in
    colors c1 , c2 , where c1 , c2 P colorPair or c1 , c2 R colorPair
 8:              allKempeIsFeasible Ð True
 9:              for all P P AllPlanarKempeChains(Rϕ,colorPair ) do
10:                  changeExists Ð False
11:                  for all P 1 Ď P do
12:                      ψprq :“ flippϕprqq if r P R1 P V pRϕ,colorPair q such that R1 P P 1 , ϕprq otherwise
13:                      if ψprq P C then
14:                          changeExists Ð True
15:                      end if
16:                  end for
17:                  if changeExists is False then
18:                      allKempeIsFeasible Ð False
19:                  end if
20:              end for
21:              if allKempeIsFeasible then
22:                  C Ð C Y tϕu
23:                  break
24:              end if
25:         end for
26:     end for
27: until No more updates are made to C
28: if C is the set of all (proper) 4-colorings then return True
29: end if
30: return False




                                                     57
    The AllRingColorings algorithm receives a free completion Zp and its ring R as input and returns the
set of all 4-colorings of R such that it can be properly extended to Z.
                                                                     p


Algorithm A.1.2 AllRingColorings(Z,
                                 p R)

Input: A free completion Zp of some configuration and its ring R
Output: The set of all 4-colorings of R such that Zp as a whole can be 4-colored
 1: C Ð ∅
 2: C Ð an empty map
 3: n Ð the number of vertices in Z     p
 4: v1 , ¨ ¨ ¨ , vn Ð an arbitrary ordering of the vertices in Zp
 5: procedure DFS(i)
 6:      if i ą n then
 7:            ϕ Ð the restriction of C to V pRq            Ź Extract the ring coloring from the coloring of Z
 8:            C Ð C Y tϕu
 9:            return
10:      end if
11:      for all c P t0, 1, 2, 3u do
12:            isColorProper Ð True
13:            for all j such that 1 ď j ă i and vi , vj are adjacent in Zp do
14:                 if Crjs “ c then
15:                    isColorProper Ð False
16:                    break
17:                 end if
18:            end for
19:            if isColorProper is True then
20:                 Cris Ð c

22:            end if
23:      end for

    The following algorithm generates a list of all planar Kempe chains. The matching parentheses
generated in Line 2 are strings that can be constructed recursively by concatenating two matching
parentheses or by adding the characters “(” and “)” to each side of a matching parenthesis string,
starting with an empty string.




                                                     58
Algorithm A.1.3 AllPlanarKempeChains(C)
Input: A cycle C of even length n.
Output: A set P of partitions of V pCq resembling the connections via planar Kempe chains
 1: n Ð |C|
 2: P Ð H
 3: for all S: matching parentheses of length n do
 4:    T Ð an empty stack
 5:    L Ð an empty list
 6:    Add 0 to top of T
 7:    cÐ1
 8:    for all i “ 1, ¨ ¨ ¨ , n do
 9:        if Si “ ’(’ then
10:            Add c to top of T
11:            Append c to L
12:            cÐc`1
13:        end if
14:        if Si “ ’)’ then
15:            Append uppermost value of T to L
16:            Pop uppermost value from T .
17:        end if
18:    end for
19:    P Ð P Y tLu
20: end for
21: return P



A.2     Homomorphism
The program corresponding to the pseudocode in the following sections is presented in this GitHub
repository . Each pseudocode is linked to the corresponding function in our C++program. The link is
removed in order to anonymize paper, will be included in the camera-ready version.
    The following routine, homomorphism, finds a homomorphism ϕ˚ from Z to Z ˚ that maps e to e˚ and
satisfies a specified degree constraint for all mapped pairs of vertices pv, ϕ˚ pvqq. If no degree constraint
is imposed, it returns a standard homomorphism between pseudo-triangulations. One of the common
constraints we use is that the intersection of the degree ranges to be non-empty (i.e., rδ ´ pvq, δ ` pvqs X
rδ ˚´ pϕ˚ pvqq, δ ˚` pϕ˚ pvqqs ‰ H ), or the degree range rδ ´ pvq, δ ` pvqs includes the other degree range
rδ ˚´ pϕ˚ pvqq, δ ˚` pϕ˚ pvqqs. We specify these constraints using the boolean functions gintersection and ginclude ,
respectively. Standard applications of this routine include verifying homomorphic images of reducible
configurations and determining whether a rule applies to a specific dart. For references, see Algorithms
A.6.8, A.9.1, A.9.2 and A.9.15.
    Note that V pZq and DpZq denote the sets of vertices and darts of Z, respectively, when Z is a
pseudo-triangulation. We use a queue data structure. The queue supports the following operations.

   • push(x): Pushes x into the queue.
   • pop(): Removes and returns the oldest element from the queue.

   • empty(): Returns true if the queue is empty, and false otherwise.

    We adopt the dot notation for these operations for data structures (e.g., T.root(x) for a union-
find instance T , Q.push(x) for a queue instance Q). For this data structure, we use std::queue in our
C++program.




                                                         59
Algorithm A.2.1 homomorphism(pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, e, pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, e˚ , g)

    a dart e˚ , a function g that takes two degree-ranges and returns the boolean flag.
Output: The homomorphism ϕ˚ from Z to Z ˚ mapping e to e˚ if it exists and further satisfies

    no such homomorphism exists.
 1: ϕ˚ Ð a map from V pZqYDpZq to V pZ ˚ qYDpZ ˚ qYtKu, initially ϕ˚ pxq “ K for all x P V pZqYDpZq.
 2: Q Ð an empty queue
 3: Q.pushppe, e˚ qq
 4: while not Q.empty() do
 5:     pf, f ˚ q Ð Q.pop()
 6:     if ϕ˚ pf q ‰ K then
 7:         if ϕ˚ pf q ‰ f ˚ then
 8:              return null
 9:         end if
10:         continue
11:     end if
12:     ϕ˚ pf q Ð f ˚
13:     h Ð headpf q
14:     h˚ Ð headpf ˚ q
15:     if ϕ˚ phq ‰ K and ϕ˚ phq ‰ h˚ then
16:         return null
17:     end if
18:     ϕ˚ phq Ð h˚
19:     if gpδ ´ phq, δ ` phq, δ ˚´ ph˚ q, δ ˚` ph˚ qq “ false then
20:         return null                                                       Ź A degree constraint is not satisfied.
21:     end if
22:     Q.pushprevpf q, revpf ˚ qq
23:     if succpf q ‰ nil and succpf ˚ q “ nil then
24:         return null                                           Ź a homomorphism from Z to Z ˚ does not exist.
                                                   ˚
25:     else if succpf q ‰ nil and succpf q ‰ nil then
26:         Q.pushpsuccpf q, succpf ˚ qq
27:     end if
28:     if predpf q ‰ nil and predpf ˚ q “ nil then
29:         return null                                           Ź a homomorphism from Z to Z ˚ does not exist.
                                                   ˚
30:     else if predpf q ‰ nil and predpf q ‰ nil then
31:         Q.pushppredpf q, predpf ˚ qq
32:     end if
33: end while
34: return ϕ˚



A.3     The free homomorphism from a pseudo-triangulation respecting identi-
        fication requests
We present the pseudocode for computing the free homomorphism of a pseudo-triangulation respecting
identification requests. Although we have already described this algorithm in Section 9.4.1, we provide
the pseudocode for clarity.
    We employ a union-find data structure, implemented as a forest of rooted trees. We denote by uf(S)
the union-find structure defined over a set S, where initially each element of S is a root. This data
structure supports the following operations.

   • root(x): Returns the root of the tree containing x.
   • unite(x, y): Unites the two trees containing x and y by setting the parent of root(x) to root(y).
   • same(x, y): Returns true if the same tree contains both x and y, and false otherwise.


                                                         60
Algorithm A.3.1 freeHomomorphismTriangulation(Z, tpei , fi quki“1 )
Input: A pseudo-triangulation Z, a set of pairs of darts tpei , fi quki“1 in DpZq.
Output: A pseudo-triangulation Z ˚ , and a homomorphism ϕ˚ : V pZq Y DpZq Ñ V pZ ˚ q Y DpZ ˚ q re-
    specting the identification requests tpei , fi quki“1 .
 1: uf V Ð ufpV pZqq
 2: uf D Ð uf(DpZq)
 3: Q Ð an empty queue
 4: for all i “ 1, . . . , k do
 5:     Q.pushppei , fi qq
 6: end for
 7: while not Q.empty() do
 8:     pe, f q Ð Q.poppq
 9:     if uf D.same(e, f ) then
10:         continue
11:     end if
12:     if not uf V.same(head (e), head (f )) then
13:         uf V.unite(head (e), head (f ))
14:     end if
15:     e˚ Ð uf D.rootpeq
16:     f ˚ Ð uf D.rootpf q
17:     uf D.unite(e˚ , f ˚ )                                Ź Now, f ˚ becomes the root representative.
18:     Q.push(rev (e˚ ), rev (f ˚ ))
19:     if succ(e˚ ) ‰ nil and succ(f ˚ ) ‰ nil then
20:         Q.push(succ(e˚ ), succ(f ˚ ))
21:     end if
22:     if pred (e˚ ) ‰ nil and pred (f ˚ ) ‰ nil then
23:         Q.push(pred (e˚ ), pred (f ˚ ))
24:     end if
25:     if succ(e˚ ) ‰ nil and succ(f ˚ ) “ nil then
26:         succ(f ˚ ) Ð succ(e˚ )
27:     end if
28:     if pred (e˚ ) ‰ nil and pred (f ˚ ) “ nil then
29:         pred (f ˚ ) Ð pred (e˚ )
30:     end if
31: end while
32: V ˚ Ð the set of v P V pZq with uf V.root(v) “ v
33: D ˚ Ð the set of d P DpZq with uf D.root(d) “ d
34: for all d P D ˚ do
35:     head (d) Ð uf V.root(head (d))
36:     rev (d) Ð uf D.root(rev (d))
37:     succ(d) Ð nil if succ(d) “ nil otherwise, uf D.root(succ(d))
38:     pred (d) Ð nil if pred (d) “ nil otherwise, uf D.root(pred (d))
39: end for
40: ϕ˚ : V pZq Y DpZq Ñ V ˚ Y D ˚ is a map defined ϕ˚ pxq “ uf V.rootpxq if x P V pZq, and uf D.rootpxq
    otherwise.
41: return Z ˚ defined by pV ˚ , D ˚ q and ϕ˚



A.4     The free homomorphism from a pseudo-configuration respecting iden-
        tification requests
In the following, we present the pseudocode for computing the free homomorphism of a pseudo-
configuration in Algorithm A.4.3. Here, we focus on the case where vertices are assigned degree ranges, as
this includes the case where vertices have single concrete degrees. Specifically, for a pseudo-configuration
with single concrete degrees pZ, δq, calling the function with input pZ, δ, δq yields the result for pZ, δq.



                                                    61
The algorithm requires several subroutines. First, we introduce the subroutine dartIdentification
that identifies the darts of the underlying pseudo-triangulation obtained by forgetting the degree func-
tion from the input pseudo-configuration. Next, it maps the degrees, calculating the intersection of the
degree ranges for each set of identified vertices. This procedure returns null if it attempts to identify
vertices whose degree ranges are disjoint (a degree-mismatch error), or the resulting pseudo-configuration
has a loop (a loop error).

Algorithm A.4.1 dartIdentification(pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, tpei , fi quki“1 )
Input: A pseudo-configuration pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, a set of pairs of darts tpei , fi quki“1 in DpZq.
Output: A pseudo-configuration pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, a map ϕ˚ : V pZq Y DpZq Ñ V pZ ˚ q Y DpZ ˚ q obtained
    from Z by identifying all pairs of darts tpei , fi quki“1 or null if the identification causes a loop,
    degree-mismatch error.
 1: Z ˚ , ϕ˚ Ð freeHomomorphismTriangulation(Z, tpei , fi quk                         i“1 )
 2: if Z ˚ has a loop (i.e., a dart d with headpdq “ headprevpdqq) exists. then
 3:      return null                                                                                      Ź a loop error
 4: end if
 5: δ ˚´ , δ ˚` : V pZ ˚ q Ñ N Y t8u, initially defined by δ ˚´ pvq “ 1, δ ˚` pvq “ 8 for all v P V pZ ˚ q.
 6: for all v P V pZq do
 7:      v ˚ Ð ϕ˚ pvq
 8:      if rδ ˚´ pv ˚ q, δ ˚` pv ˚ qs X rδ ´ pvq, δ ` pvqs “ H then                         Ź Two ranges are disjoint.
 9:           return null                                                                    Ź a degree-mismatch error
10:      end if
11:      rδ ˚´ pv ˚ q, δ ˚` pv ˚ qs “ rδ ˚´ pv ˚ q, δ ˚` pv ˚ qs X rδ ´ pvq, δ ` pvqs       Ź Calculate the intersection.
12: end for
13: return ppZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, ϕ˚ q


    We can optimize the dartIdentification subroutine by using a modified version of Algorithm A.3.1.
Specifically, each time we identify two vertices in Line 13 of Algorithm A.3.1, we verify that the intersec-
tion of the degree-ranges associated with these vertices is non-empty. To achieve this efficiently, the root
representative of each set in uf V maintains the intersection of the degree-ranges for all vertices in its
tree. Consequently, at the end of the execution, the degree-ranges of the root representatives correspond
exactly to the resulting degree-ranges δ ˚´ and δ ˚` calculated in dartIdentification subroutine. This
modified procedure is presented in Algorithm A.4.2. The optimized dartIdentification subroutine
calls this modified algorithm, checks for the existence of any loops, and then directly returns the re-
sulting homomorphic image along with its degree-ranges. However, we do not implement this since the
unoptimized algorithm is fast enough.

Algorithm A.4.2 freeHomomorphismTriangulationWithDegreeIntersection(pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, tpei , fi quki“1 )
 1: The algorithm in Line 1-11 in Algorithm A.3.1
 2: if not uf V.same(head (e), head (f )) then
 3:     h˚e Ð uf V.rootpheadpeqq
 4:     h˚f Ð uf V.rootpheadpf qq
 5:     uf V.unite(h˚e , h˚f )                                                  Ź Now, h˚f becomes the representative root.
              ´ ˚         ` ˚           ´ ˚         ` ˚
 6:     if rδ phe q, δ phe qs X rδ phf q, δ phf qs “ H then                                     Ź Two ranges are disjoint.
 7:          return null                                                                        Ź a degree-mismatch error
 8:     end if
 9:     rδ ´ ph˚f q, δ ` ph˚f qs Ð rδ ´ ph˚f q, δ ` ph˚f qs X rδ ´ ph˚e q, δ ` ph˚e qs        Ź Calculate the intersection.
10: end if
11: The algorithm in Line 15-41 in Algorithm A.3.1
12: δ ˚´ pvq Ð δ ´ pvq and δ ˚` pvq Ð δ ` pvq for every v P V ˚
13: return pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q and ϕ˚ .


   Algorithm A.4.3 is the main routine that creates the free homomorphism respecting identification
requests, described in Section 9.4.2 and Section 9.5. We first identify the given pairs of darts using


                                                           62
Algorithm A.4.1. Then, if the identification is successful, we proceed to resolve potential degree-issues
by calling the resolveDegreeIssues subroutine. For a pseudo-configuration with single concrete degrees,
we require that dZ pvq ă δpvq for any boundary vertex, and dZ pvq “ δpvq for any inner vertex v.
Generalizing this into degree ranges, a vertex is said to have degree-issues if it violates the following
condition.
   • dZ pvq ă δ ´ pvq if v is a boundary vertex and
   • dZ pvq “ δ ´ pvq “ δ ` pvq if v is an inner vertex.


Algorithm A.4.3 freeHomomorphismConfiguration(pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, tpei , fi quki“1 )
Input: A pseudo-configuration pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, a set of pairs of darts tpei , fi quki“1 in DpZq.
Output: A set Z of a pair of pseudo-configurations pZj˚ , δj˚´ , δj˚` q and a homomorphism ϕ˚j : V pZq Y
    DpZq Ñ V pZj˚ q Y DpZj˚ q respecting the identification tpei , fi quki“1 .
 1: A ÐdartIdentification(pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, tpei , fi quk                i“1 )
 2: if A “ null then
 3:     return H
 4: end if
 5: ppZ ˚ δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, ϕ˚ q Ð A
 6: Z̃ Ð resolveDegreeIssues(Z ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` )
 7: return tppZ̃, δ̃ ´ , δ̃ ` q, ϕ̃ ˝ ϕ˚ q | ppZ̃, δ̃ ´ , δ̃ ` q, ϕ̃q P Z̃u


    The resolveDegreeIssues routine in Algorithm A.4.4 is designed to resolve degree-issues by itera-
tively identifying or adding darts via several subroutines. idX denotes the identity map on a set X. We
use the four subroutines.
    First, the innerSubdegreeError subroutine detects any inner vertex v with dZ pvq ă δ ´ pvq, which
implies a subdegree error. If such an error is detected, we immediately give up on resolving degree issues.
We present the innerSubdegreeError subroutine in Algorithm A.4.5. Note that we decide that v is a
boundary vertex if some dart e with headpeq “ v satisfies succpeq “ nil. Otherwise, v is considered an
inner vertex.
    Second, the vertexSingleDegreeIssue subroutine detects a vertex with a single specified degree
(i.e., δpvq – δ ´ pvq “ δ ` pvq) and degree-issue. Specifically, it searches for
   • a vertex v with δpvq ă dZ pvq, or

   • a boundary vertex v with δpvq “ dZ pvq.
If no such vertex exists, then this subroutine returns null. We present the vertexSingleDegreeIssue
subroutine in Algorithm A.4.6.
     Third, the fixSingleDegreeIssue subroutine attempts to resolve a degree-issue found by
vertexSingleDegreeIssue. If such a degree-issue cannot be resolved due to errors, such as a loop, sub-
degree, or degree-mismatch error, this subroutine returns null. We present the fixSingleDegreeIssue
subroutine in Algorithm A.4.7. More detailed implementations are given later.
     Fourth, the singleOutLowerDegree subroutine is responsible for subdividing degree ranges if it
is necessary to resolve degree-issues. More precisely, this subroutine detects a vertex with δ ´ pvq ă
δ ` pvq and δ ´ pvq ď dZ pvq. If found, it subdivides degree range rδ ´ pvq, δ ` pvqs into rδ ´ pvq, δ ´ pvqs and
rδ ´ pvq ` 1, δ ` pvqs, creating two corresponding copies of the given pseudo-configuration. This is the only
subroutine that performs degree range subdivision. We present the singleOutLowerDegree subroutine
in Algorithm A.4.9.
     We iteratively apply these subroutines. The process terminates successfully when the pseudo-
configuration is free of degree-issues: if innerSubDegreeError returns false, vertexSingleDegreeIssue
returns null, and singleOutLowerDegree returns null. In this case, the resulting pseudo-configuration
is added to the output set.




                                                       63
Algorithm A.4.4 resolveDegreeIssues(Z, δ ´ , δ ` )
Input: A pseudo-configuration pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q with potential degree-issues.
Output: A set of pseudo-configurations pZ̃, δ̃ ´ , δ̃ ` q with ϕ̃ : V pZq Y DpZq Ñ V pZ̃q Y DpZ̃q obtained
    from pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q by resolving degree-issues.
 1: Z Ð H
 2: Q Ð an empty queue
 3: Q.push(pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, idV pZqYDpZq )
 4: while not Q.empty() do
 5:    ppZ̃, δ̃ ´ , δ̃ ` q, ϕ̃q Ð Q.poppq
 6:    if innerSubdegreeError(Z̃, δ̃ ´ , δ̃ ` ) = true then
 7:        continue
 8:    end if
 9:    v ÐvertexSingleDegreeIssue(Z̃, δ̃ ´ , δ̃ ` )
10:    if v ‰ null then
11:        A Ð fixSingleDegreeIssue(pZ̃, δ̃ ´ , δ̃ ` q, v)
12:        if A ‰ null then
13:              ppZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, ϕ˚ q Ð A
14:              Q.push(pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, ϕ˚ ˝ ϕ̃)
15:        end if
16:        continue
17:    end if
18:    B ÐsingleOutLowerDegree(Z̃, δ̃ ´ , δ̃ ` )
19:    if B ‰ null then
20:        ppZ1 , δ1´ , δ1` q, pZ2 , δ2´ , δ2` qq Ð B
21:        Q.push(pZ1 , δ1´ , δ1` q, ϕ̃)
22:        Q.push(pZ2 , δ2´ , δ2` q, ϕ̃)
23:        continue
24:    end if
25:    Z Ð Z Y tppZ̃, δ̃ ´ , δ̃ ` q, ϕ̃qu
26: end while
27: return Z




Algorithm A.4.5 innerSubdegreeError(Z, δ ´ , δ ` )
Input: A pseudo-configuration pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q.
Output: true if an inner vertex v with dZ pvq ă δ ´ pvq exists, otherwise false.
 1: for all v P V pZq do
 2:    if v is an inner vertex and dZ pvq ă δ ´ pvq then
 3:        return true
 4:    end if
 5: end for
 6: return false




                                                     64
Algorithm A.4.6 vertexSingleDegreeIssue(Z, δ ´ , δ ` q
Input: A pseudo-configuration pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q.
Output: A vertex v with δ ´ pvq “ δ ` pvq and degree-issue if exists, otherwise null.
 1: for all v P V pZq do
 2:    if δ ´ pvq ‰ δ ` pvq then
 3:        continue
 4:    end if
 5:    if δ ´ pvq ă dZ pvq then
 6:        return v
 7:    else if v is a boundary vertex and dZ pvq “ δ ´ pvq then
 8:        return v
 9:    end if
10: end for
11: return null


   The following subroutine fixSingleDegreeIssue resolves a degree-issue, which occurred in a vertex
with δpvq – δ ´ pvq “ δ ` pvq, as follows.
  1. When v is a vertex v with δpvq ă dZ pvq: let e be a dart whose head is v. If v is a boundary vertex,
     we choose the first dart. Let f be the dart reached from e by following succ δpvq times. We identify
     e and f via the dartIdentification subroutine.
  2. When v is a boundary vertex with δpvq “ dZ pvq: we make v an inner vertex by appropriately
     adding darts and updating pointers. We employ the addBoundaryDarts subroutine in Algorithm
     A.4.8 to handle this case.

In any case, it may return null, which means that a degree-issue cannot be resolved successfully.

Algorithm A.4.7 fixSingleDegreeIssue(pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, v)
Input: A pseudo-configuration pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, a vertex v with δ ´ pvq “ δ ` pvq and degree-issue.

    tained from pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q by resolving degree-issue on v or null if it causes an error.
 1: if δ ´ pvq ă dZ pvq then

    nil )).

 4:      return dartIdentification(pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, tpe, f qu)          Ź It may return null due to a loop,
    degree-mismatch error.
 5: else if v is a boundary vertex and δ ´ pvq “ dZ pvq then
 6:      A Ð addBoundaryDarts(pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, v)

13: end if

A.5     Functions for reading configuration, rule, cartwheel files
Our C++program reads and writes files representing configurations, rules, and cartwheels in specific
formats. While we omit the detailed format descriptions here, they are provided in the documentation
accompanying our program. In these formats, we do not use the dart representations to express an under-
lying pseudo-triangulation. Instead, we use rotations of neighbors around each vertex. Additionally, the
boundary is explicitly marked. Since the configurations, rules, and cartwheels we used have no multiple
darts between two vertices, this representation contains the same information as the dart representation.
To construct the dart representation from the rotations around each vertex, we use the fromVRotations
subroutine in Algorithm A.5.1. The fromVRotations subroutine takes the input rotations, and con-
structs a pseudo triangulation represented by rotations. When the size of rotations is N , the vertex
set consists of v0 , . . . , vN ´1 . When rotations[i] is a list of integers ra0 , . . . , ak s, it means that vi is
adjacent to va0 , . . . , vak in this rotations. If ai “ ´1, it represents the boundary. It creates all the darts
and assigns the appropriate pointers to them.
    In the following, we will use list data structures whose size is changing dynamically. Let L1, L2 be
instances of the list. In adding a new element x to the back of the list L1, we write L1.push backpxq. In
concatenating two lists L1, L2, we write L1 } L2. The size of the list L1 is denoted by |L1|. For this data
structure, we use std::vector for our C++program.


                                                        66
Algorithm A.5.1 fromVRotations(N, rotations)
Input: We denote the number of vertices by N and a vertex set by V “ tv0 , . . . , vN ´1 u. A list rotations,
    where rotations[i] represents the rotation of indices of vertices around vi P V , where ´1 marks
    the boundary.
Output: The pseudo triangulation represented by V and rotations or raise an error if rotations has
    multiple darts between two vertices or the rotations of two vertices have a discrepancy.
 1: darts Ð an N ˆ N array of darts, initialized by nil.
 2: for all a P t0, . . . , N ´ 1u do
 3:    for all i P t0, . . . , |rotationsras| ´ 1u do
 4:        b Ð rotationsrasris
 5:        if b “ ´1 then
 6:            continue
 7:        end if
 8:        if dartsrasrbs ‰ nil then
 9:            raise an error                                    Ź Multiple darts between two vertices.
10:        end if
11:        dartsrasrbs Ð a fresh dart
12:    end for
13: end for
14: D Ð H                                                                      Ź D becomes the dart set.
15: for all a P t0, . . . , N ´ 1u do
16:    size Ð |rotationsras|
17:    for all i P t0, . . . , size ´ 1u do
18:        b Ð rotationsrasris
19:        if b “ ´1 then
20:            continue
21:        end if
22:        e Ð dartsrasrbs
23:        headpeq Ð va
24:        if dartsrbsras “ nil then
25:            raise an error                         Ź The rotations of two vertices have a discrepancy.
26:        end if
27:        revpeq Ð dartsrbsras
28:        s Ð rotationsrasri ` 1s if i ă size ´ 1 otherwise rotationsrasr0s
29:        succpeq Ð dartsrasrss if s ‰ ´1 otherwise nil
30:        p Ð rotationsrasri ´ 1s if i ą 0 otherwise rotationsrasrsize ´ 1s.
31:        predpeq Ð dartsrasrps if p ‰ ´1 otherwise nil
32:        D Ð D Y teu
33:    end for
34: end for
35: return The pseudo triangulation that consists of pV, Dq



A.6     Reducible configurations in pseudo-configurations
In this section, we present a procedure to determine if a given pseudo-configuration pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q with the cen-
ter c˚ has a homomorphic image of a reducible configuration in D. The main routine is the containConf
routine in Algorithm A.6.6.

Cut-vertices First, we describe the procedure to convert a configuration in D to cut-vertex-free con-
figurations, as described in Section 10. The extendFromCutVertices routine in Algorithm A.6.1 handles
this process. Because this routine is called after reading a configuration file, it takes the rotations of
neighbors around each vertex as input, not the dart representation, to construct new cut-vertex-free
configurations. From (Z3), each cut vertex in a configuration has two neighbors in the ring. The
findCutPairs routine in Algorithm A.6.2 returns a set of pairs of ring vertices adjacent to each cut-
vertex. The extendFromCutVertices routine calls the findCutPairs routine to get such a set of pairs of


                                                      67
ring vertices P . Since our reducible configuration in D has at most one cut-vertex, |P | ď 1. We consider
2|P | different extensions. For each extension, we remove the vertices in the ring that are not added for
the extension. The removeRing routine in Algorithm A.6.3 handles this process.
     Also, we choose a special dart f “ xy such that both y and x have fixed degrees and maximizing
pδ ´ pyq, δ ´ pxqq lexicographically, as described in Section 10. This fixed-degree condition is satisfied when
neither x nor y is an auxiliary vertex added for a cut-vertex. The maximumDartDegree procedure in
Algorithm A.6.4 handles this process.

Algorithm A.6.1 extendFromCutVertices(N , R, δ ´ , δ ` , rotations)
Input: The number of vertices N in the free completion of a configuration and R in its ring; a vertex
    set V “ tv0 , . . . , vN ´1 u containing the ring vertices VR “ tv0 , . . . , vR´1 u, degree functions δ ´ , δ ` on
    V zVR , and a list rotations representing rotations of neighbors for each vertex in V .
Output: The set of configurations obtained by adding a neighbor for each cut-vertex, with the special
    dart.
 1: P Ð findCutPairs(N, R, rotations) Ź In all configurations from D, the number of cut-vertices
    are at most 1, so |P | ď 1.
 2: K Ð H
 3: for all S Ð t0, . . . , 2|P | ´ 1u do
 4:     remove Ð an array of size R, initialized by true
 5:     for all i Ð t0, . . . , |P | ´ 1u do
 6:         va , vb Ð P ris
 7:         if the i-bit of S is 1 then
 8:              removeras Ð false
 9:         else
10:              removerbs Ð false
11:         end if
12:     end for
13:     pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q Ð removeRing(N, R, δ ´ , δ ` , rotations, remove)
14:     f Ð maximumDegreeDart(pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q)
15:     K Ð K Y tppZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, f qu
16: end for
17: return K


    In the findCutPairs routine, for each vertex vi in a given configuration, we check whether vi is a
cut-vertex. If vi is a cut-vertex, we add a pair of neighbors in the ring to the resulting list. To check
whether vi is a cut-vertex, we calculate the set of vertices UR in the ring that are adjacent to vi , and the
number of times t that vi touches the consecutive vertices of the ring. By (Z3), vi must be a cut-vertex
if both |UR | and t are 2. Also, if t ě 2 and |UR | ‰ 2, then it would contradict (Z3). In this case, we
raise an error since an input configuration is invalid, but all configurations in D satisfy (Z3), so we have
no error.




                                                          68
Algorithm A.6.2 findCutPairs(N, R, rotations)
Input: The number of vertices N in the free completion of a configuration and R in its ring; a vertex

    representing rotations of neighbors for each vertex in V .
Output: The set of pairs of vertices in the ring that are adjacent to cut-vertices of the input configura-
    tion, or raise an error if the input is an invalid configuration.
 1: P Ð an empty array
 2: for all vi P tvR , . . . , vN ´1 u do
 3:     UR Ð H                                  Ź UR is the set of vertices in the ring that is adjacent to vi .
 4:     tÐ0              Ź t represents the number of times vi touches the consecutive vertices of the ring.
 5:     d Ð |rotationsris|
 6:     for all j Ð t0, . . . , d ´ 1u do
 7:         k1 Ð rotationsrisrjs
 8:         if k1 ă R then
 9:              UR Ð UR Y tvk1 u
10:         end if
11:         k2 Ð rotationsrisrpj ` 1q mod ds

13:              tÐt`1
14:         end if

17:         raise an error                                                               Ź Invalid configuration
18:     end if
19:     if t “ 2 and |UR | “ 2 then                                                         Ź vi is a cut-vertex.
20:         va , vb Ð UR
21:         P.push backppva , vb qq
22:     end if
23: end for
24: return P


    The removeRing routine in Algorithm A.6.3 removes the vertices vi in the ring with the input
removeris “ true. This routine consists of three steps. First, for each remaining vertex, we assign
a new vertex ID. Second, we construct new rotations of neighbors for each remaining vertex. Finally,
we update the degrees of the remaining vertices. For each remaining vertex u in the ring such that the
number of incident darts is dpuq, we set new degrees δ 1´ puq “ dpuq ` 1, δ 1` puq “ 8. For each vertex not
in the ring, the degree is the same as the input degree. The routine returns the pseudo configuration
constructed from new rotations and degrees by the fromVRotations routine in Algorithm A.5.1.
    The mirror routine in Algorithm A.6.5 converts a given pseudo configuration into its mirror by, for
every dart e, swapping pred (e) and succ(e).

Algorithm A.6.5 mirror(Z, δ ´ , δ ` )
Input: A pseudo configuration pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q.
Output: The mirror of pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q.
 1: for all e Ð DpZq do
 2:    swap predpeq, succpeq
 3: end for
 4: return pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q

every configuration in D has the special dart chosen by the maximumDartDegree subroutine. Note also

Checking configurations We use the constant CONF DEG MAX that depends on the configuration set D.
Specifically, CONF DEG MAX is 12, representing the maximum value of δ for any configuration pZ, δq P D.
    The containConf subroutine takes an input set of configurations K. For the purpose of this algorithm,
K is limited to either H, D, s or Ds Y tT73 u. The final one is necessary to handle Lemma 8.6. For T73 , we
choose the special dart in the same way as D,   s so they all have each special dart.
    Every configuration pZ , δ , δ q from D has at most one vertex c with δ 1´ pcq “ δ 1` pcq ą 8. We
                              1 1´ 1`          s
choose the special dart, say f “ xy, of Z 1 by maximizing the degrees of endpoints lexicographically, so
the head y is c, if such a vertex c exists. This is checked by looking δ 1´ pyq ą 8. In the containConf
routine, we only consider mapping of c to the center c˚ in Z ˚ . Thus, if δ 1´ pyq ą 8, then f must be
mapped to f ˚ with headpf ˚ q “ c˚ .
    Furthermore, we only need to consider mapping of f to some dart f ˚ in Z ˚ whose endpoints have the
same degree values. To facilitate this, we classify the darts e˚ of pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q according to the values of δ ˚
of headpe˚ q, tailpe˚ q “ headprevpe˚ qq. This procedure is in the dartsByDegree subroutine in Algorithm
A.6.7. Since K is either H, D,  s or D s Y tT73 u, we only need to classify the darts whose endpoints have
degrees of at most CONF DEG MAX.
    The procedure to determine the existence of a homomorphism from Z 1 to Z ˚ such that f maps to a
specified dart f ˚ P DpZ ˚ q is in the rootedContainConf subroutine in Algorithm A.6.8.

A.7     Configuration with degree ranges blocked by reducible configurations
In this section, we present the routine that determines whether a pseudo-configuration with degree ranges
pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q with the center c˚ is blocked by a given set of reducible configurations K, as described in
Section 10.1. The main routine is blockedByReducibleConfiguration in Algorithm A.7.1.


                                                        72
    By Lemma 10.2, we first select a finite set of degrees from rδ ˚´ pvq, δ ˚` pvqs, for each vertex v P Z, that
are representative to check blocking by reducible configurations in D.  s We then generate all combinations
of these degrees across all vertices. Specifically, we select

   • all degrees from rδ ˚´ pc˚ q, δ ˚` pc˚ qs if δ ˚` pc˚ q ď CONF DEG MAX, otherwise use only δ ˚` pc˚ q, or
   • all degrees from rδ ˚´ pv ˚ q, δ ˚` pv ˚ qs if δ ˚` pv ˚ q ď 8, otherwise use only δ ˚` pv ˚ q, if v ˚ ‰ c˚ .
This procedure is in the representativeDegree subroutine in Algorithm A.7.2.
   Next, for each pseudo-configuration with a single specified degree obtained by the
representativeDegree subroutine, we check whether some configuration from K has a homomorphic
image into it satisfying the mapping condition about c˚ using the containConf subroutine in Algorithm
A.6.6.

Algorithm A.7.1 blockedByReducibleConfiguration(pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, c˚ , K)
Input: A pseudo configuration pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, a center vertex c˚ , a set of configurations K.
Output: true if pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q is blocked by K, otherwise false.
 1: for all pZ̃, δ̃q P representativeDegree((Z ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` ), c˚ ) do
 2:    if containConf(pZ̃, δ̃q, c˚ , K) = false then
 3:        return false
 4:    end if
 5: end for
 6: return true




Algorithm A.7.2 representativeDegree(pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, c˚ )
Input: A pseudo-configuration pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q with a center vertex c˚ .
Output: A set of pseudo-configurations tpZ ˚ , δ 1 qu obtained from pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q by generating all com-
    binations of single representative degrees for each vertex.
 1: T 1 Ð tδu, where δ is any map from V pZ ˚ q to N.
 2: for all v ˚ P V pZ ˚ q one at the time do
 3:     if v ˚ “ c˚ and δ ˚` pv ˚ q ą CONF DEG MAX then
 4:          L Ð tδ ˚` pv ˚ qu
 5:     else if v ˚ ‰ c˚ and δ ˚` pv ˚ q ą 8 then
 6:          L Ð tδ ˚` pv ˚ qu
 7:     else
 8:          L Ð td | δ ˚´ pv ˚ q ď d ď δ ˚` pv ˚ qu
 9:     end if
10:     T̃ Ð H
11:     for all δ 1 P T 1 do
12:          for all d P L do
13:              δ̃ Ð a copy of δ 1 .
14:              δ̃pv ˚ q Ð d
15:              T̃ Ð T̃ Y tδ̃u
16:          end for
17:     end for
18:     T 1 Ð T̃
19: end for
20: return tpZ ˚ , δ 1 q | δ 1 P T 1 u



A.8     Free combination of discharging rules with degree ranges
In this subsection, we present the pseudocode that combines discharging rules to prove Lemma 7.2, 7.1.
Although we have already described the algorithm in Section 9.5.1, we provide the pseudocode for clarity.
In our C++program, a free combination R˚ of discharging rules has the flag representing the subset of


                                                           73
rules R̃ Ď R that lead to R˚ . The flag is represented by f : R Ñ ttrue, falseu satisfying f pRq “ true if
and only if R P R̃. This flag is used in the pruneByNonAssociatedRule subroutine in Algorithm A.9.12.

Algorithm A.8.1 addRuleToCombination(R˚ , R, K)
Input: A combined rule R˚ , a rule R, a configuration set K is the set of configurations to be avoided.
Output: Finds the set of rules combined from R˚ and R that are not blocked by K.
 1: pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` , e˚ , r ˚ , f ˚ q Ð R˚
 2: pZ, δ ´ , δ ` , e, rq Ð R
 3: Z̃ Ð freeHomomorphismConfiguration(pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q \ pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, tpe˚ , equ)

A.9     Enumerating bad cartwheels with tail ranges
In this section, we present the pseudocodes corresponding to the algorithm for enumerating bad
cartwheels, as described in Section 11.2.

A.9.1    Charges along an edge and a bound on the final charge
In this section, we introduce several algorithms for rule application, as described in Section 11.2.1. We
use these routines to calculate the lower and upper bounds on the amount of charge transferred by the
rules.
    Recall that ginclude , gintersection are defined in Section A.2. The alwaysApply subroutine checks whether
a rule R always applies to e˚ in pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q. This routine calls Algorithm A.2.1 with the ginclude
function, and returns true if such a homomorphism ϕ˚ from R to Z ˚ exists. Similarly, the neverApply
subroutine checks whether a rule R never applies to e˚ in pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q. This routine calls Algorithm
A.2.1 with the gintersection function, and returns true if no such homomorphism exists.
    The amountOfChargeSend subroutine calculates the amount of charge transferred across e by the rule
                                         ´ `
set R for every pZC , δC q P pZC , δC     , δC q. This value is computed as the sum of the charges associated with
                                                   ´ `
the rules that always apply to e in pZC , δC         , δC q. The amountOfPossibleChargeSend subroutine calcu-
                                                                                                            ´ `
lates the maximum possible charge transferred across e by the rule set R for any pZC , δC q P pZC , δC        , δC q
that contains no centered reducible configuration from D. This value is computed as the maximum charge
                                                                                                           ´ `
associated with the combined rules in R˚´D , excluding the rules that never apply to e in pZC , δC          , δC q.

Output: true if R applies to e˚ for every pZ ˚ , δ ˚ q P pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, otherwise false.
 1: pZ, δ ´ , δ ` , e, rq Ð R
 2: return homomorphism(pZ, δ ´ , δ ` q, e, pZ ˚ , δ ˚´ , δ ˚` q, e˚ , ginclude ) ‰ null
